Verified by running the shipped SQL against a live TimescaleDB 2.28.1/PG18 rig: routing, boundary partition, per-bucket dedup arithmetic, the database-filter variant, and the old read side by side on the same fixture.
- **Query Store gets total-CPU and total-duration compose measures** ([#2732]) - the compose catalog's Query Store roster could rank by executions, a per-interval peak, or a per-execution average - never by what a query group actually CONSUMED, so "what burned the most CPU over this window" was not expressible against the box's headline dataset (a per-execution average ranks a query that ran once at 30s over one that burned an hour of CPU at 200ms a call). `qs_total_cpu_us` / `qs_total_duration_us` fill the hole as a new weighted-sum measure shape (`MeasureRatioMode.WeightedSum`): the existing Weighted ratio's numerator with no denominator - `SUM(avg * execution_count)` on the raw route, since avg times executions IS each interval's total, and the corrected rollups' pre-multiplied `cpu_us_weighted_sum` / `duration_us_weighted_sum` columns read directly on the CAGG routes. Pure catalog/expression plumbing - no migration, no collector change, no backfill - and time-series, ranked (top query_hash by total CPU), and scalar modes all work for free.
- **Alert deliveries carry a linked triage page** ([#2710]) - option 2 of the issue's sketch: a purpose-built, read-only `GET /api/triage` endpoint in the Darling web API assembles a per-firing triage page ON READ - the matching `config_alert_log` row(s), the recent collection log, and alert-type-relevant collected data (deadlock/blocking detail for those alerts, CPU trend + top queries for High CPU, the PostgreSQL reads for the PG alert family, a summary fallback for unmapped metrics) - every section running through the SAME `/api/read/{tool}` dispatch the dashboard already serves, so there is zero SQL/projection drift and nothing is provisioned per alert (no `config.custom_views` row, nothing to GC; a link older than retention degrades to honest empties, and a missing server/metric/history row degrades to notes on the page, never a 500). The SPA renders it at `#/triage?server=...&metric=...&at=...&dedup=...` with derive.js-inferred field configs, and the Alert History page links each row to it. All four webhook channels carry the link when the new `web.publicBaseUrl` (darling.json, file-authoritative - deployment plumbing, not a store knob) is set: Slack gets a link button, Teams a `potentialAction` OpenUri, PagerDuty the Events v2 `links` array plus a `custom_details` Triage row, and the generic template a `{{triage_url}}` token. Unset (the default, and always on Lite, which serves no web page) every payload renders byte-identical to before - a link can never be the reason an alert fails to deliver.
- **Postgres targets get the Poison Wait alert** ([#2711]) - SQL Server's Poison Wait fires on THREADPOOL/RESOURCE_SEMAPHORE waits because they are near-zero in healthy operation, so any sustained accumulation is inherently abnormal. The Postgres analogue now watches the pair with the same trait from the issue's own fleet research - IPC:BtreePage (backends queueing on a hot B-tree index page) and IPC:BufferIo (backends stacked behind one another's in-flight page reads): exactly zero on 4 of 5 production servers checked, and a structural inter-backend contention signal no other alert covers on the fifth. Lock/Relation, the other researched candidate, was rejected - a relation-level lock wait IS blocking, and the Blocking alert already owns that ground. The threshold shape is deliberately NOT the SQL Server avg-ms-per-wait bar: these events average 1-2 ms per wait at six-figure volumes, a shape a per-wait average can never see, so the alert fires on TOTAL accumulated wait time - Warning when a poison event accrues 600 seconds of wait inside 10 minutes (an average of one backend continuously stuck, ~160x the worst healthy baseline observed anywhere on the fleet), Critical at ten backends, judged per event so one event's cooldown never eats the other's. Honors the shared PoisonWaitEnabled switch both engines already use, refuses to re-fire on a store row it has already reported, and seeds its cooldown from alert history so a service restart cannot re-fire on a window the previous process already covered.
- **PostgreSQL/Aurora targets get a High CPU alert, backed by a new instance-CPU collector** ([#2719]) - PostgreSQL has never had an instance-level CPU signal: `pg_stat_kcache` measures per-QUERY kernel time, not "is this instance saturated right now", and nothing else in core PostgreSQL answers that question. The new `pg_cpu_utilization` collector reads AWS RDS Performance Insights' `os.cpuUtilization.total.avg` OS counter over the RDS/PI API rather than a database connection - chosen over CloudWatch's `AWS/RDS`/`CPUUtilization` after live testing showed CloudWatch's figure reading capacity-relative and roomy on Serverless v2 (~6.8%) against PI's true OS-level utilization on the identical window (~16.8%); PI is universally enabled across the fleet already. Aurora and RDS only - self-hosted PostgreSQL has no equivalent route, so the collector no-ops there rather than fake a signal. The alert reuses the SAME `CpuEnabled`/`CpuThresholdPercent` settings SQL Server's High CPU already reads, so one threshold means the same thing on both engines.
- **Fleet overview stops false-flagging active servers as Offline** ([#2699]) - `get_fleet_overview`'s last-collection query was a bare `GROUP BY server_id` with no time bound, scanning the ENTIRE `collection_log` history (2.2M rows and growing) on every call, and a dictionary miss from that query fell through to `default(DateTime)` rather than null - a value old enough to always band Offline instead of reaching the NeverCollected branch. Live impact: a rotating set of actively-collecting servers (seen: multi-20, multi-03, epsilon-01, dummy-01, multi-31, multi-53) read “Offline - no recent collection” while their real collection_time rows were 22-100 seconds old. Bounded the query to a 48-hour window (matching the sibling FleetCollectionHealthSql's WHERE bound) and fixed the miss-handling to propagate null, so a genuine gap reads AwaitingFirstCollection/Warning, never a fake-ancient Offline.
- **`pg_wait_stats` stops re-shipping unchanged wait events** ([#2695]) - Aurora's `aurora_stat_system_waits()` reports `wait_time` cumulative since instance start with no reset function anywhere in the API, so the collector's own `WHERE w.wait_time > 0` kept matching any wait type that had ever fired, forever - the same row re-shipped every single cycle whether or not the event fired again. The identical shape [#2691] fixed for `pg_statement_stats` (176.9 GB of a 180 GB store in 36 hours) - a bounded catalog here (a fixed wait-event enumeration, not an unbounded statement set) kept it from runaway-exploding the same way, but it was still the 2nd-largest table in the pgmon store at 1.25 GB on a 1-minute cadence with nothing but idle repeats to show for it. The delta on WAITS now decides whether a row ships, computed in `ReadAsync` rather than `WritePayload` for the same reason #2691 documents - the write loop starts the binary-import row before `WritePayload` runs, so by the time a collector could refuse to write, the row already exists. A row ships unless it hits a CONFIRMED zero-waits interval; the wait-time delta series is still computed unconditionally on every row, including skipped ones, so its baseline does not go stale and falsely gap-reset on a later real accrual.
- **Collection health distinguishes a collector that has gone dark from one that is actively failing** ([#2694]) - a collector whose `AppliesTo` gate flips off for a target (`agent_status` and `running_jobs` on an RDS SQL Server instance, where SQL Agent job data is not reachable) simply stops being invoked - no new success, no new error, nothing. Its last historical success sat inside the health window and aged past the FAILING cutoff exactly like a genuinely broken collector would, so `get_collection_health` and the fleet overview reported it as FAILING - alarming noise masking any GENUINE failure surfacing the same way. A new `STOPPED` band keys on hours since the newest run of ANY status rather than hours since the last SUCCESS: a collector that keeps being invoked and keeps failing still reads FAILING correctly, since an active failure IS a recent run; a collector that has attempted nothing at all for that long now reads STOPPED, and does not count toward a server's failing-collector total, matching how `NO_PERMISSIONS` already behaves. Separately confirmed the existing PERMISSIONS/NO_PERMISSIONS design (a denied grant, a missing source object, and a platform limitation sharing one status, distinguished by the quoted server error text) is deliberate and already excluded from the failing count - no change needed there.
- **PostgreSQL wraparound Warning signals autovacuum losing the race, not routine proximity** ([#2693], closes [#2689]) - the Warning arm fired at 0.9x `autovacuum_freeze_max_age` unconditionally, and that is a ROUTINE operating point every healthy database climbs to and resets on every freeze cycle - one fleet database re-fired the identical alert every ~5-minute cycle at 9% of the way to actual wraparound, with the read tool's own severity reading `ok`. The collector already reports `freezing_is_keeping_up` (has the age come down from its own recent peak) but the alert never consulted it. The relative Warning arm now sits AT the setting (1.0x, the crossing point where autovacuum itself forces a vacuum) and is gated by FreezingIsKeepingUp per counter - resetting every cycle stays silent, stuck at or above it warns. Also adds an absolute early-Warning arm at 50% of the true 2^31 ceiling, mirroring the existing Critical ceiling arm, so a cluster tuned high enough that the relative arm is unreachable still gets an early signal. Critical arms (2x setting, 74.5% of ceiling) are unchanged.
- **`pg_deadlocks` now works on Aurora and RDS** ([#2692]) - it was failing PERMISSIONS on every managed PostgreSQL target, 100% of the pgmon fleet, with no grant able to fix it: the collector's own doc comment already described a two-transport design where the route is "chosen at dispatch", and PgDeadlockLogParser already carried an Extract(rawLogText) entry point built for it, but the actual dispatch table never grew the Aurora/RDS branch `pg_plan_capture` has ([#2538]) - so every managed target fell through to the `pg_read_file` route, which Aurora cannot serve regardless of grants (no real local log directory, `pg_read_server_files` not grantable). Adds `RdsDeadlockIngestor`, mirroring `RdsPlanIngestor` exactly, with its OWN `RdsLogSource` instance - sharing plan capture's would consume the other's resume marker and starve whichever ingestor ran second in a cycle. The existing `RdsLogUnavailableException` handling at the cycle level needed no changes: it already degrades an authorization refusal to PERMISSIONS for any collector by exception type.
- **`pg_statement_stats` stops re-shipping idle statements** ([#2691]) - `pg_stat_statements` has no server-side “changed since I last looked” filter, so at the 1-minute cadence every PostgreSQL collector runs, the collector re-wrote its ENTIRE tracked catalog every cycle whether or not a statement ran - across a 50-cluster fleet this drove `pg_statement_stats` to 176.9 GB of a 180 GB store within 36 hours of onboarding, +126 GB in a single day. A row now ships only when its calls delta is a CONFIRMED zero-activity interval's opposite: `CollectorDeltaCalculator` reports interval 0 on a first sighting, a counter reset, or a gap re-baseline, and all three are genuinely new information, so all three still ship; only a real interval with zero new calls - meaning the statement demonstrably did not run, and `pg_stat_statements` never advances any other counter without a call - is dropped. The decision moves to `ReadAsync` because the write loop starts a row's binary-import before `WritePayload` runs, so by the time a collector could refuse to write, the row already exists; the other two delta series stay unconditional on every row, including skipped ones, so an idle stretch cannot leave their baselines stale for a later real gap to falsely reset. No TOP-N cap - the measured driver was idle repeats, not a large active statement population.
- **query_store retires the runaway detector for a flat candidate-plan ceiling** ([#2690]) - [#2683]/[#2685] detected a store whose plan population churns faster than any pass can drain by watching for 24 consecutive count-clamped passes, then dropping the per-pass ceiling from 2048 to 512 with hysteresis. The 2026-08-29 peak verification showed this fail on the store it was built for: OMEGA logged ZERO clamped passes and ZERO runaway arms while plan_fetch still ran 38-73s across twelve straight passes, because the learned average happened to keep "wanted" just under the old 2048 ceiling, so the streak never advanced and the throttle never engaged. MaxCandidatePlans is now a flat 512 - always in effect, so this failure mode cannot occur - and the streak/hysteresis machinery is retired. The accepted trade: databases with the smallest measured plan sizes converge over more cycles instead of fewer, acceptable for query_store specifically because it serves historical analysis rather than in-the-moment troubleshooting.
- **`plan_correction` binds on compatibility-level-100 databases** ([#2688]) - the recommendation-JSON shred used `TRY_CONVERT`, which is not a recognised built-in below database COMPATIBILITY_LEVEL 110 and raises Msg 195. plan_correction runs per-database through `[db].sys.sp_executesql`, so it executes in the TARGET database's compat context, and a compat-100 database on a 2017+ instance is legal - so the whole read threw for such a database. The doc comment's compat-100 reasoning had covered JSON_VALUE-over-OPENJSON but missed this. All eleven numeric extractions now use `TRY_CAST`, which binds at compat 100 (verified on the SQL 2025 rig); output is unchanged. House rule: always `TRY_CAST` over `TRY_CONVERT` unless a CONVERT style code is actually needed.
- **`plan_correction` stages its recommendations before touching Query Store** ([#2687]) - the per-database read joined `sys.query_store_query`, `sys.query_store_query_text` and `sys.query_store_plan` straight off `sys.dm_db_tuning_recommendations`, a DMV with no statistics, so on a bloated Query Store the optimizer guessed its cardinality high and satisfied those joins by SCANNING the whole plan store - the 260s tail [#2673] could only backstop with a wall-clock budget. The recommendation set is invariably small (the engine keeps a handful and erases them on restart), so it is now materialised into a `#temp` with its details JSON already shredded, and the Query Store views are joined off that - a real row count the optimizer turns into a nested-loop SEEK per `query_id`/`plan_id`. The sp_QuickieStore rule again (see [#2680]): never naively join a Query Store view you can seek. Output is unchanged - the same 38 columns in the same order, the enablement-only row still survives a zero-recommendation database, and the joins stay LEFT so an aged-out plan cannot drop a live recommendation - verified against a real Query Store on SQL 2025. The seek-versus-scan flip is the DMV-cardinality mechanism, not a rig-measured delta, so the [#2686] per-item wall-clock budget stays as the backstop and `collector_cost` will show the real before/after.
- **The live Query Store fetch stops joining a view it never reads** ([#2680]) - the per-item query joined `sys.query_store_query_text` even on the Darling path, where the text column is nulled and the text is pulled separately by-ids, so the join produced one probe per row and fed nothing. `query_store` is already the fleet's single heaviest collector - the #2674 self-metric now says so in numbers - and this is the sp_QuickieStore rule applied literally: don't join a Query Store view you don't consume. It is gated on the same flag that nulls the column, so Lite, which reads the text inline, keeps the join. Measured -12% (398ms -> 351ms) on a 40,388-plan catalog on the rig, provably non-filtering because `query_text_id` keys exactly one text row so the emitted rows are unchanged, and the join-less shape was run against a real Query Store to confirm identical output.
- **The tool now monitors its OWN cost on the servers it watches** ([#2674]) - which of our collectors is the most expensive to run on a monitored server was answerable only by scraping the service log. Schema **v105** adds `collect.collector_cost`, an INTERNAL self-metric table (the store_metrics pattern, not a CollectorCatalog collector): every collector run's cost - run count, total and max target-side query DURATION (ms, not pure CPU), store-write time and rows - is accumulated in memory per (server, collector) and flushed as one hourly aggregate on the store-metrics tick, with its own 90-day retention. **MAX is kept, not just an average**, because the tail is how a collector sticks out on a target - a single multi-minute execution an average would bury. `get_collector_cost` (and `/api/read/get_collector_cost`) return the ranked fleet list or one collector's daily trend. And it alerts on ITSELF: a fleet-level self-alert fires when a collector's latest day exceeds its own 14-day baseline by 2x (guarded by a 1s/day floor and three baseline days so a cheap or new collector cannot trip it), re-fires on the cooldown, and resolves when it drops back - so a collector that regresses into a hog pages us instead of surfacing in someone's server monitoring. Grew out of the #2675 investigation, where the same numbers had to be reconstructed from logs by hand.
### Changed

- **A census count stated over a population the summary does not republish can no longer be satisfied by assertion** ([#3200]) - `RefreshCeilingProvenancePinTests` now bounds the light-refresh ceiling's census count by how many published readings exceed that summary's own stated 95th percentile, derived from the same nearest-rank definition.
- **A sizing constant cannot be tightened on a population too thin for a maximum over it to bound anything** ([#3193]) - `RefreshCeilingProvenancePinTests` now requires each hourly-refresh ceiling's published population to clear a floor derived from its own nearest-rank percentile definition.
- **Web dashboard custom views now record WHO changed them** ([#2550]) - `updated_by` carries the authenticated OIDC subject instead of the constant `web`, and `/api/session` reports the requesting seat's own edit right so a read-only seat is rendered as one. A deployment without OIDC is unchanged — a seat with no subject still stamps `web`. The MCP surface keeps its `mcp` provenance constant, having no per-user identity to stamp. No schema change.
- **Assert collection size with `Assert.Single` and `Assert.Empty` in the source-scanning test pins, clearing all 24 `xUnit2013` analyzer warnings** ([#3132]) - Assertion form only; counts of 2 or more, and the set-equality comparisons the analyzer does not flag, are unchanged.
- **Lite: log verbosity becomes a setting, and per-database collection timing moves below the default** ([#3104]) — `AppLoggerAdapter` answered `IsEnabled(Debug)` from a hard-coded `>= Debug` while `AppLogger.Debug`'s write sat inside `#if DEBUG`, so the level a caller carried and the level that decided its fate were two different decisions that disagreed in the only configuration that ships. Lite builds Release, so every `LogDebug` in the app wrote nothing while the adapter reported it enabled — and lowering a line to `Debug` to quiet it would not have quieted anything visible, it would have deleted the line from every install with no setting able to restore it. Both are now one runtime minimum level: `AppLogger.IsEnabled` is the single gate, the adapter defers to it, the static entry points consult it, and `log_minimum_level` in `settings.json` sets it (`Trace` through `None`, default `Information`, documented in `Lite/config/settings.sample.json` and the README's Lite troubleshooting section). Behaviour at the default is byte-identical to before — the same 50 Debug sites stay silent, `Information` and above are untouched — but they are now reachable without a rebuild. The per-database collection timing line (`[server] collector [db] => N rows (sql:Nms, duckdb:Nms)`, one per database per collector per cycle) moves to `Debug` on the rule Darling's fault split already stated and #3102 already applied: a phase line must not be louder than the error it decomposes, and a run that succeeded has no error beside it. It returns in full at `Debug`, and the aggregate it decomposes was never only in the log — `collection_log` records `duration_ms`, `sql_duration_ms`, `duckdb_duration_ms` and `slowest_item` per run at every level.
- **The fleet card's collection flag is named for what it measures** ([#3098], reported from two agents' readings) - `has_collector_errors` on `get_fleet_overview` / `/api/fleet` was derived from collection freshness and nothing else - no error count, no `collection_log` read, no window - and the card's own reason string already called it "collection stale". It is now `collection_stale`. Two agents drew four wrong conclusions from the old name in one day, each falsified by a store query and each a reasonable reading of the name: cards flagged with all 39 collectors `HEALTHY` and `failed_collector_count: 0`, the flag set by errors 15 to 26 hours outside the card's published window, and the flag clear on all 43 cards while `ERROR` rows sat inside the window those cards stated. The error axis is unchanged and still separate - `failed_collector_count` and `collector_severity` - and the card already carried `last_collection`, the value the flag is a function of, so a reader can now recompute it from the response alone. **Breaking for an MCP client keying on the old field name**; the value is recoverable from `status` or from `last_collection` against `generated_at`. Lite's `HasCollectorErrors` is unaffected: it is fed from a real error count, on a different ladder, where the name is accurate.
- **Darling: per-cycle collector timing telemetry moves to `Debug`** ([#3102]) — the eight success-path run lines that decompose a collector's cost (`=> N rows (sql:Nms, pg:Nms)`, the `open:`/`drain:`/`other:` phase split, the per-database split, and the `plan_fetch:`/`text_fetch:` sub-splits) were emitted at `Information` and therefore written on a default log: roughly two lines per collector per server per cycle, and three per *database* for the collectors that fan out, which on a 42-server store is ~2M lines a day of near-uniform text. The cost was never disk. It was that the collector `ERROR`, `PERMISSIONS` and store-cancellation lines the log is read for are dozens a day inside that, so an empty grep result against the file became likelier to be a bad filter than a true absence — a change in what a reading MEANS, which had already produced a false negative in a live investigation. The lines are unchanged in text, gates and numbers, and return in full with `Logging__LogLevel__PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service=Debug` (documented in `Darling/README.md`, scoped to the namespace so raising it does not turn on every other component's `Debug`); the distribution was never only in the log, since `collection_log` records `duration_ms` and `sql_duration_ms` per run at every level. The fault-path split is untouched — it already takes its arm's level, and its remarks already carried the rule this applies: a phase line must not be louder than the error it decomposes, and a run that succeeded has no error beside it. Lite's twin line is deliberately left alone because the same edit there suppresses nothing: `AppLoggerAdapter` is built without a filter and enables `Debug` unconditionally, so it needs a gate before it needs a level.
- **Consolidated the 34 private `ReadRepoFile` helpers in `Darling.Tests` onto one shared `RepoFile` authority, in the `Compile Include` shape #2913 sanctioned, reconciling eight different path-resolution semantics onto one** ([#3090]) - A derived count assertion names a 35th private copy instead of tolerating it, and the resolution equivalence of every replaced shape is pinned rather than assumed
- **Rewrote the 33 abbreviated `int` data types across 7 collector and service query strings to `integer`, and moved `CONTRIBUTING.md`'s data-type abbreviation rule from unguarded to enforced in `TsqlConventionGuardTests`** ([#3087]) - The guard's derived cost pin is retired with the cost it measured.
- **The cross-app guard's C# matcher stops at three syntactic shapes, and that boundary is now a decision with a census behind it** ([#3082]) - four widenings in one night each found the next spelling the matcher could not see (a project item [#3063], a sibling test tree [#3067], a bare directory [#3076], a root literal that is not `Path.Combine`'s first argument [#3082]), so the question stopped being what the next widening fixes and became where the sequence ends. Measured over both test trees by feeding the shipped `CSharpPaths` by reflection and blanking one site at a time, the corpus names a cross-app file in **seven shapes no arm reaches**, and #3082's is one of them: a root literal in a non-first `Path.Combine` argument (nine live instances, on both arms), a root literal as the LAST argument, segments split across a helper boundary with a variable leaf, segments handed to a variadic user helper with no `Path.Combine` in the expression at all (and **34 separate private `ReadRepoFile` declarations** in `Darling.Tests`, so the helper name is not a token anything can anchor on), a segment array in a field, an inline collection expression as a sibling constructor argument, and a collection declared in one member and projected in another. **The widest regex this arm could plausibly become reaches three of the seven and is WRONG on one of them**: with the root literal accepted at any `Path.Combine` position including the last, and one level of call nesting in the base, the split-helper shape resolves to a bare `Lite` - the whole tree for a read that names one directory - which is the precision trade [#3076] already refused; and on the live tree it turns the build red with a **false positive**, because `ParitySource` locates the repo root by probing `Directory.Exists` on a root name and the widened arm reports that as a whole-tree read of `Darling`, on the Lite arm, which has no exemption list and no backstop job. The only ways to green that are `Darling/**` in the `lite` filter or one more carve-out, so precision stays bought with structure. The remaining four shapes compose a path across MEMBERS and need constant-value and data-flow analysis over a compilation rather than a wider pattern, which is recorded as the option it is. **Nothing is unguarded either way**: every unseen instance resolves to a tree the gating filter already reaches - the Darling arm's under the derived `Lite/**/*.cs` entry or the `Lite.Tests` tree `DarlingTestsBackstopped` exempts, the Lite arm's two under the Viewer and Service trees the `lite` filter names - confirmed from the other side by the widening run, where all six newly-visible references were accepted as reachable and only the false positive failed. `build.yml` is unchanged. `TheCSharpMatchers_StillCannotSeeTheseSpellings` becomes that census: one text per shape, named, floored on quoting the root it is about (so a fixture typo cannot make a case vacuously empty, and the app root cannot satisfy a test-root case), asserted by equality against an empty answer with the shape name in the failure message, and with the shape names required distinct because a duplicate drops a shape while every remaining entry still reports a pass. Three claims about #3076's own measurement were also wrong in the tree and are corrected: the bare-token count on the Lite arm is **21, not 20** - and was already 21 at #3079's merge commit - and "every one of its 20 is a label, an assertion key or one of this class's own root constants" is **false for 13 of the 21**, since ten are first-argument `Path.Combine` reads the second arm resolves and three name a tree in a spelling no arm reaches. `CONTRIBUTING.md` gains the contributor-facing half: the three spellings the guard sees, and that anything else leaves it unable to say whether CI can run you.
- **The cross-app guard asks MSBuild for a project's items instead of evaluating MSBuild by hand** ([#3074]) - the project-file stage of `CrossAppGuardCiGateTests` parsed project XML with regexes, and [#3065] fixed seven correctness findings in it during review - nearest-wins `Directory.Build` probing, `HintPath` as an element rather than an attribute, `<HintPath Condition=…>` attribute skipping, the `@(Item)` and `@(Item->'…')` expression forms, `Import`'s own resolution base, an unasserted collection and a doc nit - every one of them real, with the next constructs already visible: `Condition=` on an item group, `Choose`/`When`, imported props that DEFINE the item being read. `dotnet msbuild <project> -getItem:… -getProperty:…` answers the same question fully evaluated - properties expanded, imports followed, conditions applied, wildcards expanded, `Directory.Build.props` probing performed by MSBuild - and measured on a fresh never-built worktree with no network it exits 0 in **0.30 s** for `Lite.Tests` and **0.34 s** for `Darling.Tests`, creating no `obj/`. Items and properties come back from ONE invocation. **The found set is byte-identical**: 22 cross-app references on the Lite arm and 19 on the Darling arm, the same paths, with the project-item stage's own contribution unchanged at `Darling/Darling.Tests/CSharpSourceWalker.cs` and nothing on the Darling arm - so the switch is a superset by equality rather than a different answer, and the comparison stays as a live assertion against a deliberately crude XML parse rather than a one-time measurement. Two of the issue's measurements are corrected: **`-p:EnableWindowsTargeting=true` is not required for evaluation** (only a build needs it; it is passed anyway because it is a no-op on the Windows runner and removes a host-dependent difference), and `-preprocess` does not combine with `-getItem`, which is why the imported-build-file answer comes from `-getProperty` - a better answer, since **`DirectoryPackagesPropsPath` returns the repo-root `Directory.Packages.props` that a two-name upward probe of the `Directory.Build.*` names could never see**, and an `ItemGroup` in that file is an item group of every project. **A relative `HintPath` resolves against the CONSUMING PROJECT's directory, not the declaring file's, and measuring that corrected a defect this change introduced into itself** - a `Reference` in a `Directory.Build.props` one level up whose relative `HintPath` names a file beside THAT props file does not resolve, while the same `HintPath` naming a file beside the consuming project does, per `-t:Restore,ResolveAssemblyReferences -getItem:ReferencePath` against a real assembly moved between the two directories one at a time. The first fixture could not have caught it, because it declared the `Reference` in the project file where both candidate bases are the same directory; the imported props file now sits ABOVE the project and carries the `Reference`, so both the relative `Include` and the relative `HintPath` resolve out of the fixture tree under the wrong rule. **Shelling out is a new failure mode in this guard's dangerous direction, so four ways of answering "nothing" without looking are floored separately**: the process never started (`Win32Exception` caught, exit -1 with the OS message - `DOTNET_HOST_PATH` is not set for a process launched by `dotnet run`, so `dotnet` is resolved from `PATH`), it exited non-zero, its output did not parse (strict - a banner, an error summary or an empty stream is a FAILURE and not zero items), and **MSBuild answered about an item type it does not recognise, which exits 0 with a present-but-empty array** - measured, and the reason the six-name item-type list cannot be floored against this repository at all, where `Content`, `EmbeddedResource` and `Reference` are legitimately empty. A synthetic two-SKU fixture in a temp directory carries one item of every requested type and one instance of every construct - so a typo in that list reds - and is the only place `HintPath` can be proven, since there is no `Reference` item anywhere in this tree; it is written RELATIVELY because MSBuild returns that metadata as written, unlike `FullPath`. **The one cross-app read the evaluated answer does not carry is an `<Import>`'s own path**, so that is a loud gate rather than an evaluator: items an imported file defines arrive with the rest, the imported file's path is not an item, and there is no `<Import>` element in any `.csproj`, `.props` or `.targets` file in this repository - three lines instead of a rule per construct, failing the moment one appears. Origin attribution now comes from MSBuild's `DefiningProjectFullPath`, which names the file that DECLARED an item rather than the project that consumed it, reduced to a bare file name outside the repository so a public CI log carries no machine path. Every project file under a test tree is evaluated rather than the first of them. Seventeen mutations were red-proofed on a committed tree with the build result checked separately from the run, each restore verified by grepping for the patched token, and the baseline re-run LAST to prove nothing leaked between cases: nine of them red **exactly one** test each - a typo in the item-type list, a dropped `HintPath` read, the wrong `HintPath` resolution base, a swallowed non-zero exit, a `Condition="false"` cross-app include the parse over-reads, a broken import detector, a timeout branch returning success, an ignored deadline and a dropped `ValueKind` guard - while a `dotnet` that cannot be launched reds five, including the pin whose success condition is emptiness. The per-type count is taken from MSBuild's own array lengths rather than from the paths the extraction found in them, so the type-NAME claim and the EXTRACTION claim fail independently: a typo shows `References=0` in the counts, while a broken `HintPath` read leaves `Reference=1` and the path missing from the found set. The `.cs` string-literal half is unchanged; MSBuild says nothing about a path in a comment, a `Path.Combine` or a `const string`.
- **Recorded the real provenance of `HeaviestHourlyRefreshObservedCeilingSeconds` — the single status-verified run behind it, the ten-run post-boundary distribution (194–594 s, mean 338 s), the transition-run ambiguity, the rule that keeps the unstatused self-metrics series out of it, and why the value is unchanged in both directions** ([#3069]) - and pinned every derived figure against the constants and the published series
- **`CrossAppGuardCiGateTests` now sees a cross-app read spelled as a bare directory name** ([#3076]) - The C# stage anchored a cross-app path on a root followed by a separator, so `Darling.Tests/ControlPlaneReloadDurabilityTests`' sweep of `new[] { "Lite", "Lite.Tests", "PerformanceMonitor.Common" }` — a whole-tree read of Lite and of Lite's test project — matched nothing at all. A bare root is now accepted where it is an element of a collection projected one element to one whole path, which is what makes a directory name a path; accepting the string alone would have reported 37 mentions in `Darling.Tests` and 20 in `Lite.Tests`, none of which an on-disk-existence filter can reject, because a root IS a real directory. `Lite.Tests` joins the tree-guards exemption list on the bound the existing entries rest on; `Lite` needs none, the `darling` filter already reaches it. Three spellings that remain unfound are pinned with empty expectations and positive controls rather than left implied.
- **Cross-app guard coverage now sees a SKU's test tree, not just its app directory** (#3067). `CrossAppGuardCiGateTests` anchored every cross-app source reference on the app directory, so `Darling.Tests`' four reads of `Lite.Tests` were invisible to it — `Lite.Tests` is a sibling of `Lite` rather than a child. The anchor is now every root a SKU owns, on both the C# and MSBuild populations, and each arm is floored on finding at least one reference under the other SKU's test tree, because a reference total stays healthy while a whole tree drops out of it. The four references are covered by the `darling-tree-guards` backstop rather than by a `darling` filter entry, and that exemption is recorded per reference with its bound, compared for set equality against what the filter cannot reach, and honoured only while the backstop is verifiably running the whole suite.
- **The cross-app CI-gate guard now reads project files as well as C# source, so a cross-app source read expressed as an MSBuild `Compile Include` is seen rather than silently missed** ([#3063]) - Both scan stages are floored by name, because a widened scan that opens no project files would pass exactly as the narrow one did.
- **Kept `FleetIdentifierScrubTests`' commentary to what maintaining the guard requires, and restated its ordinal ceiling as a property of the shape being matched rather than an inference from past renames** ([#3018]) - Measured rather than assumed: widening the ordinal to any two digits would double the false-positive vocabulary, 11 words to 28 and 103 occurrences to 204, with every added word a documentation measurement or a version number, so both patterns and both allowlists are unchanged.
- **The migration rung bound turns out to bind, and the residual it leaves is a rung SHAPE rather than a missing mechanism** ([#2894]) - `MigrationCommandTimeoutSeconds` is a client-side cancel REQUEST, so [#2935] left open whether anything bounds a migration-lock HOLDER that overruns it, and recorded the holder as unbounded. Measured, it is not. On a `timescale/timescaledb:latest-pg17` store (PostgreSQL 17.11 / TimescaleDB 2.29.2) carrying **13.5 million rows and 90 chunks** in each of the four floor-setting rungs' targets - **14.7 GB**, `maintenance_work_mem` squeezed to force the external sorts a cold busy store does - the rungs cost **V22 6.08 s, V39 4.28 s, V104 10.57 s, V23 25.61 s** unbounded, and every one of them aborts **within 50 ms** of a scaled budget, from inside `IO/DataFileRead` and `IO/BuffileRead` waits, with the backend gone from `pg_stat_activity` inside 0.4 s and the rung's transaction rolled back whole. The realistic overrun was then run at the real 300 s through the real applier: V22 parked behind a peer's `RowExclusiveLock` sat in `Lock/relation` for the entire budget and gave up at **300.06 s**, nothing applied and nothing stamped. So the lock-wait floor is a **ceiling on real holds too**, one budget per rung. **What the bound covers is backend SILENCE, not wall clock**, because Npgsql implements it as a socket READ timeout that every message restarts - matched pair, same statement and the same 40 s of work: emitting one `RAISE NOTICE` a second it **ran to completion at 40.18 s with the 5 s budget never firing**, while silent it was cancelled at 5.02 s. That is also the whole of V23's 3.00 s overshoot, which is exactly when `create_hypertable`'s own last message lands. Today's ladder is quiet (**no rung contains a `LOOP`**, and V23's is its only `RAISE`, in a handler that runs only when the rung is already failing), so V23's real ceiling is 303 s and the other three are 300.05 s.
- **A migration rung can no longer trap its own cancellation** ([#2894]) - `WHEN OTHERS` deliberately does not match `query_canceled`, which is what makes V23's `DO` block safe; naming the condition instead reverses every part of that, and it is the SILENT direction. Measured: a rung whose handler is `WHEN query_canceled`, cancelled at its budget, returned to the applier as **SUCCESS**, which **committed the rung and would have stamped its version** while the rung's own work never happened - leaving the store permanently recorded at a version it never reached, and destroying the "a stamp of N proves rung N and everything below it committed" property `MigrationLockWaitTimeoutSeconds`' expiry split reads. `MigrationDataMovingRungCensusPins` now fails the build on either spelling of the condition, over every rung's comment-stripped SHIPPED SQL, with four controls so green means something: the pattern fires on a rung that does trap the cancel, the ladder still holds a real exception handler for the scan to be looking at, the token in a rung COMMENT alone stays green, and each of the five real condition spellings matches while `5701400`, `157014`, `570140` and `my_query_canceled_flag` do not - the tokens are word-bounded because unbounded they would fail the build on any longer digit run or identifier that merely contains them, and measured, the boundaries cost nothing on the real spellings since the quotes around `'57014'` already satisfy them. **Three mechanisms were declined on measurements instead**: a `pg_cancel_backend` / `pg_terminate_backend` watchdog buys nothing, since those stopped the same four rungs at 2.01-2.03 s and 2.01-2.05 s against the applier's own 2.04-2.07 s (all three arrive through the same `CHECK_FOR_INTERRUPTS`) and terminate is worse than redundant because its `57P01` is retryable to `StartupFailureTriage`; splitting the expensive rungs for a per-statement budget helps **one of four**, V22, V23 and V104 each being a single statement, and pays for it by raising the floor from four multiples to five; and both server-side timeouts still have the wrong unit or the wrong reach. `MigrationLockWaitTimeoutSeconds` stays `5 * MigrationCommandTimeoutSeconds` with its four floor-setting rungs, so the derived-form pin still derives `5 == 4 + 1` unchanged - this changes what is known about whether the rung bound BINDS, not how many rungs spend it.
- **#2894's own account of what breaks is corrected** ([#2894]) - it recorded the lock-wait expiry as reaching `DarlingWorker`'s `LogCritical`-and-return "with no retry", which [#2936] has since made false in mechanism: there IS a retry loop around this `MigrateAsync` call, and `StartupFailureTriage` does classify the expiry's bare `TimeoutException` as retryable. It is gated on a **120 s WALL-CLOCK budget** though, an order of magnitude under a single 1500 s wait and deliberately so, precisely to stop 25 attempts becoming ten hours - so the first expiry has already exhausted it and lands in the terminal arm anyway. The outcome is what the issue said; the reason is by design rather than by omission, which matters because "no retry" invites adding one.
- **`procedure_stats` renders execution-plan XML on one cycle in four instead of on every cycle, which buys us-east-1 roughly 30 s of collection cadence at no CPU cost** ([#2862]) - it is the most expensive collector on that fleet by a wide margin: **129.9M ms** of target-side duration over 32,093 runs in 24 h, and per-day averages of 5,389 ms (09-03) and 3,706 ms (09-04) against the us-east-2 fleet's 567 ms and 424 ms for the same collector - **9.5x** and **8.7x** - while being **66% of the entire 1-minute collection body** on a sampled sweep (4,735 ms of 7,221 ms). A controlled decomposition (6 heaviest servers, 3 reps x 3 variants, serial AND 6-way concurrent, 108 executions, 150 rows every time) put **73.8%** of the read loop in RENDERING the plans, **26.0%** in shipping them, and **0.2%** in the same query with no plan apply at all. The render happens inside `sys.dm_exec_text_query_plan`, a **server-side TVF**, so three quarters of the cost is CPU burned on the MONITORED production server - which is why the lever is cadence and not a dedup key: hashing at the source removes the transfer and leaves the render on customer hardware. **The schedule is untouched.** [#2843] pinned that no DETACHED collector sits on the 1-minute tier, because a detached run skips while its predecessor is still in flight, so moving this collector off that tier would convert starvation into guaranteed misses; gating the render instead keeps runtime statistics at full 1-minute resolution and amortises only the expensive half. Riding the existing `CollectorContext.CapturePlanXml` seam means a skipped cycle omits the `OUTER APPLY` **entirely** rather than rendering and discarding, so the saving is that measured 0.2% floor and not a smaller one - pinned on the SQL the collector actually builds, because a gated path that still rendered would pay the whole 73.8% while looking correct. The [#1767] reader guard already skips NULL-plan rows and returns the latest row that HAS a plan, so a skipped cycle is invisible to the viewer, `DarlingStoredPlanReader` and the MCP's `analyze_procedure_plan` rather than rendering as a missing plan. **The phase derives from `server_id`, and that is load-bearing**: a bare `ordinal % interval` puts all 42 servers on the SAME capture cycle - `[42, 0, 0, 0]` against the shipped `[10, 10, 11, 11]`, reflected out of the built assembly - three cheap cycles and then the whole fleet paying full render cost together, which is worse than collecting every time because peak is what produces the 120 s wall-clock abandonments. It is also what makes an in-memory counter safe: a fleet-wide restart re-enters at cycle 0 and still spreads across the interval, so this needs **no stored state and no schema rung** - `collect.collector_state` was considered and rejected, because persisting the counter would buy nothing the server-derived phase does not already give and would cost a store write per server per cycle on the hot path. **The cost paid is plan-data granularity**, worst-case plan age going from ~1.9 min to ~5.5 min, and 4 is bounded by the collector's own `last_execution_time >= DATEADD(MINUTE, -10, ...)` candidate filter rather than by taste: `interval x cadence` has to stay inside that 10-minute window or a module can enter and leave the candidate set between two captures, which caps the useful interval near 7 and leaves 4 with margin. Runtime statistics are unaffected, and the first capture after a gap is not more expensive - us-east-1 reports `rows_collected = 150` on **every** run, so the `TOP (150)` candidate set is already saturated and a skipped cycle cannot enlarge it. `procedureStatsPlanCycleInterval` in `darling.json`, clamped [1,60], read through a live provider, and **1 is byte-identical to the pre-change collector** so the knob is fully reversible; only `procedure_stats` is gated, with `query_stats`, `query_store`, `deadlocks` and `blocked_process_report` deliberately left alone. Against [#2849]'s cadence equation the change alone takes us-east-1 from **~113 s to ~82 s** and **cannot** reach 60 s at `max_concurrent_sweeps = 4` - the floor there is ~72 s even at an infinite interval - so this is a large free improvement rather than the whole distance, and closing the rest is the concurrency decision [#2849] measures
- **The identifier guard now sweeps for a server named by short name and ordinal, and records what it cannot sweep for** ([#2490]) - [#2490]'s sweep matches only the full hostname SHAPE, so the two kinds of reference #2900 and #2903 had to fix BY HAND were never detectable, and the residual risk was a future change reintroducing one against a green CI. One of the two is now a sweep of its own: a short name carrying its ordinal - `omega-01` without the `prod-sql-use1-` prefix in front of it. The ZERO PADDING is what makes that shape checkable, because prose writes a measurement as `top-25`, `sev-10` or `phase-2` and never pads one, while a fleet pads every ordinal: requiring a padded ordinal rather than any number collapses the false-positive vocabulary from 114 words (985 occurrences) to 11 (103), and requiring a purely alphabetic word drops the escape-sequence and version debris with it. Those 11 are all role words - `prod`, `dev`, `local`, `sql`, `aurora`, `srv`, `box`, `web`, `target`, `bench`, `gone` - and they sit on their OWN allowlist rather than joining `SyntheticSlugs`, so widening the looser sweep can never quietly widen the stricter one. Replanting either identifier #2903 scrubbed by hand turns the new sweep red, and `apex` trips it the moment it carries an ordinal while all ~150 bare `apex` uses stay green - the ordinal is the discriminator, not the word. The other two kinds of reference are now written down as OUT of reach instead of left as an open question: a bare short name in prose has no shape to match at all (matching capitalised prose tokens instead returns 606 hits across 102 words on this tree, every one of them an acronym or this codebase's own emphasis caps), an identity-bearing fixture field has none either (the tree assigns 251 distinct free-form descriptive values to `ServerName`/`display_name`, which is good test naming and worth keeping), and hashing a denylist of the real names rescues neither - a three-letter name has 17,576 candidates, so a hash committed to a public repo publishes the name it hides, and moving the salt into a CI secret would make the guard silently no-op on every fork. Those two stay a review matter; the sweeps are a tripwire, not a proof.
- **The references #2900 left unpinned now use synthetic slugs too** ([#2490]) - [#2490]'s guard matches only the full hostname SHAPE, so three references that are not hostname-shaped survived: two bare short names in a `CHANGELOG.md` server list, now `epsilon-01`/`dummy-01` (slugs that named no other server anywhere in the tree), and a third-party product name used as a workload descriptor in two comments - `DarlingWorker`'s `plan_correction` note and `DetachedCollectorGate`'s #2701 paragraph - which now read `workload-class`, so each signature is still named by its own mechanism (`distinct-plan-population`, `decimal-parameter-instability`) rather than by a vendor. Every measurement in those comments is unchanged. `apex` is untouched everywhere else: it is this codebase's term for a blocking chain's head blocker, and the fleet's "apex box/replica" superlative.
- **Fixture display names and short-name comment references now use synthetic slugs too** ([#2490]) - [#2490] pinned fixture HOSTNAMES to invented placeholder slugs, but `FleetIdentifierScrubTests` matches only the full hostname shape, so two other kinds of reference went unpinned: a `display_name` sitting beside an already-synthetic hostname on the same fixture row, and comments naming a server by its short name rather than a full hostname. Thirteen locations across `PerformanceMonitor.Collectors`, the Darling service and `Darling.Tests` now read `omega`, a slug that named no other server in any of those files; every measurement in those comments is unchanged. `pgmonitor` joins the allowlist as well - a role word like `monitor`, so a legitimate reference to that host cannot trip the guard.
- **Darling's slice-repair collapse keeps staging the FULL projection, because on PostgreSQL the width is what makes the plan safe** ([#2876]) - the sibling question raised by [#2771], which rewrote Lite's collapse after pushing `query_plan_text` through a per-group aggregate raised a hard `Out of Memory Error` at 31,426 split intervals. Measured on PostgreSQL 18 rather than assumed to differ, at the same 31,426 groups and again at 51,426, carrying 3,033 MB of decompressed plan text. Three independent reasons it does not transfer: PostgreSQL SPILLS where DuckDB dies - the planner picks `GroupAggregate` over an external merge sort (4.3 s, 125 MB temp, negligible resident), and forced onto the `HashAggregate` that is Lite's failure shape it still bounded itself at 45 batches / 1.1 GB peak and completed; TOAST keeps the wide values out of the sort payload, so 3,033 MB of logical plan text spilled as 125 MB because the tuples carry out-of-line pointers that `min()` detoasts lazily; and the ~50 transition states per group are what make `GroupAggregate` win on cost, where a cut-down three-column form of the same query plans as an UNSPILLED `HashAggregate` at 1.6 GB. So narrowing this projection toward Lite's shape would move it TOWARD the risk. Independently, the shipped survey over the whole production hypertable returns zero split groups - [#1907] stopped the collector emitting them and the historical ones have aged past raw's 4-day edge. Pinned over the collector's own `PayloadColumns` so the narrowing cannot be made quietly, and the reasoning recorded beside `BuildCollapseSql` so the question is not re-opened.
- **Lite's server-scoped watermark read stays UNBOUNDED, and the asymmetry with its per-database twin is now documented as deliberate** ([#2800]) - filed as the Lite half of [#2795], on the reasonable read that `GetLastCollectedTimeForDatabaseAsync` taking a `collectedSince` bound while `GetLastCollectedTimeAsync` does not is the same oversight [#2344] left on the Postgres side. Measured against DuckDB rather than inherited from Darling's numbers, it is not. **The failure [#2795] fixed cannot occur here**: its mechanism was Npgsql's undocumented 30 s default `CommandTimeout` cancelling a 40,743-50,560 ms read, the cancellation being swallowed, and the resulting null being indistinguishable from a first run - silently downgrading the collector to its fallback window. `DuckDBCommand.CommandTimeout` defaults to **0**, meaning no limit, so there is no ceiling to exceed and nothing to cancel. **And the bound does not pay.** Measured on DuckDB 1.5.5 (the version Lite ships) against this table's shipped generated DDL at 50M rows / 4.7 GB - far past any realistic Lite store - with the connection already open so the figure is query cost rather than connect cost: with ONE monitored server, the common Lite deployment where `server_id` selects everything, unbounded is **0.34 ms** and bounded is **0.56 ms**, a 65% LOSS, because DuckDB answers the unfiltered `MAX` from column zonemap metadata and the `collection_time` predicate forces real evaluation instead. With five servers the bound is a genuine 9.8x (8.26 ms to 0.84 ms) but saves ~7 ms once per five-minute cycle. **The per-database twin's bound is earned and stays**: 8.40 ms to 0.79 ms at one server and 10.42 ms to 0.93 ms at five, roughly 10x in BOTH shapes, because `database_name` is not in `idx_query_store_time(server_id, collection_time)` so that `MAX` genuinely scans and genuinely prunes. The two reads have different cost structures in a columnar engine; in Postgres both scanned every chunk, which is why the shapes match there and diverge here. Verified across 1M-50M rows, narrow and realistic payload widths (identical, as expected when the query reads three narrow columns), and correlated vs scattered `last_execution_time` - the conclusion holds on all of them. No behaviour change: the method's doc comment now carries the measurement and names `CommandTimeout`'s default as the load-bearing assumption to re-check.
### Fixed

- **Fleet cards now report a PostgreSQL/Aurora target's instance CPU** ([#3267]) - `get_fleet_overview`, `/api/fleet` and the viewer's Overview cards read CPU only from the SQL Server ring-buffer table, so every PostgreSQL card carried a null CPU and an Unknown band permanently. Instance CPU is now read from `collect.pg_cpu_utilization` (Performance Insights) through the same severity ladder, and a new `cpu_source` field on each card names which collector answered — separating a reading that has not arrived from a PostgreSQL target for which none is collected at all. The transfer of `CpuSeverity`'s cutoffs was measured rather than assumed: Performance Insights' `os.cpuUtilization.total.avg` and the ring buffer's `100 - SystemIdle` share quantity, denominator and averaging window, and across a 50-target PostgreSQL fleet the median reads 8.4% with 0.48% of samples reaching 80. Memory, buffer pool and the threads block stay null on those cards: no PostgreSQL equivalent is collected and none is invented.
- **A server card no longer reports a metric as Healthy when nothing measured it** ([#3271]) - the memory-pressure, blocking and deadlock bands read off SQL Server DMV captures a PostgreSQL target has no row in, and their non-nullable inputs meant the resulting zeros banded Healthy — a green dot claiming health beside a `deadlock_source` already reporting the count as structurally uncountable. All three now band Unknown for an absent source, as CPU and worker threads already did, and the discriminant is the target's ENGINE rather than collector state, so a SQL Server whose collector is permission-denied still bands on its own failure count. The published counts are unchanged, so the fleet totals and their coverage denominator still reconcile, and an Unknown can move neither a band nor the worst-first ranking.
- **Azure master-connected size collection no longer dies the moment sys.resource_stats has sibling rows** ([#3262]) - #2643's sibling arm deliberately emits rows whose database_id, file_id and physical_name are NULL, because sys.resource_stats carries per-database sizes with no per-file breakdown, and ReadAsync read exactly those three ordinals unguarded - so the first sibling row, which appears once the view finishes ingesting (roughly an hour after database creation, which is why every fresh-server validation window missed it), threw a DBNull cast that aborted the whole read, master's own file rows included, permanently, on every master-connected Azure target. The reader now passes the NULLs through as nullable Row fields; Lite's DuckDB store drops its NOT NULL on the three columns (schema v57, dropping the table's index first per the #2748 ALTER COLUMN trap; Darling's Postgres store was always nullable here); and the has_user_databases inventory screen in both SKUs' collection health admits sibling rows through an IS NULL arm, since on a master-connected Azure target they are the only evidence of user databases the store has. A reader-level seam test now drives ReadAsync over the sibling shape the arm's own tests always pinned, proven red against the unfixed reader.
- **The Query Store tab's first/last-execution columns had no row-level value pin — the coverage shape that let the #3207/#3221 clock-frame defects ship** ([#3253]) - the ConsumedTimestampFrameDisciplineTests census proves the two properties reach the stored-UTC renderer by NAME, not that the right value comes out of the row, so a cross-wired or misformatted property would pass every existing test. Both stamps now carry #3252's machine-independent pin — expected derived through the same ViewerTimeHelper.ForDisplay conversion the renderer uses, distinct inputs so first wired to last fails, and the empty-for-null contract — and the ViewerQueryStoreHistoryRow drill-down twins, the same naive-UTC query_store_stats stamps rendered through the same conversion one window over, are pinned the same way rather than left as the next #3248. Both pins were proven red against a temporarily cross-wired property on a UTC-4 machine, where the derived expectation genuinely converts.
- **A test pinned the Query Store regression row's last-execution display as the raw server clock, which after the #3207 clock-frame work fails on any non-UTC machine and verifies nothing on CI's UTC runners** ([#3248]) - the row model converts the stored naive-UTC stamp to the display mode, so the pinned literal agreed with it only where that conversion is the identity. The expected string is now derived through the same ViewerTimeHelper.ForDisplay conversion the renderer uses - the sibling display tests' established pattern - and the test is renamed to the contract it asserts.
- **The PostgreSQL first-target runbook stopped describing a nine-collector product** ([#3247]) - the onboarding runbook had drifted a product-generation behind dev, and every stale claim pointed the wrong way: making a working install look broken, or the product look smaller than it is. Step 3 pre-flighted "all 9 PostgreSQL collectors" and step 7 scheduled 13 where dispatch runs **27**; step 5's proof line pinned "schema v68" against a store at **v114** - presented as the thing to verify, so a reader following it exactly concluded their store migrated wrong; and section 12 denied features that shipped - plan capture ([#2566] self-hosted, [#2538]/[#2692] Aurora/RDS), blocking chains (`pg_blocking`), and the Viewer's PostgreSQL tabs ([#2530]) - while the status preamble still swore "the service has never been pointed at a PostgreSQL target", which live fleets long since disproved (the runbook itself already admitted "found the hard way on the first real run" five steps later). Every number is re-derived from the code it describes: the per-shape applies/skip table and the 27-row cadence table from `CollectorCatalog` and `CollectorScheduleDefaults`, all **33** `get_pg_*` MCP tools from the live registrations, background workers 72/83 for 70 hypertables, the engine-column floor corrected v68 to **v70**, the per-collector console lines documented as Debug-level since [#3102] (the old proof point told a reader to expect lines a default console no longer prints), the step-1 optional block widened from `pg_stat_statements` alone to all five extension-backed collectors, stale "step 9" cross-references pointed at step 10 (Failure modes), and section 12 rebuilt around what genuinely remains: scheduled analysis now SAYS not-applicable in `analysis_state` rather than sitting blank, thresholds are still non-configurable, the PostgreSQL tabs have no charts, and the helper-function route is still unimplemented. Three `Darling/README.md` claims that would have contradicted the corrected runbook moved with it: `pg_statement_stats` is no longer described as Aurora-only ([#2625] made it dual-source), the "plan capture has no PostgreSQL equivalent" opener is retired, and the store-version pin reads v114 instead of v73. Five double-encoded em dashes in the runbook's helper-function section are real em dashes again. Deliberately untouched: step 1's grant story, the IAM subsection, and step 10's grant rows, which [#3239]'s fix owns.
- **The Darling README's PostgreSQL inventory stopped at 12 of 27 collectors, and now cannot silently do that again** ([#3261]) - the "What gets collected" table sat at twelve rows across five collector waves with every check green, because nothing failed when a collector shipped without a row - the drift [#3072]'s count pins guard against, one resolution finer than a count. The table now carries all 27, each row derived from the collector's own doc comment, its migration rung header and `CollectorScheduleDefaults`, and a new `ReadmeDerivedCountPinTests` fact pins the row names set-equal to the catalog's PostgreSQL roster in BOTH directions - a bespoke fact with its own delete-a-row drift proof, because several collector names legitimately appear inside other rows' source cells ahead of their own and the shared drop-one-token mutation would call the pin vacuous. The migration ladder's V1 row said "twenty PostgreSQL tables"; it says 27 with the exact rung list, and the notable-rungs table gains the seven rungs it never listed (V96-V98 wait sampling, kernel and predicate stats [#2603], V99 plan capture [#2566], V102 server configuration [#2658], V103/V104 deadlocks [#2661], V106 instance CPU [#2719]), each sourced from that migration's own header. Three "six of the seven PostgreSQL collectors" comments narrating the connection-engine defect are era-anchored ("then shipping") so they read as the history they are - while `PgMigrations`' "Seven PostgreSQL collectors branch on postgresMajorVersion" was verified CURRENT and left alone, exactly seven collectors passing the major version into their query today. And `get_pg_top_queries`' MCP description no longer opens "for Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL targets" - the one copy of the pre-[#2625] claim agents actually read now says the read works wherever `pg_stat_statements` is installed, with the I/O source breakdown and peak memory scoped to Aurora where they belong.
- **MCP `list_servers` no longer names the wrong engine in its version key** ([#3245]) - #3145 made the value engine-aware, so a PostgreSQL row read `sql_version: "PostgreSQL 18"` — no longer wrong, but no longer self-describing: a consumer that trusts the key name has to parse the value to learn the engine, the same key-vs-value mismatch the label fix removed from the UI. Each server row now also carries `engine_kind` — the raw registry token (`sqlserver` / `postgres` / `aurora-postgres`, null before any connect stamps the row), the same key and vocabulary `get_fleet_overview`'s cards already publish — and `engine_version`, the same label under an engine-neutral name. `sql_version` stays, carrying the identical value, because a field an MCP client keys on is a consumer API; it is documented as DEPRECATED in the tool description, the MCP instructions and the README, and retiring it belongs to a later major of the MCP contract, not to this change.
- **The viewer's test-connect request now names the engine it wants probed, and the probe reply's engine facts are pinned as contract** ([#3244]) - deferred from the #3145 close-out: `TestConnectServer` had no engine field, so a viewer-built `test_connect` could only ever describe a SQL Server target, and the engine-aware reply reader the Add-server dialogs gained in #3149 was unreachable with a PostgreSQL payload. The args class now carries `engine` with the same token contract as the service's `MonitoredServer` (omitted or unrecognized means SQL Server), non-null with the SQL Server default, so every request states its engine explicitly with no dialog change and a future PostgreSQL-authoring dialog sets the same field. The reply side needed no code: `MapProbeResult` already emits `engine`, `postgresMajorVersion`, `postgresVersionNum` and `isAurora` beside the SQL Server fields - but none of that was pinned, so the discriminator could have been dropped from the payload without a test going red. It is now, on both engine arms, along with an executor-to-reader seam test proving the reply's property names are the ones `ProbeVersionLabel` reads - the reader's own tests parse hand-written JSON, so both sides could previously drift apart while testing green.
- **A PostgreSQL target's registry row no longer claims a SQL Server major of 0** ([#3243]) - `CollectorTargetInfo.SqlMajorVersion` is a non-nullable `int` the PostgreSQL connect path never assigns, and the registry upsert bound it unguarded, so every PostgreSQL target persisted `collect.servers.sql_major_version = 0` - a claim-shaped value in a column any future reader can consume without the engine-aware label path #3149 put in front of it. The parameter is now guarded on engine and value exactly as `postgres_major_version` directly below it: a PostgreSQL target writes NULL, and so does a SQL Server target whose probe never read the major. Rows written before the guard need no migration - every reader already treats 0 and NULL alike as "no claim", and a live server's next connect corrects its row through the upsert's re-connect arm.
- **An absent optional PostgreSQL extension no longer reads as a permissions failure** ([#3240]) - on a target without `pg_buffercache`, `pg_stat_kcache`, `pg_qualstats`, or `pg_wait_sampling` installed, the collectors that read them failed every cycle with SQLSTATE 42P01/42883, recorded `PERMISSIONS`, and Collection Health banded them `NO_PERMISSIONS` under the hint that `pg_monitor` covers every collector - a grant fixes none of them, so a stock self-hosted target showed five red grant claims about optional modules the operator may not even want. A missing object on a collector that DECLARES its extension dependency (#3191) now records `EXTENSION_MISSING`: the sentence names the extension, the exact `CREATE EXTENSION` and the database to run it in, and the `shared_preload_libraries` restart where the declaration says installing costs one; all five banding reads count the new status and the shared classifier bands it `EXTENSION_MISSING` apart from grant problems, checked ahead of `NO_PERMISSIONS` so a window straddling the upgrade lands on the newer name. Genuine 42501 denials keep `PERMISSIONS`, an undeclared missing object keeps the generic non-fatal skip, and the MCP `precondition` miss vocabulary carries the new status so an extension-missing read still explains itself.
- **The PERMISSIONS hint on the self-hosted PostgreSQL log readers no longer prescribes a role that cannot fix them** ([#3239]) - `pg_deadlocks` and `pg_plan_capture` read the server log with `pg_read_file()`, and on a target granted exactly what the runbook said they failed every cycle with a hint insisting "pg_monitor covers every collector here; check that it is granted" — a circle, because the product's own [#2566] entry documents from measurement that this read needs the `pg_read_server_files` role AND an explicit `GRANT EXECUTE ON FUNCTION pg_read_file`, the role alone not carrying it since the function's ACL is `postgres=X/postgres`. The two collectors now get their own hint naming the grant pair with every `pg_read_file` signature spelled out, plus the nuance a live PG18 soak proved the hard way: EXECUTE grants live in each database's own catalog, so the pair only counts issued in the database Darling connects to — granted anywhere else on the same cluster, nothing changes and the failure looks identical, which is why the hint names that database the way #2638's extension sentence does (verified live: the in-database grant flipped `pg_deadlocks` to `SUCCESS` on the next cycle, no restart). The runbook's step 1 gains the self-hosted log-reader grant block with the working sequence and a proof point mirroring the collectors' own read, and its failure-mode table routes the denial to it; Aurora/RDS are untouched on their log-API + IAM route (#2538), and the classification mechanics are untouched — still a non-fatal PERMISSIONS skip, exactly as before.
- **A full solution Rebuild is warning-clean again** ([#3241]) - the release checklist's zero-warning bar failed on dev with 56 warnings that only a cold Rebuild surfaces, most of them outside the `WarningsAsErrors` gate. The bulk were test assertion idioms rewritten to the analyzers' canonical forms with identical pass/fail semantics: filtered `Assert.Empty`/`NotEmpty`/`Single` become the predicate overloads (`Assert.Single(collection, predicate)`, `Assert.Contains`/`DoesNotContain`), regex checks through `Assert.True(x.IsMatch(...))` become `Assert.Matches`/`DoesNotMatch`, three `Assert.Equal`/`NotEqual` calls put the constant in the `expected` slot, a byte-identical duplicate `InlineData` row in both `CollectorHealthClassifierTests` twins is removed (no distinct NEVER_RUN-with-abandonment shape exists, since an abandoned cycle increments `total_runs`), and two blocking `Task.WaitAll`/`.Wait(timeout)` calls become awaited async tests. The rest sat in shipping projects: `DarlingManagedRoles` and `DarlingManagedPostgres` carried type-level `[SupportedOSPlatform("windows")]` that wrongly Windows-flagged the platform-neutral compose `statement_timeout` re-assert surface and the TLS SAN reader the web dashboard shares - a member cannot widen a type-level platform annotation, so the attribute now sits on the genuinely Windows-only DPAPI/lifecycle members and the cross-platform reload and web-TLS call sites compile clean without runtime guards that would have skipped live behaviour on Linux; `SettingsValue`'s `Int`/`Long`/`Double` readers are renamed `WholeNumber`/`BigWholeNumber`/`Number` (CA1720 bans type-name tokens, `Int32`/`Int64` included); a `Where`-filtered field and a brush factory take their concrete types (CA1859); and one `as`-plus-`switch` expression gains the parentheses CS8848 asks for.
- **The onboarding probe no longer claims msdb access on Azure SQL Database targets** ([#3237]) - the connection probe behind MCP `add_servers` and the `--test-connection` CLI verb reported "msdb access: yes" for engine edition 5, a claim about a capability the platform cannot have — Azure SQL Database has no SQL Agent and no real msdb surface — on the first line an onboarding operator reads. The shared describer now reports "Agent surface: not applicable (Azure SQL Database)" for edition-5 targets, whichever way the meaningless msdb probe answered, matching the dispatch gate that already keeps the Agent-family collectors off those targets. Every other platform keeps the msdb clause, which gates three collectors and is worth saying.
- **`upgrade-darling.ps1`'s post-upgrade checklist no longer reads a healthy server count as a failure** ([#3238]) - item 2 told the operator that `COUNT(DISTINCT server_id)` over the last 15 minutes of `collect.collection_log` equals the fleet size, but on a healthy store it is fleet size plus one, because the fleet-level `data_retention` sweep logs under `server_id = 0` / `'(fleet)'`. The line now names the maintenance row, so N+1 reads as the healthy result it is instead of sending the operator bug-hunting.
- **The de-skew's accuracy claim is now scoped where a column can outlive a DST transition** ([#3231]) - `server_properties.utc_offset_minutes` is `DATEDIFF(MINUTE, GETUTCDATE(), GETDATE())`, the offset in force at collection time, so subtracting it from a stored timestamp is exact only while the timestamp and the offset sit on the same side of a transition, and #3212's sixteen de-skewed fields shipped without saying so. Thirteen of the sixteen describe current state - a running job, an open transaction, a cleaner that ran seconds ago - and are always in the same DST period as the collected offset. `get_index_usage`'s `last_user_access` is not: it is the `GREATEST` of four `sys.dm_db_index_usage_stats` columns that persist since the instance restarted, routinely months on a stable box, so a large share of those values predate the most recent transition and come back 60 minutes early - silently, and in the plausible direction, which is the bad one. `get_pvs_stats` gets the same note one tier down, since `aborted_version_cleaner_*` can reach back weeks on a quiet database. Documented rather than fixed, deliberately: the proper fix is a zone instead of an offset - `CURRENT_TIMEZONE_ID()` collected alongside `utc_offset_minutes`, then `AT TIME ZONE` at the read boundary - which is a collected column, a migration rung and five readers, and the offset path has to be *kept* rather than replaced, because `CURRENT_TIMEZONE_ID()` is SQL Server 2019+ and the product supports servers older than that. **There is no backfill**: the collector ships the column verbatim and the offset is subtracted in the projection, so nothing stored is wrong today, and a single current zone id is legitimately valid retroactively where a single current offset is not - which is the whole defect.
- **Desktop timestamp columns were rendered in the wrong clock frame in both SKUs, in opposite directions, with each SKU correct exactly where the other was wrong** ([#3207], [#3221]) - a column holding the monitored server's own wall clock — `sys.dm_exec_*` creation / cache / last-execution stamps, `plan_correction`'s recommendation and action stamps, the blocked-process report's transaction and batch attributes, `query_snapshots.tran_start_time`, msdb Agent's job start time and the deadlock graph's `lasttranstarted` — was sent through the renderer that adds the collected UTC offset, displaying it four hours early on a fleet at UTC-4; and Query Store's first- and last-execution stamps, which the collector normalises to UTC, were rendered raw and displayed four hours late. Each of twenty-nine render sites now takes the renderer matching its column's frame, and Lite gains `ServerTimeHelper.FormatServerClock` for the frame it had no renderer for, so a server-clock value converts exactly once and still honours the Server / Local / UTC display preference.
- **Four doc comments stated a column's clock frame incorrectly, one of them appealing to a sibling tab as precedent and one asserting a cross-SKU parity that did not hold** ([#3207]) - they are the reason one wrong site became several, and each now states the frame the code uses.
- **Composed panel queries now assert that every compiled statement binds exactly the parameters its plan predicts and cites every one of them** ([#3211]) - `ComposeCompiler` allocates positional parameters by hand at each site, so a site binding a value it already bound gets a fresh ordinal and shifts every later one — a class that survived the whole suite. Measured correction to the proposed fix: when the second bind's placeholder is the one the new site uses, every ordinal still appears in the SQL, so placeholder coverage and any max-based comparison are both satisfied; only a count derived from the plan sees it. Coverage is kept alongside for the spelling where the placeholder never reaches the text. Both sweep a catalog-derived corpus whose reach is asserted as membership rather than as a total, and the prediction's own reach is pinned against the compiler's `ParamList` call sites.
- **`ServiceCommandDeadlines.SerialLoopSeconds` keeps its value of 5 and loses the chain bound that justified it** ([#3204]) - `DarlingWorker.SweepWatchdogSeconds` clocks per-server collection bodies from their own launch and only selects a log level, so it never observed the serial loop; and the site census that formed the product's other operand covers only the commands carrying this constant, while the same thread awaits others under `CollectionSweepSeconds`, `AlertPassCommandTimeoutSeconds`, `AnalysisCommandTimeoutSeconds` and two 300 s budgets. Nothing bounds the chain in aggregate. The pin now enforces the relationship that does hold — the constant must stay under the collection loop's own 15 s tick, whose delay is finish-to-start, so every second spent pushes the next tick's launches out one for one — and records that nothing in the service forbids 6.
- **The SQL-text pins over the object-stats, PVS and plan-correction reads are now insensitive to alias qualification and whitespace** ([#3217]) - so a semantically neutral rewrite no longer reds four untouched files, while a removed filter, ordering key or cap still does. Schema qualifiers and case are deliberately not normalised. `TheCountAndTheRowsShareTheirFilter` is untouched: it asserts textual identity across two statements, which is what the invariant is, and under the rewrite that broke the others it is the one pin that correctly fires. The database filter and the unused-first ordering are additionally asserted by running the query against a real PostgreSQL over a fixture planting two databases in one capture, since both claims are properties of the returned rows.
- **`CONTRIBUTING.md`'s build instructions name the deprecated Dashboard and CLI Installer projects at their real paths under `deprecated/`, matching what `README.md` already said** ([#3226]) - the two documents no longer disagree on the first command a new contributor runs. The three root scripts carrying the same stale paths — `build-dashboard.cmd`, `build-all.cmd` and `package-release.cmd` — are deleted rather than repaired: nothing outside themselves referenced them, no workflow invoked them, CI publishes no Dashboard or Installer artifact, and none of the three built Darling at all, so repairing the paths would have preserved a "release" script that omits the flagship product. `build-lite.cmd` is unaffected.
