Ordering on the dimension with `sample_time` as the within-batch tiebreak earns TimescaleDB's ordered `ChunkAppend`, which stops at the newest chunk: measured 47,752 rows and 839 buffers down to 27 rows and 6, 3.27 ms to 1.08 ms end-to-end, on a seeded thirty-chunk store. **Deliberately without a time predicate.** `sample_time` is the monitored server's LOCAL wall clock on the ring-buffer arm and naive UTC on the Azure SQL DB arm, so `sample_time > now() - interval '1 hour'` compares two clock frames and returns ZERO rows - read as "no CPU data" rather than as an error, so CPU alerting simply stops - for every server behind the store's clock (measured: 0 rows at UTC-7 and UTC-4, 759 at UTC+10). `collection_time` IS naive UTC and a bound on it would be frame-correct, but it buys nothing once the ordering prunes to one chunk and costs two failure modes of its own: it drops a server that has stopped reporting out of alerting entirely, and a bare `now()` is a `timestamptz` whose comparison against a naive column is re-framed by the STORE session's own TimeZone (a 1-hour window becomes 4 on an `America/New_York` store). All four `Darling/` copies move together - the alert sweep's, the viewer's server-summary card, the MCP health reader's, and the MCP fleet reader's - the last for the frame rather than the cost, since `DISTINCT ON` with no per-server `LIMIT` reads the whole relation whatever the time key is. `ViewerServerTab.Memory`'s frame note is corrected: it still described the memory-pressure `sample_time` as server-local after that collector moved to `SYSUTCDATETIME()`.
- **A future copy of the latest-CPU read can no longer drift back to the old shape unseen** ([#2973]) - the tree-wide guard located candidate SQL spans by `@"`, but 127 files under `Darling/` write SQL as a C# raw string literal, `DarlingPgCpuUtilizationReader.LatestCpuSql` among them, so a `"""…"""` copy was invisible to exactly the guard meant to catch it. The nearer opener now wins and its own closing form bounds the span, with both interleavings pinned.
- **`/api/ping` answered `"ok"` while collection had never started; it now reports the collector's real state** ([#2953]) - the route was `Results.Json(new { status = "ok" })`: a literal, no store touch, no runtime state, no conditions. Every OTHER health surface reads the store to find out whether collection is running - `get_fleet_overview`, `/api/fleet` and the Viewer fleet grid all funnel into `DarlingFleetReader` - so with the store unreachable they fail or come back empty, which reads as a UI or permissions fault rather than as the store being down; and the one surface that did NOT read the store could not report anything either. That matters more than it sounds, because the stand-down at the end of the worker's startup steps `return`s, and **that completes the worker task successfully**: the host stays up, both Kestrel hosts keep serving, and on Windows the service keeps reporting **Running**, so SCM recovery never fires - no crash, no restart loop, no non-zero exit, and for a monitoring product the specific failure that matters most is silently not telling you, while reporting that it is fine. `CollectorRuntimeState` is the third seam of the `McpRuntimeState` / `WebRuntimeState` family and the same shape (one immutable snapshot swapped through a `volatile` field, null until published), carrying the collector's own startup verdict - **in-process rather than a row or a file because a durable witness has its own failure mode, and that failure mode is exactly the condition being reported**. `/api/ping` now answers **200 `ok`** (the collection loop started, `collecting: true`), **200 `starting`** (no verdict yet - ordinary for the first seconds of a start, and 200 because a check that alarmed here would alarm on every restart), **503 `degraded`** (a startup step failing on [#2936]'s retry budget, carrying `step`, `attempt`, `attempts` and the reason, self-clearing inside `StartupFailureTriage.RetryBudget`) and **503 `stopped`** (a terminal stand-down, carrying the reason the critical line gives). The status **code** carries the verdict because that is the field a load balancer or uptime check reads without parsing a body - a body-only signal would have left every existing check as wrong as it was - and the healthy body still says `status: ok`, so an existing check keeps passing and only stops passing when collection genuinely is not running; null members are omitted, so a `degraded` body has an attempt count and an `ok` body does not. The cost is that a load balancer health-checking this route pulls the host from rotation during a store outage, which is the correct verdict for an API whose data all comes from that store - and the dashboard's static surface and its other routes are untouched, so a human still opens the UI and reads the reason out of this body. Published at nine sites, all in the startup path: a retry publish beside each of [#2936]'s three retry warnings, a terminal publish at each of the five collection-blocking stand-downs (config load, config validate, `postgres.managed` on a non-Windows host, managed bootstrap, store connect/migrate), and one `PublishCollecting()` where the loop logs that it began. Cancellation stays silent - it means the service is stopping, not that the collector failed. Only the FIRST line of a failure message travels, capped at 400 characters with the truncation marked: PostgreSQL answers a failing rung with the message, a blank line, then a character offset into SQL a health-probe reader cannot see, and this text is the only part of the critical line that leaves the ACL-protected file log for an HTTP body. A **startup** verdict deliberately, and not a liveness heartbeat: `Collecting` is set once and never re-published per cycle, so an outage beginning later leaves this reading `Collecting` - the division of labour rather than a gap, because by then `collection_log` exists and the self-alert engine polls it, and the window that had no surface at all was the one before the first successful store interaction. **The census pin is what actually protects this**: a stand-down that forgets to publish is invisible on every automated surface and silently restores the whole defect, and the compiler cannot catch it, so the pin asserts structurally over the source that every collection-blocking `return` publishes first - across a self-anchoring region (`ExecuteAsync`'s brace-balanced body plus `RunCollectionLoopAsync` up to the publish that says collection began) so a startup step added later joins the pin the day it is written. Two of the issue's four sketched parts turned out not to be work, and are recorded here so nobody re-opens them: the Viewer already distinguishes an unreachable store as its own connect arm with its own seat state ([#2117]) and has no HTTP transport at all, so it cannot read this seam without a new dependency; and hoisting `DarlingSelfAlertEvaluator` above the migrate is not the cheap reorder it looks like - the constructor is genuinely I/O-free, but the evaluator has no startup-failure condition to raise and its delivery tail above `ApplyToConfig` would run on `darling.json`'s alert settings with an empty mute set and no history row, which is three semantics to decide rather than a reorder.
- **The last two command-timeout pins stopped certifying a site from a NEIGHBOUR's deadline** ([#2972]) - `McpReadCommandTimeoutTests` and `StragglerCommandTimeoutTests` were the holdouts still asking "did this site set a deadline" with a private `CommandTimeout\s*=` over a two-statement span, and that span reaches a SIBLING: an untimed `using (…)` header whose block opens with a timed command read as timed, and so did an untimed construction sitting directly ahead of one carrying an initializer. Both now call the shared `CommandDeadlineScanner` [#2938] extracted, which reads an initializer from the CONSTRUCTION span and qualifies an assignment by the name the construction is bound to. **Proven red-first in both scopes**: a sibling-borrow planted in `Mcp/DarlingHealthReader.cs` and another in `DarlingCollectorRunner.cs` each passed these pins before the change and fail them after. **No verdict on real source moves** - a reflection probe over each pin's own construction regex and scope enumerator asked both rules at all **150** sites (**126** on the MCP/web read surface, **13** across the three straggler regime files, **11** in the collector runner) and every one agrees, the strictness splitting cleanly by shape: all **126** read-surface sites are certified by the name-qualified ASSIGNMENT half and all **13** regime sites by the CONSTRUCTION-span half, so neither pin was relying on the looseness. The value-bound halves stay, because they answer a different question - the straggler pin's per-regime `Assigns(constant)` asserts a site carries ITS OWN regime's constant, which no deadline-shaped check can express, and the two `McpRead` assertions that read a RAW span keep the local regex because their claim is about the span rather than the judgement.
- **A control-plane reload whose store read failed no longer discards the operator's change** ([#2951]) - the reload beacon assigned `_lastConfigVersion` on *detecting* a new `config_version` and reloaded afterwards, so a reload whose store read failed had already recorded the version as applied. The change stayed live in the store, absent from the process, and unreported until some later write bumped the version again - the beacon's next tick compared equal and never retried, and nothing anywhere compared the two. **This is why a command deadline on that thread is not a delay but a discard**, which is what `ServiceCommandDeadlines.SerialLoopSeconds` is floored against; bounding the read without fixing the ordering would have left the silent-loss path intact and only made it rarer. The watermark now advances inside `if (await ReloadFromStoreAsync(...))`, and `ReloadFromStoreAsync` reports whether it applied. **The success line is `LoadViewAsync`, deliberately**: that call reads all five `config` rows under one try/catch and returns null if any throws, so either nothing was read or `ApplyToConfig` has already swapped the whole view into the live config - a swap that cannot un-happen. Everything after it is propagation and already carries its own isolation (`ReassertComposeStatementTimeoutAsync` is non-throwing and advances its baseline only on success; `SyncServerEnabledStatesAsync` catches everything at Debug), and a propagation step that *did* throw leaves the watermark behind too, so the retry re-applies idempotently. The residual window is "applied in memory, not yet mirrored" rather than a discarded version. **The retry this creates is rate-limited rather than left to flood**: holding the watermark back means a persistently unreachable store is re-attempted every 15 s tick, roughly 5,760 times a day, and `LoadViewAsync` warned on each one; it now warns on the first failure of a streak, drops to Debug, and reports recovery once. Deliberately *not* a retry policy - [#2936] owns that question for the adjacent migrate path, where it is a real design decision because that path has no loop of its own and its failure is terminal. This path already re-runs every tick by construction and its failure is recoverable, so the only defect was the ordering.
- **Role provisioning no longer writes a `statement_timeout` it failed to read, and no longer swallows cancellation to do it** ([#2952]) - `DarlingManagedRoles.ReadComposeStatementTimeoutAsync` caught every exception with a bare, unlogged `catch (Exception)` and returned 15, which its caller then wrote onto the viewer and mcp roles as `ALTER ROLE ... SET statement_timeout`. Three things wrong, in increasing order: it swallowed `OperationCanceledException`, so a shutdown arriving mid-start was reported as "the store said 15"; it logged nothing; and it **overwrote the last known-good horizon with a number nobody chose**. Since [#2931] the *client* half of that same backstop reads the same `config_service.compose_statement_timeout_seconds` column live per run, so a silent default desynced the two: an operator who set 120 got a client-side deadline of 120, a server-side ceiling of 15, every composed query dying at 15 s, and 120 visible in the UI throughout. Nothing corrected it either - [#2918]'s reload gate does fire on the difference, but only on a `config_version` bump, and a value set before the restart bumps nothing. **The two outcomes that used to look identical are now separated.** "The store has no opinion" - no `config_service` row yet, because provisioning runs before `SeedIfEmptyAsync`, or a store predating the column (`42703`/`42P01`) - still defaults to the shipped 15 and is logged at Debug, because it is expected on a first start. "I could not hear the store" returns null, is logged as a warning, and **omits the two `ALTER ROLE` statements entirely** so the roles keep the horizon the last successful provisioning gave them. Provisioning then reports `ComposeStatementTimeoutUnknown`, a negative sentinel that no clamped store value can equal, so [#2918]'s gate re-asserts on the next reload rather than concluding the roles are already correct - the omission is self-healing, not merely non-destructive. Cancellation now propagates via the `ex is not OperationCanceledException` filter the rest of the file already used, and a **second** unfiltered catch found by the same file-wide sweep - `TryHardenRoleCredential`, which logged but would still have reported a shutdown as an ACL failure to go fix - carries the filter too, so the guard needs no exemption. **Neither defect has a Lite twin**, checked rather than assumed and now pinned: Lite has no login roles and no `statement_timeout` concept (its store is embedded DuckDB), and no `config_version` beacon - its settings are applied in-process by the Settings window, so there is no store-side watermark to advance early.
- **Every store command on the startup path now carries an explicit deadline, and this is the one regime in the sweep whose value goes UP** ([#2874]) - twenty-eight sites across the managed-Postgres bootstrap, the in-place major-upgrade path, least-privilege role provisioning, the delta-baseline re-seed and the config store's first-run seed set no `CommandTimeout` and inherited Npgsql's undocumented 30 s default. Every other group in this sweep derived a value bounded from above by something scarce the caller was holding - a sweep permit, a connection permit, a cadence - and every one of them landed at or below 30 s. This path holds nothing and is bounded from above by nothing: verified on the current tree, there is no `CancelAfter` anywhere on it, `HostOptions.StartupTimeout` is unset so the framework default is infinite, and the repo has no health check, no readiness probe, no container `HEALTHCHECK` and no orchestrator manifest. What it has instead is the worst failure asymmetry in the sweep - two of the four handlers on it are `LogCritical` followed by `return`, so a managed bootstrap throw exits the service and a migrate-path throw ends collection for the life of the process with no retry - which means a deadline firing during a slow-but-HEALTHY start converts a recoverable delay into a dead service, while one firing late only delays this instance's own first cycle, the MCP and web hosts being separate `BackgroundService`s already serving on their own data sources. **The defect was never that 30 s is too long; it is that 30 s is a number nobody chose and that renders as `Exception while reading from stream`**, which on this path misreads a reachable-but-slow store as an unreachable one in the exact log line an operator acts on. `ServiceCommandDeadlines.BootstrapSeconds` is therefore **60 s**, floored roughly 400x above the measured cold worst case - 151.6 ms for the post-upgrade `count(*)` over a 0.69 GB `collection_log`, against 117 ms for the four delta seeds over a 3.69 GB `wait_stats` at production per-chunk density and 79 ms for the 63-statement provisioning batch - and capped strictly below the 300 s `PgMigrations` gives ONE migration rung, read out of that file's source rather than duplicated, because these sites are catalog reads and single-row `config` seeds and taking a data-moving rung's budget would be borrowing rather than deriving. **The group needed a second number**, because two of the twenty-eight are the only sites in it with a retry above them: `DarlingManagedPostgres.EnsureDatabaseOnceAsync`'s probe and `CREATE DATABASE` sit inside a 6-attempt / 2 s loop, and a command deadline is inside what that loop retries - measured against Npgsql 10.0.3, an exceeded `CommandTimeout` arrives as `NpgsqlException` wrapping `TimeoutException`, which `IsTransientConnectionFault` accepts, while a server-side `statement_timeout` arrives as `PostgresException 57014`, which it rejects. So the deadline multiplies there: `BootstrapConnectProbeSeconds` is **10 s**, keeping the worst case at 70 s of startup delay - inside the installers' 2-minute `WaitForStatus('Running')` - against 190 s at the inherited default and 370 s had it shared the group's 60 s. **[#1772] is this group's justification and not its bound**, and the distinction is the point: that field failure - a 276 GB store where the delta seed could not finish inside the 30 s default, silently degrading restart continuity to first-cycle-zero on every start - was fixed by binding `$1` on BOTH halves of the seed query, and measuring the two shapes side by side says why (117 ms bounded against 566 ms for the unbounded inner `MAX()`). A deadline was never that fix; what #1772 establishes is that the inherited default does fire here in the field and that when it does the failure is silent, which argues for a generous deadline rather than a tight one. `StartupCommandTimeoutTests` is scoped by MEMBER with no directory glob at all, because three of these files also hold other regimes' sites, and it **checks each construction's own object initializer rather than a two-statement window** - the window every landed pin uses can be satisfied by a NEIGHBOUR when a command is built in a `using (...) { ...; }` block, which is the literal shape of `DarlingStoreUpgrade.BridgeTimescaleAsync`, where the bridge's already-bounded `ALTER EXTENSION UPDATE` sits immediately ahead of an extension-verify read. The ten control-plane sites that share these files - the 15 s reload beacon, `LoadViewAsync`'s five reads, `SyncServerEnabledStatesAsync`'s two and the reload-path role re-assert - are asserted as NEGATIVES rather than merely documented: seven of them do also run once on this path, and a dual-caller site has to take the tighter of its two bounds, which is the 15 s serial-loop tick and not this one. **A third number, `SerialLoopSeconds` = 5 s, covers the ten commands the collection loop's own SERIAL thread runs ahead of every per-server launch** - `LoadViewAsync`'s five config reads, `SyncServerEnabledStatesAsync`'s two registry statements, the managed-role `statement_timeout` re-assert, the disk-check store-size read and the daily retention sweep's own run-record. **Seven of those nine also run once on the bootstrap path**, and they take this constant rather than the bootstrap's because a deadline is a property of the command, so a dual-caller site has to take the tighter of its two bounds. Floored on a cold 16.1 ms for `LoadViewAsync`'s five commands together, 3.8 ms for the two registry statements and 6.2 ms for `pg_database_size`, and capped by the CHAIN rather than by any one command: ten sequential commands at 5 s is 50 s and stays inside the 60 s sweep watchdog, where ten at the inherited default would be 300 s and would report a merely-slow reload as a hang. Ten is the count on the worst tick rather than a bound on the longest path - a tick where the reload beacon fires AND the 24 h purge comes due runs both back to back on that thread. It is deliberately looser than #2940's 3 s reload beacon, which runs on every 15 s tick and is a single-row lookup, and its binding asymmetry is sharper than the beacon's: the beacon advances `_lastConfigVersion` BEFORE calling the reload body, so a read that dies mid-reload has already consumed the version bump that would have retried it and the operator's change is silently lost. The sites in these files that belong to OTHER regimes are asserted as NEGATIVES rather than merely documented - #2940's reload beacon, the alert pass's CPU read and the two command-plane handlers - so no later pass can sweep them into one of these constants by adjacency. **The pin also matches its value patterns over STRIPPED source rather than raw**, closing the third pin defect this sweep has found: every landed pin counts a `CommandTimeout` written in a *comment* as a deadline set, which makes an untimed site read clean.
- **`memory_pressure_events.sample_time` was written in the monitored server's LOCAL wall clock while both of its readers assume naive UTC, so the memory-pressure series sat one UTC offset away from every other lane** ([#2932]) - `MemoryPressureEventsCollector` derives a stored `sample_time` from `SYSDATETIME()`, and nothing on either read path corrects for it: Darling's `ViewerDataService.Memory` feeds the column straight to `ViewerTimeHelper.ForDisplay`, which is documented as taking naive-UTC input, and Lite's `LocalDataService.Memory` windows it with the UTC-based `GetTimeRange` and then adds `UtcOffsetMinutes` again when plotting. Both consumers want UTC, so the collector is the side that is wrong. Measured on the us-east-2 production store while investigating [#2932]'s read cost: `cpu_utilization_stats.sample_time` sits **exactly 4 h** behind the `collection_time` written by the same collector run on **42 of 42** servers, with the collector reporting SUCCESS **12,526 times in six hours and zero failures** - the same ring-buffer arithmetic off the same `SYSDATETIME()` base, so the memory table carries the same offset. **The CPU collector is deliberately NOT changed, and that is the interesting half.** Its server-local `sample_time` is an intentional, documented convention with a shipped read-side correction: `ViewerDataService.Cpu`'s doc comment states *"Unlike every other stored column, sample_time is the MONITORED SERVER'S LOCAL wall clock (SYSDATETIME() on the server, minus each ring-buffer sample's age), NOT naive UTC"*, and [#1262] de-skews it in SQL per batch (`round(MAX(sample_time) OVER (PARTITION BY server_id, collection_time) - collection_time)` to 15 minutes) while Lite windows it through a purpose-named `GetTimeRangeServerLocal` and shifts it by `ServerTimeHelper.UtcOffsetMinutes`. Converting that column to UTC would contradict [#1262] and break Lite's CPU chart on both the query window and the plotted x-position; Darling would survive only because the per-batch de-skew self-calibrates to zero. So the store does **not** have a single timestamp frame, and `CollectorTimestampFrameTests` now pins the real invariant per column - CPU stays local because [#1262]'s correction depends on it, memory becomes UTC because its readers require it - rather than the frame-wide rule the first cut of this change assumed. Proven red both ways: reverting the memory fix fails the UTC pin, and converting the CPU collector to UTC fails the local pin, each with its mutation's anchor count asserted first. **Not corrected retroactively** - existing memory rows keep the offset they were written with, which is EDT or EST depending on when each landed, so a blanket shift would corrupt half of them; `ServerPropertiesCollector.utc_offset_minutes` already records each server's offset, so a per-row backfill is possible and is left as its own decision. **One operational note:** the first post-fix memory sample jumps forward by the local offset, and because the collector's watermark IS `sample_time` and the jump is forward, whatever remains in the retained ring-buffer window is re-read and stored a second time under its corrected timestamp - a one-time burst of duplicate physical samples at different stamps, not a gap. No schema change and no version bump.
- **The horizon convergence is now pinned to name `config` and nothing else, so it cannot arm a retention policy** - `ConvergeRetentionHorizonSql` runs on every start, and runs BEFORE the coverage gate, so [#1937]'s doc comment rested the whole thing on one sentence: only `config` is named, therefore "this statement cannot arm anything". Nothing tested it. The statement's text had **zero** test references, and the live convergence test cannot reach it: `EnsureRetentionPoliciesAsync` re-asserts the gate's own verdict through `ArmRetentionPolicySql`/`HoldRetentionPolicySql` one statement later, so the only end state a live test can observe is the gate's. Measured on TimescaleDB 2.29.2 / PostgreSQL 17, splicing either `scheduled => false` or `scheduled => true` into the convergence leaves that test green in **both** directions - while running the spliced statement by hand flips a paused policy to armed, which is the transient armed window [#1680] exists to keep closed, over exactly the tier [#1877] is holding paused because a rollup under it has not yet materialized what the raw table still holds. `RetentionHorizonConvergeCannotArmTests` now reads the named arguments off the SHIPPED string - not a transcription, which would pass forever while the statement drifted - and requires them to be exactly `config`, with neither `scheduled` nor `next_start` appearing anywhere in it. **Every negative is positive-controlled through the identical helper**, because a does-not-contain can pass by matching nothing: the arm/hold pair is the shipped counter-example for `scheduled`, and `next_start`, which nothing ships, is controlled by the shipped statement with the argument spliced in - asserted to differ from the original, so a shape drift that breaks the splice fails loudly instead of controlling nothing. The `next_start` half was already covered behaviourally (the live test's unmoved-`next_start` assertion does fail under mutation); the `scheduled` half had nothing at all.
- **A second migrator no longer dies undiagnosed waiting on the migration advisory lock, and no longer dies at all when it had nothing to apply** ([#2894]) - [#2888] gave the acquire its own budget, `MigrationLockWaitTimeoutSeconds = 5 * MigrationCommandTimeoutSeconds` (1500 s), derived as four floor-setting data-moving rungs at one per-rung bound each plus one of margin. [#2920] pinned that census. What neither closed is the residual this fixes: the budget was spent by an Npgsql `CommandTimeout` on a blocking `pg_advisory_lock`, and **the operator's entire diagnostic was `Exception while reading from stream`** - measured, not inferred, against PostgreSQL 17.11: Npgsql wraps the expiry as `NpgsqlException` over `TimeoutException("Timeout during reading attempt")`, and the one production caller logs `ex.Message` alone. A 25-minute wait on a sibling migrator reached the log as a sentence about a socket, which is precisely the [#2874] misdiagnosis shape. The acquire now retries `pg_try_advisory_lock` on a one-second cadence, says **"still waiting, N s elapsed of 1500"** every 30 s while it does, and fails with a message naming the lock, both schema versions and the `pg_locks`/`pg_stat_activity` query that finds the holder. **Expiry is deliberately not one answer.** The lock is needed to APPLY rungs, not to decide there are none - the applier commits each rung's DDL and its version stamp in one transaction, so a stamp of N proves every rung up to N committed. So on expiry the stamp table is read once: a store already at `StorageVersion.SchemaVersion` had nothing to apply and continues with 0 applied and a warning, while a store BELOW it has rungs that cannot be applied safely without the lock and throws. That split matters because the case that actually strands an instance is not a slow migrator but a dead one - a session advisory lock outlives its client whenever the server never notices the peer is gone, and then the store is perfectly current while every restart died at the budget for want of a lock it had nothing to do with. **The 1500 s is unchanged and so is its derivation**, because what was unsound was the enforcement, not the number; the census pin needed no edit. **What is still not bounded is a rung that overruns its own budget, and that is now recorded as measured rather than assumed:** `MigrationCommandTimeoutSeconds` is client-side, and neither server-side alternative fits - `statement_timeout` is per STATEMENT, not per transaction (three one-second statements all survive a two-second setting), so it would cost V39's two `CREATE INDEX`es one budget EACH and spend the whole margin, and `transaction_timeout` has the right unit but is PostgreSQL 17+ against readers here that gate as low as 13 and ends the session with FATAL. The waiter is therefore made independent of whether it was covered, which is the part that was reachable. `MigrationLockWaitContentionTests` exercises all of it against a live store under real two-session contention.
- **The alert pass's CPU read now carries the deadline the rest of the pass already had, and the guard that declared the pass clean can no longer miss a site by living in the wrong file** ([#2874]) - `DarlingWorker.ReadLatestCpuAsync` is the FIRST read `EvaluateAlertsAsync` performs, on every server, on every 30 s tick, and it set no `CommandTimeout` - so it inherited Npgsql's undocumented 30 s default and would render a deadline as `Exception while reading from stream`, which reads as a network fault and is the exact misdiagnosis [#2826] exists to prevent. It was the last site in [#2882]'s regime still on the default, and it was found by [#2928] while that lane censused the neighbouring collection-sweep regime rather than by any guard of this one. **The value is `DarlingAlertReadAdapter.AlertPassCommandTimeoutSeconds` = 10 s, and it is [#2882]'s already-derived constant rather than a new number** - this site is in that regime, not merely shaped like it: its one caller is `EvaluateAlertsAsync`, dispatched from `ProcessServerSweepAsync` on the 30 s `s_alertSweepInterval` with the plain stopping token, so it has the same absent enclosing budget the constant was bounded for, below by the **1,744.9 ms** cold forced-plan read and above by that same 30 s cadence. Deriving a second value here would have invented a distinction the callers do not have. **The floor is measured for this query rather than inherited**, read-only against the use2 production store (`cpu_utilization_stats`, 2,046,967 rows, 61 distinct `server_id`s), running the SQL extracted from source and kept parameterized through `PREPARE`/`EXECUTE` so it matches what Npgsql sends: six consecutive round trips on the heaviest server land **11.5-17.2 ms**, `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)` reports **13.9 ms** execution against 0.032 ms planning, and timing all 61 servers server-side gives avg **12.5 ms** / worst **50.6 ms** cold and avg 9.4 ms / worst 14.8 ms warm - so 10 s clears the measured fleet-wide worst by **~200x**. **The plan also disproved a claim this entry would otherwise have carried**: it is not a cheap seek on a covered ordering but a `Limit` over a top-N `Sort` over a `ChunkAppend` of **29 compressed chunks**, scanning **47,752 rows and 2,228 shared buffers to return one row**, with `Chunks excluded during startup: 0` because the query carries no `sample_time` predicate - fast in absolute terms, but by brute force rather than by an ordering. That shape is untouched here and belongs to its own issue, not to #2874. It takes the object-initializer form rather than a trailing `command.CommandTimeout =` assignment for a reason internal to the guard: the scan examines TWO statements from a construction, `AddWithValue` already occupies the second, and an assignment placed after it would sit outside the window and read as untimed - correct code failing its own pin. **Why it was missed, which is the substantive half of this change.** `AlertPassCommandTimeoutTests` scoped its sweep with a hardcoded array of SIX filenames, and `DarlingWorker.cs` is not one of them, so 45 sites were guarded and the 46th was invisible - the [#2786] shape, a guard that names the arm it was written for. **Adding the filename would not work, measured rather than assumed**: run the same scanner over the whole of `DarlingWorker.cs` and it finds **8** command sites of which **6 are untimed**, every one of them deliberately owned by another budget - `TryRefreshPgStatementTextAsync` (2) and `ReadCollectorWatermarksAsync` are the collection sweep's, `ReadStoreSizeBytesAsync` runs on the disk-check cadence, and `RunTestHypotheticalIndexAsync` / `RunExecuteActualPlanAsync` are the command plane - so a whole-file sweep would fail on correct code six times over. **So the scope is COMPUTED rather than listed.** Mixed-regime files are named by their entry point, and the members swept are the transitive closure of calls out of it, which makes the pin's claim literally "runs inside `EvaluateAlertsAsync`" instead of a restatement of today's answer: a read added to any member the pass reaches is covered on the day it is written, which is the only form of this scope a future helper cannot slip past the way this one did. The closure is 13 members and 1 command site, so it excludes all six of the above without naming them. **Review caught the same defect one level down in the first cut of that walk, and it was already live.** `MemberBodies` took only the `) {` body shape, so an `=>`-bodied member never registered as a graph node at all — and the closure's `ContainsKey` test then made it *invisible* rather than unreachable, silently, with no count moving. Three members on this exact graph are that shape: `BuildPgDeadlockIncident`, `BuildPgBlockingIncident` and `BuildPgLongRunningQueryIncident`, all `internal static ... => new(...)` and all referenced by METHOD GROUP (`rows.Select(BuildPgDeadlockIncident)`) from members inside the closure. They hold no commands, so nothing failed and the census still read 1 — which is precisely what made it worth fixing rather than noting: turning a small read into `private Task<Foo> ReadFooAsync(...) => ...;` is an ordinary refactor, and it would have been invisible to this pin exactly the way `ReadLatestCpuAsync` was invisible to the file-list scope. Both body shapes are now taken, which moves the closure **10 → 13** and leaves the command census at 1; the expression-bodied span is delegated to `CSharpSourceWalker.StatementSpanFrom` rather than a local scan for `;`, because an expression body can carry a block-bodied lambda, verbatim SQL and interpolated holes. Proven both directions with a planted expression-bodied untimed read reachable from the pass: **named as an offender with the fix, and a fully green 7/7 with the pre-fix pin against the identical mutation.** Note this is the opposite choice from #2928's `BodyStart`, deliberately — that helper uses the block-body test to pick one NAMED member's declaration out of its own call sites, where treating an `=>` match as "not a declaration" is correct; this one enumerates every declaration as a NODE, so a shape it cannot see is a hole in the graph. Edges are IDENTIFIERS rather than parsed calls, which over-approximates deliberately - an extra member can only demand a deadline on a site that does not need one, which fails loudly, while a missing edge reports clean on a site inheriting 30 s. Both scopes now run through ONE scanner, so a site cannot change verdict by moving between them. **A fifth construction shape turned up while building the walk.** `new Npgsql.NpgsqlCommand(` - the fully-QUALIFIED construction - is invisible to `new NpgsqlCommand\s*\(`, and it occurs once in the project (`DarlingWorker.ReadPgStatementTextAsync`, another regime's site and already explicitly timed at 60 s), so an alert-pass read written that way would have been missed exactly as this one was. The ctor pattern now matches it, and the bare `.CreateCommand` method group with it; both leave the census at **45**, so the widening asserts nothing new about today's tree. **Review also caught that nested declarations were swept twice** - accepting both body shapes means a local function or an `async (x) => { ... }` lambda matches too, and its span sits wholly inside its enclosing member's, so the sweep scanned that text once through each, inflating the census and duplicating any offender. One such match exists in the real file today (an async lambda registering under the name `async`), reachable from nothing, so it was latent and failed loud rather than silently. Nested matches are dropped now: a local function is scoped to its containing member, so it can have no caller the enclosing span does not already cover, and a nested type's methods are unaffected because a type declaration has no parameter list and never matches here. Declarations go 86 to 85; the closure stays 13 and the census stays 1. **Proven red eleven ways, each with its mutation's anchor count asserted before the result was read** - a control that earned itself here, since one mutation's expected count was wrong and was correctly reported as proving nothing rather than passing silently. Reverting the fix names `DarlingWorker.cs ReadLatestCpuAsync:3892`; renaming the entry point fails the declaration assertion; planting a second reachable site fails as an offender untimed and as the census tripwire timed; planting the qualified shape untimed is named by the widened pattern and **passes silently under the narrow one**, which is the pair that earns the widening; and the value bounds still fail at 30 and at 3. The load-bearing control is the one no count can supply: **cutting the CALL** so the site is no longer reachable, leaving it present and correctly timed, fails the walk's positive control - the count of occurrences in the file is invariant under it. Executed in a throwaway `net10.0` xunit host, because `Darling.Tests` is `net10.0-windows` and cannot run on macOS; expected suite delta **+2**. No schema change and no version bump (still 110).
- **Collection Health counts an abandoned cycle from the row, so a window that predates [#2803] no longer reports clean** ([#2926]) - [#2803] gave a wall-clock-budget-abandoned cycle its own `ABANDONED` status and [#2804] made the count first-class on both MCP tools, the web grid, both WPF grids and the shared `CollectorHealthClassifier`. `collection_log` is an append-only hypertable, so the cycles abandoned BEFORE that status existed cannot be rewritten: they sit in the same retention window carrying `status = 'SUCCESS'` beside `rows_collected = 0` and the abandonment's own `error_message`. All five banding reads keyed on the status alone, so those rows counted as **zero abandonments and as successes** - the abandon rate stayed under its 0.5% threshold and the collector banded `HEALTHY` while losing cycles. Observed as six such rows on one server, hiding roughly a third of its abandonment events from a status-filtered first pass; they were recovered only by filtering on `error_message` instead. The five reads (`DarlingDataReader.CollectionHealthSql`, `DarlingFleetReader.FleetCollectionHealthSql`, both `ViewerDataService` reads, and Lite's) now count `status = 'ABANDONED' OR (rows_collected = 0 AND COALESCE(error_message, '') LIKE 'wall-clock budget (%s) reached; cycle abandoned')` - the conjunction that holds across the whole series, rather than the status, which holds for one era. **The budget is interpolated, which is why the match is a pattern**: `procedure_stats`, `query_stats` and `plan_correction` carry 120 s while `query_store` carries the 600 s `PerDatabaseWallClockBudget`, so an equality against either rendered sentence answers for one collector and stays silent about the rest. The pattern is derived from `WholeCycleBudgetNoteFormat` with `{0}` widened to `%` and pinned equal to it, so re-wording the note cannot leave five reads matching a sentence nothing writes. **The same rows also leave `success_count`**, because the Success and Abandoned columns sit side by side in both WPF grids and would otherwise both claim one run; `COALESCE` rather than a bare `LIKE` is what keeps an ordinary empty cycle counted, since `NULL LIKE` is NULL and a NULL under that `NOT` would have dropped it. `rows_collected` is projected through the three reads whose aggregates sit outside a column-enumerating subquery - an aggregate naming a column the subquery does not carry fails at the STORE and nowhere earlier, which no compiler or text assertion can see. **The write path is unchanged and no migration rung was added** (schema stays at V110): the historical rows are correct about themselves, and the durable fix belongs to the readers. Lite's inline query is now the named `LocalDataService.CollectionHealthSql`, so its shape is pinned by reference like its four Darling twins rather than by grepping a file whose SQL comments quote predicates and column names. Pinned over a seeded two-era window against live PostgreSQL - one `ABANDONED` row, one pre-era `SUCCESS` row at 120 s, one at 600 s, plus an empty-enumeration cycle and a silent empty cycle that must both stay successes - positioned so the two counts land on OPPOSITE sides of the band: three abandonments of 205 runs is 1.46% and `WARNING`, while the one row a status-only count can see is 0.49% and `HEALTHY`. Same window, same collector, opposite verdict. Every negative assertion carries a positive control on the identical form.
- **A transient failure of any of the three collection-blocking startup steps no longer ends collection for the life of the process** ([#2936]) - loading `darling.json`, bootstrapping the managed Postgres, and opening the store connection to run `PgMigrations.MigrateAsync` each sat behind a single `catch (Exception)`, a single `LogCritical` and a single `return`, so a config file another process held open for a moment, a store that was restarting or still coming up, and a migration rung that can never apply all ended collection identically. **And the process does not exit on that `return`:** the worker task completes SUCCESSFULLY, so the host stays up, the MCP and web hosts keep serving, and the Windows service keeps reporting Running - SCM recovery never fires because nothing crashed. So the failures that most deserved a retry were exactly the ones whose symptom looked least like a small problem. **A transient failure now retries for two minutes at all three sites, through ONE shared classifier, and everything else keeps the critical line and the stand-down byte for byte.** **The config-load site** is #2038's conclusion reaching the worker: that change already made the MCP and web hosts retry a failed `DarlingConfig.Load()` on a 30 s backoff, and their comments point at the worker for the critical case. `Load()` is `File.Exists` then `ReadAllText` then deserialize, so a sharing violation on a file an installer, the Viewer's Settings save or a config-management tool is mid-write is transient; a malformed file, an ACL problem or a file that is not there never is. **The bootstrap site is the sharper one, because the judgment already existed and was being discarded:** `EnsureDatabaseAsync` inside it classifies transient connection faults and retries 6 times 2 s apart, and when that ran out it threw and the outer catch dropped the fact that the failure had been RULED transient. The shared classifier is also deliberately WIDER than that inner one, so a bootstrap the inner layer gave up on can still be retried. Re-entry is safe by that class's own documented contract - "a second `EnsureRunningAsync` against an initialized, running cluster does no initdb, no restart, no credential rewrite". **The three startup steps that stay terminal were checked, not overlooked:** a config failing `Validate()` (a named per-field problem), `postgres.managed = true` on a non-Windows host, and the `Program.cs` single-instance guard, which notably EXITS non-zero rather than idling. **Retryable, each shape observed against PostgreSQL 17.11 rather than reasoned about:** transport failures where the server never delivered a verdict (`NpgsqlException` over `SocketException`/`IOException`/`TimeoutException` - nothing listening, an unroutable host, the store dying mid-statement); a bare `TimeoutException`, which is how [#2935]'s polling waiter reports a spent lock-wait budget against a store below this build's version, and the `NpgsqlException` over `TimeoutException` the blocking `pg_advisory_lock` it replaced expired as; `57P01`, `57P02` and `57P03`. **Those last three are the whole reason this needed a new classifier rather than the one next door.** `DarlingManagedPostgres.IsTransientConnectionFault` rules that a `PostgresException` means the server replied and so is never transient - correct at its own site, where this process started the server moments earlier - and TWO of the three transient cases here arrive as exactly that: SIGKILL the store and the in-flight session gets `57P01` ("terminating connection due to unexpected postmaster exit"), then every connect during the crash recovery that follows gets `57P03` ("the database system is not yet accepting connections", measured at 74 consecutive attempts). That type is also `[SupportedOSPlatform("windows")]` and this path runs on Linux. Also retryable: class `08` (`08000`, `08001`, `08003`, `08004`, `08006`), and `40001`/`40P01`/`55P03`/`55006`, which are safe for a reason specific to this applier - each rung's DDL and its `darling_schema_version` stamp commit in ONE transaction, so a rung that failed part-way left nothing applied and nothing stamped, and re-entering resumes at that rung instead of redoing the ladder. **Terminal, which is where the boundary actually sits:** the classifier is an ALLOWLIST, and anything it cannot place positively keeps the old behaviour. Class `42` is a rung that cannot apply here and class `23` a rung the store's existing rows violate (V62's shape); `28P01`, `3D000` and `55000` are configuration; class `53` and class `58` are capacity and filesystem findings an operator has to see now, which two minutes clears none of. **One carve-out is load-bearing:** `FileNotFoundException` and `DirectoryNotFoundException` both derive from `IOException`, so the transport arm would sweep them in - and `Load()` throws the former for a config that is not there, which on a first install is the likeliest way it ever fails. Retried, the operator would get warnings for two minutes and then the line naming the missing path. Classified by TYPE rather than per site, so the store path needs no special case and the two file-touching sites cannot drift apart. **The two worth naming are `57014` and `57P04`**, because they share class `57` with the three retryable states above - `57014` is somebody else's `statement_timeout` cancelling a rung, which will cancel the identical rung identically forever - so this classifies state by state and never on the two-character class. **Default-deny is the load-bearing choice, not caution:** refusing to retry something transient costs an operator a restart and hands them a loud, literal, documented line asking for it, while retrying something permanent costs them that line entirely, and a retry policy that retries everything is indistinguishable from a working one until the day it matters. **Bounded rather than a supervisor loop, at 25 attempts 5 s apart under a 120 s WALL-CLOCK deadline:** both caps are load-bearing, because an attempt is not quick just because a refused connect is - one that blocks behind a peer instance's migration advisory lock spends `MigrationLockWaitTimeoutSeconds`, 1500 s, and 25 of those is over ten hours of retrying rather than two minutes, which would push the one definitive line most of a day past the failure it describes. Measured live: a second session took that lock and the service waited 38 s inside a SINGLE attempt, silently, and never retried - correct, and exactly the shape that makes an attempt cap alone misleading. So a store that is not listening spends all 25 attempts inside the budget, and a store that accepts the connection and then makes the attempt wait spends the whole budget inside attempt one; both land on the same terminal line. Below, two minutes is twice the 60 s this repo already allows a PostgreSQL start (`PgCtlWaitSeconds`), where the measured case was 2.0 s for an unclean kill of a 1.7 GB store carrying 701 MB of unreplayed WAL; above, it stays finite because a wrong host or port in `darling.json` presents as a retryable `SocketException` forever and an unbounded retry would convert the single most common permanent failure from one critical line into a service that reports Running and never concludes anything - and it stays inside the 150 s `ColdStartSpreadSeconds` the fleet's first sweep is already staggered across, so even a fully spent budget delivers the first cycle within a normal cold start. Giving this step a supervisor of its own, [#2038]-style, is a different change from classifying its failures, and this leaves the long-outage case behaving exactly as it does today rather than half-solving it.
- **A viewer surface that carries every scope's answer no longer paints an answer read for a scope the operator has since left** ([#2924]) - the status bar's collector-health field read its scope - a per-server tab's server, or the whole fleet - from the active tab BEFORE its store read and applied the result AFTER it, so a tab change inside that window left a health string describing the previous tab until the next fleet tick. One read, bounded at `ViewerCommandDeadlines.InteractiveReadSeconds` (15 s) by [#2901], and it takes a tab change inside it: **cosmetic, a wrong number rather than a wrong action.** [#2907]'s single-flight guard does not cover this and could not - it stops the same method STACKING, while this is one call whose captured scope goes stale across its own await. The guard is also narrower here than the issue assumed: `MainTabs_SelectionChanged` calls the method directly, under no guard, so two differently-scoped reads genuinely coexist and the later-STARTING one can land first. Both sites now read the scope through one expression, re-verify it after the store read, and drop rather than paint on a mismatch. A drop is right here where [#2910] needed a replay, because the control that changed the scope has already started a correctly-scoped read of its own; `ViewerScopedPaintGuardTests` pins that call, since deleting it would turn the drop into a silent loss. A sweep of all 190 viewer sources for UI state captured before an await and applied after it found 46 candidates and exactly one sibling: the database-state override window's load, whose server combo re-triggers it with no guard, no replay and no cancellation. That one is **not** cosmetic - the shared row model it paints is what the Save button writes from, while that button resolves the selected server fresh, so rows belonging to one server could be saved against another. Lite's twin of that window was line-for-line identical and is fixed in the same change, so the two apps do not drift apart on a correctness bug; all three sites are pinned by one table in `ViewerScopedPaintGuardTests` rather than split across a Darling pin and a parity pin. The other 44 are one-shot actions on a clicked target, read no mutable scope, paint only into their own scope's surface, or re-trigger through a replay that re-reads the scope (`RefreshVisibleAsync`, `ViewerServerTab.RefreshActiveInnerTabAsync`, `FinOpsTab.RefreshActiveSubTabAsync` between them cover the per-server tabs, FinOps and every aggregate tab).
- **The collection sweep's store reads and writes now carry an explicit deadline - including the binary COPY, a construction shape this sweep could not see** ([#2874]) - seventeen sites across the per-server collection body set no deadline and inherited Npgsql's undocumented 30 s default, and **four of them are not commands at all**. `NpgsqlConnection.BeginBinaryImportAsync` returns an `NpgsqlBinaryImporter` whose deadline is its own `Timeout` property - a `TimeSpan` on a different type, not the `int` seconds of `NpgsqlCommand.CommandTimeout` - initialised from the connection's `CommandTimeout` and therefore inheriting the same 30 s. There is no `NpgsqlCommand` and no `CreateCommand` anywhere in the expression, so **every regex in [#2810], [#2871], [#2882], [#2888] and [#2901] reported this family clean while all four sat on the default**, and one of them, `DarlingCollectorRunner.WriteBatchAsync`, is the store write of every collector on every server on every cycle. The other three are the RDS/Aurora ingestors, which `DarlingCollectorRunner` dispatches as collector-equivalents onto the same `collection_log` row, so they share the budget rather than merely the shape. That makes the shape inventory for this sweep **four**, and it was verified with the property itself rather than from documentation: against Npgsql 10.0.3 on a live TimescaleDB store, an untouched importer blocked behind an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock failed at **30.0 s** with `Exception while reading from stream`, and the same COPY with the connection string's `CommandTimeout` set to 5 failed at **5.0 s** - the inheritance, measured. **The value is 10 s, and it is not the alert pass's 10 s.** Bounded below by measurement: `collection_log.store_duration_ms` already records the store-write half of every run, and over 24 h on the busiest server of the larger production fleet (43 servers, a 305 GB store taking ~15-20 GB/day) the worst of 200 runs was **1.53 s** - `query_store` writing 11,614 rows - while the per-run store-write average across all 39 collectors on **both** production stores tops out at **1,511 ms** (`index_object_stats`, ~21,090 rows/run) with most collectors in the single-digit-to-low-tens ms; store connection acquisition adds the **673-893 ms** [#2819] measured. Bounded above by the **60 s** `SweepWatchdogSeconds`, not by a cadence, because the body runs its due collectors SEQUENTIALLY: real cycles on that server span 13 s to 48 s, so one blown deadline at 10 s lands a heavy cycle at 58 s and stays under the watchdog, while one blown 30 s default lands it at 78 s and manufactures a "collection body has not completed after 60s" warning on a body that is merely slow - re-creating the [#1581]/[#2170] warning herd the watchdog was tuned to stop. **Derived at `max_concurrent_sweeps` = 8, not the seeded 4**, because that knob is moving: the pool is not what binds at either width (post-[#2822] each body borrows ONE store connection, so peak demand is the sweep width itself and even the 16-wide clamp fits inside `MaxPoolSize = 24` with the seams), but the DURATION of a stalled hold is - C bodies each stalled T seconds withhold C x T connection-seconds from retention, alerting and observability, and at the 30 s default with C = 8 that is a third of the pool rather than a sixth. So the measured 1.53 s was doubled to ~3.1 s for twice the concurrent writers before headroom was taken, which is the ~4.0 s that 10 s clears by 2.5x; the value stays correct at 8 and at 16. It lands on the same number [#2882] chose from a 30 s cadence and a 1,744.9 ms forced-plan read, and that is **two derivations meeting, not a number reused** - neither of those quantities appears anywhere above. **It narrows the COPY rather than closing it, measured rather than assumed**: `importer.Timeout` bounds `StartRowAsync`/`Write`/`CompleteAsync`, proven by a server-side 45 s trigger that failed at 6.0 s with the property set and 30.0 s without, but it cannot bound the `BeginBinaryImportAsync` above it - Begin sends the COPY and awaits `CopyInResponse` under the CONNECTION's `CommandTimeout`, which Npgsql exposes read-only and for which `BeginBinaryImportAsync` has no overload, so the start phase, which is where a lock wait stalls, stays on 30 s. Both phases surface as the same string, which is why the distinction is invisible in a log. Deliberately not closed by putting `CommandTimeout` on the shared data source's connection string, which would silently re-bound the 188 sites this sweep has not reached yet. **The group's census is smaller than the issue's, and the reason is a regime boundary rather than a miscount**: the ~30 sweep sites recorded on [#2874] were attributed by FILE, and three of those files span budgets - `DarlingDeltaCalculator`'s four seeds and seven of `StoreConfigProvider`'s thirteen sites run ONCE at startup, `DarlingWorker`'s `RunTestHypotheticalIndexAsync`/`RunExecuteActualPlanAsync` are the command plane, and `ReadStoreSizeBytesAsync` runs on the disk-check cadence. Thirteen genuine sweep-body commands plus the four COPY writers is seventeen, and the rest are left to the groups whose budgets they share. **The thirteenth was found by review, not by me, and the miss is instructive**: `DarlingObservability.WriteAnalysisStateAsync` reads as the analysis pass's state write, so I excluded it as budgeted - but `passCts.CancelAfter(s_analysisTimeout)` threads its token ONLY into `analysisService.AnalyzeAsync`, every `WriteAnalysisStateAsync` call takes the plain `stoppingToken`, and one of them (`DarlingWorker.cs:1877`, the PostgreSQL-target tombstone) is called straight from `ProcessServerSweepAsync` with no pass around it at all. Being lexically inside a budgeted method is not the same as being under its budget - the same file-boundary trap this entry flags for the other groups, walked into once here. Two findings for those groups: `DarlingWorker.ReadLatestCpuAsync` is reached from `EvaluateAlertsAsync` and so belongs to [#2882]'s already-closed 10 s regime - a site that pin's file-scoped list missed - and [#1772]'s documented 276 GB field failure, cited as this group's live exposure, is a STARTUP site whose actual remedy was bounding both halves of the seed query, not a deadline. Pinned by MEMBER rather than by directory, which is new to this sweep and forced by exactly that overlap - a file-scoped guard here would fail on sites this group deliberately left alone - with the COPY shape swept PROJECT-WIDE instead, so a fifth writer anywhere trips it even when correctly timed, and the importer's constant pinned relationally so it cannot drift from the commands beside it. Built on the shared `CSharpSourceWalker` from [#2913] rather than a sixth private copy. Proven red ten ways, each failing a different assertion and each with its mutation's anchor count asserted first: removing one command's deadline, removing one importer's, decoupling an importer to a bare literal (which the shape guard still passes, proving the two are independent), planting a fifth COPY writer untimed and then timed, removing the thirteenth site's, and the three value bounds at 30, 3 and 20. Two of those are controls the count-based checks cannot supply - RELOCATING a deadline out of its member leaves every occurrence count in the file invariant at seven, and renaming a guarded member is caught by the extractor's declaration count rather than by any site scan. No schema change and no version bump.
- **`ViewerFleetTimerGuardTests` no longer passes when the fleet-timer fan-out moves BELOW the tab early-return, which is the regression [#2907] named** ([#2923]) - `NoStoreRead_IsFiredByBothFleetTimers` cuts `OnRefreshTimerTick` at its first `return;` and intersects only the region ABOVE it with `OnOverviewTimerTick`, so relocating the fan-out below that return empties the region, empties the intersection, and satisfies the assertion BECAUSE the regression happened; measured, that mutation leaves all four of that class's facts green, and the `firstReturn.Success` staleness guard cannot see it because a relocation leaves the `return;` exactly where it was. [#2907] warned about this move in the same breath as the duplication that pin does catch - it "would stop the alert poll and the store-size read entirely while the Overview or a per-server tab is up" - and the Overview is the tab that ships selected. `ViewerFleetTimerFanOutPositionTests` now asserts the ORDERING: every fire-and-forget in the tick has a call above the first `return;`, the awaited `RefreshVisibleAsync` stays below it, and the pinned set is DERIVED from the tick with the three names [#2907] argues about asserted to still be in it, so a rename is loud rather than vacuously green. Read over `CSharpSourceWalker`'s output ([#2913]) rather than a sixth private copy of the walk. **A count-based control cannot prove this red** - a relocation leaves every occurrence count invariant, 1 to 1 - so the control is the offset comparison itself, carried in CI by a third fact that runs the check twice over one relocated fixture: detected through the walker, missed over the same text raw, where the fixture's own comment prose supplies the above-the-return hit. Nine mutations, each verified applied before it was trusted (by offset for the relocations, by count for the rest) and compiled first, each failing a different assertion - the relocation now fails the new position fact with all four old facts still green, and those four each still go red on their own mutation. Same PR folds `BraceBalanced` into `CSharpSourceWalker` and deletes both private copies - the new pin's and `ViewerFleetTimerGuardTests`' - because brace-matching is only correct over the walker's output: braces in prose and in literals are exactly what unbalance it, which is already why blanking a hole's DELIMITERS is part of `StripCommentsAndStrings`' contract. The two copies had already drifted textually, the newer one having fused the decrement into its condition, and they were proven equivalent at **every** offset of all **190** viewer `.cs` files (**2,836,304** positions, zero differences) before one was picked, with the differential harness positive-controlled against a third form that omits the decrement; the older expanded form was kept because it is the one on `dev` and matches `StatementSpanFrom`'s style in the file it moves into. `BodyAfterSignature` deliberately stays put - one class, two call sites, no other caller in the repo, and a contract specific to that pin's call-graph walk (it returns an expression-bodied member's expression WITHOUT braces so the walk can follow `RefreshAgAsync => LoadAsync();`), so moving it would grow the shared surface without removing a duplicate. The move is shown behaviour-preserving THROUGH the pins rather than merely still-green: the relocation mutation's failure message is byte-identical across it (offsets 2344 / 2773 / 2814 / 2852), so the shared helper extracts the same body both copies did, and all seven mutations still fail the same assertions with the test count unmoved at 7.
- **The source-walking idiom behind five test pins no longer blanks interpolated-string holes, so a call written inside an interpolation is visible to the scans built on it** ([#2913]) - `AlertPassCommandTimeoutTests`, `AnalysisPassCommandTimeoutTests`, `StorageCommandTimeoutTests`, `ViewerCommandTimeoutTests` and `ViewerFleetTimerGuardTests` each carried a private copy of the same walk (three copies of `StripCommentsAndStrings`, three of `StatementSpanFrom`, **five** of `SkipVerbatimString`/`SkipRegularString`), and every one blanked the ENTIRE span of a string literal, holes included - so `Log($"count={Foo()}")` had no `Foo()` in it as far as any scan could tell. **For interpolation this was latent and stayed latent: no pin's verdict changes.** Every one of the **362** command-construction sites the three timeout pins judge (193 viewer, 124 storage, 45 alert-pass) comes back with a byte-identical statement span and the same timed/untimed verdict, the method-group hand-off sweep still finds zero on all four scanned sets, and the fleet-timer pin's four assertions still pass - it is worth fixing because the failure direction is the dangerous one, not because it was lying. **Two of the gaps were not latent.** Raw string literals did not exist for the old walk: it read the opening `"""` as an empty `""` plus the start of another string, so the delimiter itself desynchronised the parse - and the viewer project alone holds raw strings in **36** files, `$"""` in **7**, and one `$$"""` (`ViewerDataService.Waits.cs`, whose wait-trend SQL interpolates `{{typeParams}}`). A `char` literal was plain code, which makes `value.Contains('"')` - a real line in `ViewerServerTab.ChartContextMenu.cs`, inside the directory both viewer pins glob recursively - open a string that runs to the next `"` anywhere in the file. **Measured over the 269 files the five pins read: the old walk leaves 2 of them bracket-unbalanced after stripping** (`ViewerServerTab.ChartContextMenu.cs` at braces -1 / parens +2, `StoreConnectionSelfTest.cs` at braces +3), which is not a curiosity when the fleet-timer pin extracts method bodies by matching braces in that same output; the new walk leaves none. Across the viewer set it puts **12,438** characters of hole contents into the code stream and takes **74,111** characters of literal text out of it (10,076 in `ViewerDataService.QueryStore.cs` alone), which for that pin's call graph is **25 call edges it could not previously see** and **235 phantom ones** it was following - `AND`, `ANY`, `COALESCE`, `date_trunc` and friends, SQL keywords leaking out of desynchronised raw strings. `_dataService` read sites are unchanged at 308 either way. All five now share one `CSharpSourceWalker` that classifies each character ONCE - hole contents are code, hole delimiters and format specifiers are not, `{{`/`}}` are escapes, `$$` needs two braces to open a hole, and `"""`, `@"..."`, `$@"`/`@$"` and `char` literals are all recognised - with `StripCommentsAndStrings` and `StatementSpanFrom` built on that one mask, so the two walks can no longer disagree about what a literal is. Widening the balance sweep from those 269 files to all **2,087** `.cs` files in the repo found one more, in the new walker rather than the old: a raw string literal cannot carry the `@` prefix, so a quote RUN after `@` is an escaped quote and not a delimiter - `@"\[""" + Regex.Escape(name) + @"""\]..."` in `Lite.Tests/CrossAppPresetValuePinTests.cs`. It left exactly two of the 2,087 unbalanced, both that shape, and **none** of the 269 the pins read, so it is pinned as an arranged fixture rather than by keeping a corpus assertion over code nobody scans. Repo-wide the old walk leaves **43** files unbalanced and the new one none. The `$$` brace-run rule is the compiler's, checked rather than assumed: `$"""{{"""` does not compile at all (CS9006), which is why the doubled-brace escape is pinned on the non-raw forms where it is legal. **The copies had already drifted** - `ViewerFleetTimerGuardTests`' regular-string skip had grown a newline stop the other four lacked, so one of five had hardening the rest did not, which is the other half of the argument for having one. The walker carries its own witnesses (**15** new tests, one per construct, each stated as a difference against the pre-#2913 walk transcribed alongside them rather than against a comment claiming it was wrong, and every `DoesNotContain` confirming the FIXTURE holds the needle before asserting the stripped output does not): re-blanking holes fails seven of them, forgetting raw strings fails two, treating `{{` as a hole fails exactly one, dropping `char` literals fails three, treating a format specifier as code fails one, reading a quote run after `@` as a raw delimiter fails one, and breaking newline preservation fails one - **seven** mutations, seven distinct failure sets, each one's anchor count asserted before the run, with all five consolidated pins staying green under every one of them, which is the latency claim above restated as a test.
- **The compose `statement_timeout` now reaches the live roles on a reload, instead of only on a service restart** ([#2918]) - it was the **only** `config_service` knob whose change an operator had to restart the service to deliver, and the reason is that it does not live in a query: it lives on the ROLES, as `ALTER ROLE viewer/mcp SET statement_timeout`, written once per process by startup provisioning. [#2917] fixed the held config's truthfulness and deliberately changed nothing here, so a reload still observed the new value and left the roles on whatever the last start wrote. **That is worst in the one situation the backstop exists for** - a runaway aggregation pinning a raw, no-rollup store - where "restart the collector" is a bad answer. `ReloadFromStoreAsync` now re-asserts the two statements, **only on a real change**: a `config_version` bump fires on any `config_service` or schedule write, and this is a catalog write, so re-asserting unconditionally would charge every unrelated reload for a knob nobody touched. There was no privilege obstacle to find - the service's store connection is the owner (`PostgresConfig.ConnectAs` is the *Viewer's* role selector and is documented as ignored by the service). Gated exactly as startup provisioning is (managed + Windows), because that is where these roles are CREATED; a BYO store provisions them out-of-band through `tools/provision-roles.sql` and names them itself, so `ALTER ROLE viewer` there would be guessing at an identity we do not own. **A role `SET` only takes on that role's NEXT session**, which is what makes it cheap and safe - it cannot disturb a query already running, and an already-connected viewer keeps the old ceiling until it reconnects, so lowering the ceiling bounds the next runaway rather than the one in flight. **The load-bearing invariant is no-drift**, because two paths now write the same pair of statements and a disagreement would fail nothing - both would run, and the live ceiling would depend on which touched the roles last. So there is exactly ONE renderer (`DarlingManagedRoles.BuildComposeStatementTimeoutSql`), the provisioning batch embeds its output, and the pin splices the **shipped** renderer into the assertion rather than a retyped copy - plus a count check, since an embed that left the old inline copy behind would still satisfy `Contains` while writing the ceiling twice. The reload decision is a pure static predicate rather than a condition buried in the worker loop, so **when to pay a catalog write** - the whole design - is testable without a host, including that the baseline advances only on SUCCESS: recording the attempt would make one transient failure permanent, leaving the roles on the old ceiling forever while the held config claimed otherwise, which is [#2917]'s split reintroduced one layer down. **Review caught a first-run ordering trap in the first cut of this**, worth recording because it is the same defect one level down: role provisioning runs BEFORE `SeedIfEmptyAsync`, so on a brand-new managed store there is no `config_service` row to read and the roles get the 15 s default, and only then does the seed insert `darling.json`'s value. Seeding the reload baseline from the post-seed store view therefore recorded a value the roles never received - and because the gate fires only on a difference, that mismatch was PERMANENT for the life of the process, defeating no-restart delivery for precisely the operator who set the knob up front. `EnsureProvisionedAsync` now RETURNS the clamped value it wrote and the baseline is seeded from that, so it means what its name says; it stays at the -1 sentinel if provisioning threw, so the first reload converges rather than trusting a write that did not land. The clamp itself is now the one in `StoreConfigProvider` at all three sites rather than a re-derived formula - clamping the SQL text's bounds by a second copy of the rule, in a change whose whole point is removing that hazard from the SQL text, was not a coherent place to stop.
- **A control-plane reload no longer drops the compose `statement_timeout` knob on the floor** ([#2917]) - `StoreConfigProvider.ApplyToConfig` assigns every scalar in `StoreConfigView` that has a same-named `DarlingConfig` property, and `ComposeStatementTimeoutSeconds` was the **one exception** out of seven - seeded to `config_service`, selected by the view read, clamped to [5,600], mapped into the view, and then never assigned, so `config.ComposeStatementTimeoutSeconds` reported the `darling.json` value for the life of the process while the store said otherwise. **This did not change the effective ceiling**, and the distinction is the whole finding: nothing reads the held property, because `DarlingManagedRoles.ReadComposeStatementTimeoutAsync` reads `config_service` with its own `SELECT` and bakes the value into `ALTER ROLE viewer/mcp SET statement_timeout` at startup provisioning. So an operator's change was delivered on the next managed start all along - [#2357]'s stated design, and what the doc comment on `StoreConfigView.ComposeStatementTimeoutSeconds` has always said. What was broken was the held config's **truthfulness**, made worse by the `DarlingConfig` doc comment inviting a reader to trust it ("the store is authoritative afterwards, like every other value here"): a future consumer reading the held value would have silently gotten the file value. That comment now states the restart-scoped delivery, and the *delivery* question - a reload observes the new value without re-asserting it onto the roles, unlike every other `config_service` knob - is filed separately as [#2918] rather than changed here. **The pin is family-derived, not another per-knob assertion** ([#2917]): every existing test on this path names its own knob, which is exactly why the seventh was dropped and why the eighth would be, so `StoreConfigViewPropagationTests` reflects over the real types and round-trips **every** view scalar with a same-named, same-typed, settable `DarlingConfig` property through `ApplyToConfig` - two probe values apiece, each pre-setting the held config to the other so an absent assignment cannot pass by landing on a shared default. The deliberately-unmatched scalars (`ConfigVersion` and `Paused`, both worker-held; the four `Mcp*`/`Web*`, nested under `config.Mcp`/`config.Web`) are pinned as a set with written reasons, so a new view scalar either gets covered automatically or fails until someone justifies the exclusion. **"Scalar" means any value type or `string`, not `IsPrimitive || string`** - the filter the pin was first written with, and a hole in it, since `IsPrimitive` is false for `enum`, `decimal`, `DateTime`, `Guid` and `TimeSpan`: a future knob of any of those types would have been invisible to *both* tests, unprobed for propagation AND absent from the unmatched set meant to force a written reason, with the unprobeable-type throw unable to fire because it only sees what the filter already admitted. Widening it is what makes that throw load-bearing; enums are handled generically (two distinct declared members) and the widened filter admits exactly the same 13 properties today, so nothing about current coverage changed. Verified red first: reverting the one assignment fails `EveryMatchedScalar_IsPropagatedByApplyToConfig` naming the dropped knob.
- **The per-database phase split is now printed on the fault path, which is the one case it was added to explain** ([#2896]) - [#2855] gave the per-database branch a `connect: + open: + drain: + other:` split and stamped every figure, plus `PerDatabasePhasesMeasured`, from a `finally` - so a database whose connect times out or whose read is cut by the per-database wall clock really does record what it burned. Its only log site sat on the SUCCESS path past the flush, and a fault reaches one of three catch arms, so "this database is bound by connect" produced an error string and nothing else. The generic arm logs at Debug, so on a default level it produced only the error string. **Neither option [#2896] recorded was taken, and the reason is that one of them was unsound rather than merely expensive.** The line decomposes `dbSqlMs`, which is not log-only - it feeds `sqlMs`, `fanout.Observe(...)` and `collection_log.sql_duration_ms` - and the stopwatch was declared inside the `try`, below the watermark read and `BuildQuery`. A **second stopwatch** started above the `try` would not have measured the same interval: `PerDatabaseOtherMsFrom`'s own contract states that the watermark read and `BuildQuery` are OUTSIDE the parent, so its residual would have meant something different from the success line's under the same name - the same shape as printing a decomposition that does not add up, only harder to notice. A **second, parentless line shape** needed nothing hoisted but taught the log parsers outside this repo a second shape, which is the [#2811]/[#2851] objection. Instead only the DECLARATION moved above the `try`; the START is on the same statement it always was, so the interval is unchanged, `dbSqlMs` is the same number, and one stopwatch still covers it. A null slice now means exactly "the fault landed before the timed region", and the compiler enforces that check rather than a comment claiming it. **The per-iteration stamp clear had to move too, and no measurement suggested it.** [#2855] cleared the three figures and the flag just below the stopwatch - sufficient while only the success path read them, because every success passes it - but a fault in the watermark read, in the adaptive shrink's store write, or in `BuildQuery` reaches the catch arms WITHOUT having passed it, holding the previous database's flag and figures. Reading the split there would have confidently attributed another database's connect to this one, which is precisely the failure [#2855]'s clear exists to prevent, on the half of the iteration it did not cover. The clear now runs at the top of the `try`, which also makes the flag a SUFFICIENT gate on the fault path: it is set only from the connect's `finally`, downstream of both the clear and the stopwatch. **The generic arm stays at Debug, and that is the level question answered rather than deferred.** What makes a database that has failed every cycle for a week visible is not a log level - it is `BuildPartialFailureNote`, which composes "N of M database(s) failed", up to three named plus the first error, into `collection_log.error_message`, where it is persisted, queryable, and reported by `get_collection_health` as `last_note` / `note_count`; a total loss is already a Warning plus a rethrow that reaches the Capture Down self-alert. Raising the arm would emit one Warning per database per cycle per collector, the flood [#1875]'s one-capped-burst rule exists to stop, and would add no signal that is not already stored. So the split rides its arm - Warning beside the budget arm, Debug beside the skip - because a phase line louder than the error it decomposes puts a timing on the default log with no reason next to it. **This adds visibility and nothing else**: no work moves, nothing is persisted, the schema stays at V110, and the success line and both `dbSqlMs` consumers are byte-identical. **The frequency measurement [#2896] asked for could not be taken, and the reason is worth recording.** `RunsPerDatabase` is `target.IsAzureSqlDb` for all nine SQL Server collectors that can reach this branch, and `IsAzureSqlDb` is `engineEdition == 5`; across the 84 monitored SQL Server targets on the two stores that can be read there is **no target at edition 5** - all are Amazon RDS at edition 3 - so the branch has executed **zero** times there, by construction rather than by sampling, and `note_count` is 0 on all 39 collectors of a sampled member over 1,809 runs.
