That is not the same as the branch being dead: **seven PostgreSQL collectors return `RunsPerDatabase => true` unconditionally** (`pg_index_bloat`, `pg_index_usage_stats`, `pg_table_bloat_stats`, `pg_autovacuum_stats`, `pg_column_stats`, `pg_predicate_stats`, `pg_extension_availability`), so this identical loop - same three catch arms, same stamps, same Debug - runs on every Aurora target, where one connection per database per cycle is exactly the cost `connect:` exists to name. The store holding that fleet was not reachable for this change, so the fault-path RATE on it is unmeasured and the level decision above rests on the persisted-note argument rather than on a count. Pinned by calling the shipped method against the built assembly rather than by asserting on its source text, with the ordering claims - clear before the watermark read, stopwatch start after it and after `BuildQuery`, one stopwatch and not two - scoped to the per-database branch, because the first cut of that pin counted whole-file and matched the PLAIN branch's own `sqlSlice` as a second one. Proven red nine ways, each failing a different assertion and each with its anchor count asserted first: dropping the null guard, gating on `connect > 0` instead of the flag, reading the slice twice, starting it above the watermark read, moving the clear back to its [#2855] position, raising the generic arm to Warning, borrowing the success line's `(N rows, pg:Nms)` tail, dropping one arm's emission, and emitting on the success path as well.
- **The viewer's two fleet timers can no longer stack store reads, and the Overview no longer double-fires one of them** ([#2907]) - `OnRefreshTimerTick` fired `RefreshServerStatusAsync` and `RefreshStoreSizeAsync` unawaited with **no in-flight guard**, while the two siblings in that same fan-out both had one (`_alertPollInFlight`, and `_refreshInFlight` plus its `_refreshRequested` replay). So a read that outlived the interval was simply joined by the next tick's. Three numbers make that expensive rather than untidy: the interval is `NocRefreshIntervalSeconds`, default 30 but clamped to a floor of **10**; the store connection string sets **`MaxPoolSize = 10`** deliberately ([#1566]), so read eleven waits `ConnectionTimeoutSeconds` for a slot and then throws a **connect** error that misattributes a slow store to the network; and the fan-out is not the "cheap pair of single-query reads" its comment claimed - `RefreshServerStatusAsync` awaits a second read through `UpdateCollectorHealthTextAsync`, `PollAlertsAsync` awaits a third through `UpdateServerSilencedAsync`, so one tick is **five** store reads before `RefreshVisibleAsync` starts, six while the AG tab is still hidden. **The two guards are deliberately different shapes**, because a dropped request is recoverable in one case and not the other: store size takes no scope, so the in-flight read paints the same field with the same answer and a plain drop loses nothing, whereas the freshness read is fetched before an add/edit/remove and applied to the fleet after it, so a server registered mid-read is absent from the dictionary and paints as never-collected - that caller gets the coalescing replay, and it is **opt-in per call site** so the periodic callers still drop, since replaying a timer would delete the interval's gap precisely when the store is slowest. `OnOverviewTimerTick` also stopped duplicating the fan-out; moving `OnRefreshTimerTick`'s fan-out below its tab early-return instead would have been a regression, silencing the alert poll and the store-size read entirely while a per-server tab is up, which is what "regardless of the visible tab" is there for. [#2901] bounded each of these reads at 15s and deliberately left the guarding alone; a deadline caps how long a **stacked** read holds a permit, which is a strictly smaller claim than not stacking. Pinned by walking the shipped source rather than by a unit test, because the defect was a seam and not a logic error - no path from a fan-out target to a `_dataService` read may exist without a single-flight guard on it, the guard matched by SHAPE so the three that predate the pin (`_refreshInFlight`, `_alertPollInFlight`, `AvailabilityGroupsTab`'s `_loading`, one level down) all satisfy it without an allowlist. Proven red five ways, including the one a naive check would have excused: the fix moves the freshness read into a helper, so deleting the guard leaves the guarded method itself touching no store at all.
- **Deferred plan and text fetch debt is no longer paid by whichever collector visits the database next** ([#2902]) - the two carryover dictionaries [#2312]'s activity-driven fetch defers budget-capped ids into were keyed by `(server, database)` with no collector in the key, while the fetch that spends them is gated on a SKU flag - `CapturePlanXml` for plans, `FetchQueryTextSeparately` for text - rather than on a collector identity. **Five collectors reach that fetch off Azure** (`query_store`, `plan_correction`, `query_store_health`, `index_object_stats`, `database_scoped_config` - derived by invoking their own `RunsPerDatabase`/`BuildEnumerationQuery`, never listed by hand), and only `query_store` can ever CREATE debt there, because `ExtractPlanReferences` and its text twin return empty for any other collector's batch. So the other four arrived having referenced nothing, found `query_store`'s deferred ids under the key they happened to share, drained them, and had the fetch's wall clock recorded against their own `collection_log` row. **~346 s of Query Store fetch time over 38 h on one monitoring host was billed to `plan_correction`, `query_store_health` and `index_object_stats`** - parsed from the service log 2026-09-03 00:00 to 2026-09-04 14:12 UTC across 2,769 `query_store` runs, and identifiable because those runs carry a signature nothing else produces: **`probed = 0` with `ids > 0`**, a collector that fetched ids it never probed for. The store's own two-day per-collector aggregate says the same thing from the other side: `query_store_health` reads one cheap row per database per hour and averages 304 ms per run over 1,731 runs on 42 servers, with a **72,454 ms worst execution** in that window - a 238x tail on a collector whose own read cannot produce one. The key now carries the collector, so a collector drains only debt it created. The same widening covers the two sibling dictionaries built from the same key (`_observedPlanSize`'s learned plan size and backoff count, and `_textFetchFailures`), whose per-database counters those foreign visits were also resetting on their nothing-owed return path. **What this does NOT change.** It moves no work and saves none - the same ids are still fetched and the same bytes still ship, and `query_store`'s own measured cost rises by roughly what its siblings stop being charged. It is an attribution fix, and it is worth making because [#2847], [#2841] and [#2860] all read `collection_log` per collector, so this silently moved time between collectors and between cadence TIERS in the series those investigations reason from. It does change WHEN the debt is paid: it now waits for `query_store`'s next visit rather than the first of five, which at the default cadences is at most one 5-minute cycle later - `query_store` and `plan_correction` are both on the 5-minute tier while `query_store_health` is hourly, `index_object_stats` daily and `database_scoped_config` on-load only, so the drain opportunities that materially existed were the two 5-minute ones and the change roughly halves them. **There is no cap and no eviction on the debt** - ids leave only by landing, by proving target-side-gone on a provably uncut pass, or with the process - so halving that drain rate makes an existing capacity question VISIBLE rather than creating one: if `query_store`'s own candidate cap cannot cover what its probe finds missing, the backlog grows, and that was already true with `plan_correction`'s free passes masking it. [#2860]'s persisted phase columns are the instrument to watch it with. Pinned behaviourally rather than by shape - "the key has three fields" would pass for a key carrying a collection time, which fixes nothing and drops every deferred id on the floor - over the family derived from `CollectorCatalog.All`, with the dictionaries' own declared key type and the runner's own key builder as the artifacts under test, plus an IL read of the built assembly - on [#2898]'s shared decoder, per state machine, with the probe calls as positive controls checked first - proving both fetch bodies call that builder instead of re-inlining a tuple. Each of the five assertions was proven red by its own mutation of the shipped source: reverting the key (siblings drain the owner's ids), making it unique per call (the owner loses its own), dropping the database (two databases alias), re-inlining one key (only one body calls the builder), and a family member ceasing to enumerate (the non-vacuity floor). No schema change: the per-phase persistence rung is [#2860]'s and comes after this.
- **The server-scoped watermark read no longer runs for a collector whose own cycle throws the answer away** ([#2797]) - `RunAsync` dispatches down exactly one of three paths, and two of them assign `context.Watermark` from a PER-DATABASE read before any query is built: the Azure per-database connection loop, and the enumerated per-item loop. A collector taking either path still executed the server-scoped `GetLastCollectedTimeAsync` on every cycle and read its answer with nothing. It is now skipped on exactly those two paths. On the current catalog that is `query_store` on every target, plus `deadlocks` / `blocked_process_report` / `long_query_completions` on Azure SQL DB only - **7 of 168 (definition, target) pairs**, verified by invoking the shipped predicate over `CollectorCatalog.All` in the built assembly rather than by reading the source. **The gate the issue itself proposed would have shipped a data-correctness bug, and that is the whole reason this is a DISPATCH-PATH predicate rather than a watermark-column one.** [#2797] suggested `PerDatabaseWatermarkColumn is not null`, the signal the per-item paths already key on. Four collectors declare both watermark columns and all four declare `RunsPerDatabase => target.IsAzureSqlDb`, but only `query_store` overrides `BuildEnumerationQuery`; the other three inherit `CollectorDefinitionBase`'s `=> null`. So OFF Azure - the entire on-prem and RDS fleet - three of the four fall through to the plain server-scoped path and genuinely CONSUME the value, and gating on the column alone would have handed them a null watermark every cycle and made them re-collect their whole fallback window forever: [#2795]'s defect, arrived at from the other side. The fleet said the same thing before the change, because V108's phase columns are written only on the server-scoped path and so identify dispatch directly - over 90 minutes on 42 servers `blocked_process_report` populated `sql_open_ms` on 2,122 of 2,122 runs and `deadlocks` on 632 of 632, while `query_store` populated it on **0 of 633**. **What this is worth, with its provenance, because the figure is easy to misattribute.** The read removed is `MAX(last_execution_time) ... WHERE server_id = $1 AND collection_time > $2` over `collect.query_store_stats`, the store's largest table, once per `query_store` cycle per server. The only direct measurement of that read is [#2796]'s, taken as it was being bounded: **2.9-5.9 s** warm-to-cold against a 62.5 GB table (40.7 s and 50.6 s before the bound). It cannot be confirmed from the fleet today, and that is a stated gap rather than an omission - `ServerWatermarkMs` reaches the phase log only under `ServerPhasesMeasured`, which the enumerated path leaves false, and V108's `watermark_ms` column is persisted on that same branch, so for `query_store` specifically this read's cost is recorded in neither the log nor the store ([#2860]). The `wm:` field that IS populated for `query_store` in the phase log belongs to the per-item line and measures the PER-DATABASE read (p50 41 ms over 822 samples): a different read, legitimate incremental work, and untouched here - so reading it as the server-scoped cost understates this change by whatever the ratio is between a whole-server `MAX` over the three-hour read-floor window and one database's. The `deadlocks` and `blocked_process_report` server-scoped reads visible in that same log (p50 9 ms and 5 ms) are also legitimate and deliberately stay. **Pinned over the FAMILY derived from `CollectorCatalog.All`**, not by naming collectors, the way [#2796]'s pins are derived from the runner's method family: a fifth collector that declares both columns and enumerates is covered the day it appears, and one that stops enumerating is un-covered automatically. The load-bearing assertion is the NEGATIVE one - that the three server-scoped members still get a non-null watermark off Azure - and it was verified red against the issue's own proposed gate before being left green. The enumeration half of the predicate is derived by CALLING the definition's `BuildEnumerationQuery` rather than mirrored into a second declared flag, so the two cannot drift apart; the probe context that makes that answerable before the cycle's own context exists (`HasCollectedBefore` is computed FROM the gated read, and it, `NumericWatermark` and `State` are init-only) is sound only while null-ness depends on `Target` alone, which a further pin asserts across **5,376** (definition, target, context) combinations instead of assuming. Skipping the read also had to gate `HasCollectedBefore`, whose branch keys off `watermark is null` and would otherwise have fired its own store query on precisely the cycles the change exists to make cheaper.
- **The IL-scan idiom behind five reachability pins is now a real instruction decoder, and can see generic callees** ([#2898]) - `ServerScopePhaseSplitTests`, `FetchProbeStampReachabilityTests`, `ProbeDenominatorTests`, `FetchStoreConnectionBorrowTests` and `ServerWatermarkDispatchGateTests` each carried their own copy of a walk that tested every byte offset for `call` (0x28) / `callvirt` (0x6F) and read the next four bytes as a metadata token. Two soundness problems, neither of which was producing a wrong answer yet - which is the reason to fix them before one does. First, two of the five advanced the cursor four bytes on a match; a byte inside another instruction's operand can look like a call, so a match is not necessarily an instruction boundary, and skipping from a non-boundary can land past a real call's own token. That is a **false negative**, the dangerous direction for these pins, because a stamp that stopped being called from its handler would read as still called - the #2816 defect they exist to prevent. On the built service assembly: **13,007 method bodies, 70,775 genuine call sites, 415 byte offsets that look like a call and are not, and 587 genuine call sites the skipping arithmetic can never visit.** It did not bite: with the tracked set widened to *every* MemberRef/MethodDef/MethodSpec token in the assembly the skipping form loses zero call sites, because no phantom match happens to carry a live token within four bytes of a real one - a property of one build's token VALUES, not of the scan. Second, all five built their token map from `MemberReferences` and `MethodDefinitions` only, so a call to a **generic** method - emitted against a `MethodSpec` token (table 0x2B) pointing at the underlying member - reported zero call sites. **4,356 of the assembly's genuine call sites carry a MethodSpec token and 51 distinct callee names are invisible without resolving it** - mostly BCL generics, but three of them ours: `WithGeminiCompatibleTools` (50 sites), `CollectAsync` (7) and `WriteBatchAsync` (4), the last defined in `DarlingCollectorRunner.cs`, the same class these pins scan. That gap had already cost [#2890]'s sibling pin a red on `ServerWatermarkIsDiscarded`, generic in `TRow` and called every cycle, and it is worse than a loud failure in `FetchStoreConnectionBorrowTests`, whose assertion is that a count is ZERO: a generic acquisition would have satisfied it vacuously. All five now share one `IlCallSiteScanner` that decodes instructions with an operand-length table **derived by reflection over `System.Reflection.Emit.OpCodes`** rather than hand-transcribed, resolves `MethodSpec` back to the underlying member so callers still ask by plain name, and **throws** on an unknown opcode or a body that does not decode to exactly its own length instead of quietly reporting offsets that cannot be trusted. The scanner carries its own witnesses, and the two defects needed different shapes of proof: the MethodSpec gap is reproducible against the shipped assembly (deleting the loop takes `WriteBatchAsync` to zero and fails the pin), while the skip is not, so it is pinned on a hand-built body where the coincidence is arranged rather than hoped for - substituting the skipping form for the decoder fails that test and only that test, leaving all twelve pre-existing assertions green, which is precisely the measurement above restated as a test. A third witness decodes every body in the service assembly and requires each to end exactly on its own length; deliberately mis-declaring one operand length turns it and the pins that depend on it red.
- **Every command on the Darling service's MCP and web read surface now carries an explicit deadline** ([#2874]) - all **125** of those sites set no `CommandTimeout` and inherited Npgsql's undocumented 30 s default: **118** under `Mcp/` (114 of them the `.CreateCommand(` shape [#2874]'s census could not see, and `Mcp/DarlingDataReader.cs` is 26 on its own), the six `CustomViewStore` statements both surfaces persist through, and the composed-query runner in `DarlingWebEndpoints`. Re-derived rather than taken from the issue, with the landed pins' own machinery lifted verbatim and **positive controls first**: the harness reproduces `.Storage` at 124 sites (74 / 50 by shape), `.Viewer` at 193 (2 / 191) and `.Analysis` at 73, **zero untimed in all three**, which is what establishes it counts the way the landed pins count; it then puts `.Service` at 261 sites / 201 untimed, of which these 125 are group A - 126 sites after the change, the extra one being the deadline resolver's own read, which is a shipped-shape read and carries `ReadSeconds` like any other. **The enclosing constraint here is a kind this sweep had not met.** Not a clock ([#2871]'s `CancelAfter`) and not a connection permit ([#2874]'s viewer group, `MaxPoolSize = 10`), but a SERVER-SIDE `statement_timeout` on the login role - `ALTER ROLE mcp/viewer SET statement_timeout`, written by startup provisioning from `config_service.compose_statement_timeout_seconds`, shipped default 15 s, clamped 5-600. It is a ROLE setting, so it bounds every statement those identities run and not merely the composed queries it is named for. **Why a client-side deadline does not simply defer to it, per deployment shape.** (1) **Bring-your-own PostgreSQL has no such GUC at all**: `Darling/tools/provision-roles.sql` creates `admin` and `viewer` and deliberately no `mcp` role, and sets `statement_timeout` on `viewer` only, while the MCP host in that mode connects with the operator's own `postgres.connectionString` - so on a BYO store this surface has NO server-side bound and Npgsql's default is currently the only thing bounding it. A BYO operator also has no knob here, the store column reaching the roles only through managed provisioning, which is why these values are deliberately not aggressive. (2) On a managed store the GUC is **operator-tunable to 600 s and per-ROLE**, so raising it for one heavy custom view raises it for all 124 shipped reads. (3) A role `SET` takes effect on that role's **next session** - `DarlingConfig` says so outright, "this is not a kill switch" - whereas a `CommandTimeout` binds the very next command on an already-open pooled connection. **The asymmetry that makes it necessary rather than tidy:** none of the 136 `[McpServerTool]` methods takes a `CancellationToken` - every reader declares `CancellationToken cancellationToken = default`, every tool calls it without one, and the single tools file that names a token passes `CancellationToken.None` explicitly - so an agent that abandons a call leaves the query running AND the pooled backend held. The web half is the mirror image and threads `HttpContext.RequestAborted` throughout, so the deadline is a backstop there and the only bound on the MCP side. **The permit is stated at its real strength rather than borrowed.** The MCP host builds its OWN data source, separate from the web host's and from the worker's collection pool - three independent `NpgsqlDataSource.Create` calls on connection strings that differ by `Username` - so a stalled MCP read **cannot** starve fleet-wide collection through the pool, and the widest fan-out here is a `Task.WhenAll` over TWO reads against `MaxPoolSize = 24`, nothing like the viewer's ten-against-ten. What the bound still buys is the reason it exists: every pooled connection is a live `postgres.exe` PROCESS, each spawn re-reserves the shared memory region, and this store logged 8 "could not reserve shared memory region" retries on 2026-09-03 - so a longer hold means more spawns, not just a busier pool. **Two regimes, not one.** The 124 shipped-shape commands take a new **20 s** `McpCommandDeadlines.ReadSeconds` - "read" naming the SURFACE rather than the verb, since **six** of them are writes (the custom-view insert/update/delete, the `config_alert_settings` update behind the alert-tuning tool, and the server-registry insert and delete behind `add_servers`/`remove_server`), which share this bound because they share the REGIME exactly and are each a single-row primary-key statement on a `config` table, strictly cheaper than the reads the bound was floored on. Its FLOOR is the managed store's own ceiling: 15 s, and it **fires on this surface in production today** - on 2026-09-04 `get_query_store_duration_trend` over a 7-day window returned `57014: canceling statement due to statement timeout` against the live 42-server store with [#2736]'s corrected-rollup routing already engaged, while the same read at its shipped 24 h default returned its full series. Sitting **above** 15 s is what keeps the server-side timeout the one that fires on a managed store, because `57014` NAMES its cause where Npgsql's own deadline renders as "Exception while reading from stream" and gets read as a network fault - the misdiagnosis [#2826] exists to prevent. That floor dominates the measured one by more than an order of magnitude, this family's verified reads being sub-second. Its CEILING is the 30 s it replaces, which the viewer group's standard requires it to sit **meaningfully** under rather than at, plus a RELATIONAL bound against `StorageCommandDeadlines.McpReadSeconds` - the `DarlingPg*Reader` family serving these same 136 tools from `.Storage` - so one tool call can never get a looser ceiling depending on which project holds the reader. **The composed-query runner could not take that constant**, and that is the split: it is the one site running OPERATOR-AUTHORED SQL, and `compose_statement_timeout_seconds` is the operator's declaration of how long that class may run, so a fixed 20 s would cut off the panel the knob was raised to 120 s for - a regression dressed as a fix. So it does not take a constant at all: `McpCommandDeadlines.ResolveComposedQuerySecondsAsync` READS `config.config_service.compose_statement_timeout_seconds` and clamps it through the store's own `ClampComposeStatementTimeoutSeconds`, so the client-side deadline is the operator's ACTUAL setting rather than the ceiling they could theoretically have chosen. **Read rather than plumbed, and that is the load-bearing choice.** The store is authoritative for this knob and [#2918] made it hot-swappable, so a value captured at host start is simply wrong after the first change - and the value a host COULD plumb is the file-loaded `DarlingConfig.ComposeStatementTimeoutSeconds`, which is only the SEED. Reading per panel run has no cache to go stale, and it is also what keeps the two callers identical: `MapAll` has the host's config in scope while the MCP tool has only the data source, so plumbing would have handed one surface the file value and the other a store read - exactly the divergence the single shared runner exists to prevent. The near miss is three lines away in the same method: `ComposeStoreAvailability.GetRollupsAsync` is deliberately cached PER DATA SOURCE, because which rollups a store has changes only when someone migrates it, and caching the deadline the same way compiles and passes every band assertion while pinning it to whatever the knob said the first time a panel ran. Resolved once per RUN - so a panel with annotation overlays pays one read rather than one per source, and every query in one run shares one ceiling, matching the role `statement_timeout` they all run under. `ComposedQueryFallbackSeconds` is **15**, the same shipped default `DarlingConfig`, `ComposeSpec` and the clamp's own non-positive arm all use, so a store that cannot answer behaves like a store nobody has tuned; failing OPEN to something larger would grant more time precisely when the store is least able to answer for itself, which is the wrong direction for the only bound a BYO store gets. **The pin carries a claim none of the landed ones needed: store-vs-target.** `.Storage` and `.Viewer` are Npgsql-store-only projects, so "every command sets a deadline" was the whole invariant there; `.Service` also builds commands against MONITORED targets, where a store-derived constant would be wrong in a way nothing else would catch. All 125 sites here were classified by hand and all 125 are store commands - 115 `postgres.CreateCommand(`, 6 `_dataSource.CreateCommand(`, and 4 `new NpgsqlCommand(sql, connection)` whose connection comes from `postgres.OpenConnectionAsync` - and a receiver allowlist writes that classification down, with the two real target shapes (`ITargetProvider.CreateCommand(plan, connection, commandTimeoutSeconds)`, where the deadline is threaded and stronger than a constant, and the transaction-scoped HypoPG form) pinned as negatives so a future target command here fails asking for a decision instead of inheriting a store bound. Scoped to `Mcp/` **recursively** plus those two files by NAME rather than globbing the project, because the project root is shared with four further groups of this sweep and a project-wide glob would claim regimes this pin has not derived. It also covers the **fourth** construction shape - `BeginBinaryImportAsync`, where `NpgsqlBinaryImporter.Timeout` is initialised from the connection's `CommandTimeout` and no landed pin's regex can see it - asserting zero in this scope, so the project's four live importers being elsewhere is a checked fact rather than an assumption; and the method-group shape, which is absent from `.Service` and stays absent. Every scan reads the **stripped** text, including the construction scan, which the landed pins run over raw source: a prose mention of `.CreateCommand(` would be a phantom untimed site there, at a line number no edit could fix. This is not hypothetical in kind - `Mcp/DarlingMcpStoreMetricsTools.cs` contains the sentence "the two readers above go through postgres.CreateCommand and leave nothing open", which misses both regexes only because what follows the name is a space and a word rather than a delimiter. The divergence changes no count (125 either way) and closes a false-positive direction. **What this does not do**: no user-reported failure is closed by it. The 7-day Query Store window measured above is bounded by the ROLE, not by this change, and stays bounded by it; no timing was taken with a client-side deadline in place, and the production `compose_statement_timeout_seconds` was not read back - the 15 s used throughout is the shipped default and the clamp, verified in source rather than on the box. A local PostgreSQL 17 / TimescaleDB 2.29.2 store WAS stood up for the follow-on, built by the product's own `PgMigrations.MigrateAsync`, but it was used to prove the resolver's BEHAVIOUR - that it sees a changed value, clamps an out-of-range row, and falls back on a migrated-but-unseeded store and on a store with no `config_service` table - not to measure any read's duration; it holds no collected data.  **A FIFTH construction shape, found by a red-first variant that came back green.** The planted monitored-target offender used `new Npgsql.NpgsqlCommand(` and the pin did not see it: no regex in this sweep matches a QUALIFIED type name. The form occurs exactly once in the repo - `DarlingWorker.cs`'s `pg_stat_statements` text fetch - and that site is a monitored-TARGET command already carrying its own 60 s, so the blind spot has no defect behind it and the untimed census is unchanged; the SITE census for `.Service` is 262 rather than 261. Both this pin's construction scan and its receiver allowlist now accept the qualified spelling, with the qualified store form pinned as ACCEPTED so the widening cannot turn a legitimate construction into an unclassified-receiver false positive. **Proven red fifteen ways, one mutation at a time, each verified applied by reading the file back rather than by a diffstat and each rebuilt** (a mutation that does not compile proves nothing): removing a deadline from the 26-site reader, from a block-form `using` body and from `CustomViewStore`; planting an untimed command in an `Mcp/` subdirectory; planting a TIMED monitored-target command both unqualified and qualified; planting a bare method-group hand-off; planting an untimed `BeginBinaryImportAsync`; moving the read value to 15, 5 and 30 (three different bounds, three different messages); dropping `StorageCommandDeadlines.McpReadSeconds` to 15 to isolate the relational ceiling from the numeric one; decoupling the composed-query value from the clamp; narrowing the receiver allowlist so a real store shape is rejected; and - because a count-based control cannot see a relocation, every occurrence count staying 1 - MOVING `CustomViewStore.cs`, which the named-file existence check catches. The receiver allowlist is also **word-boundaried**, and the boundary guards the direction that matters: without it `targetPostgres.CreateCommand(` matches `postgres\.CreateCommand` as a substring, so a monitored-target command on any variable whose name ENDS in a store receiver's name would be waved through as a store command - a false ACCEPT in the very guard that exists to stop one. Pinned with `notpostgres`, `targetPostgres` and `targetConnection` as rejected and `this._dataSource` as accepted, and proven red by dropping the lookbehind. **The live-value resolver adds eight more, and two of them are the reason it has a live test at all:** memoising the resolved value in a static, and memoising it per data source the way the rollup probe beside it deliberately does - both compile, both pass every band assertion, and both are caught only by a test that CHANGES the column and requires the second resolve to see it. Also proven red by dropping the clamp, moving the fallback to 600 and to 0 (`CommandTimeout = 0` meaning NO LIMIT, the worst possible reading of "I could not find out"), reverting the runner to a compile-time constant, stripping the deadline from the resolver's own read, and - a MOVE, so every occurrence count stays invariant at 1 - relocating the resolve out of the shared runner into the per-query method, which only the structural ordering control can see. Both staleness variants first came back GREEN because the planted cache was never populated: a mutation that compiles but changes no behaviour proves exactly as little as one that does not compile, and "assert the mutation applied" has to mean the behaviour moved, not that the bytes did. No schema change and no version bump.
- **Every command in the Darling viewer now carries an explicit deadline** ([#2874]) - all **193** of the project's command sites set no `CommandTimeout` and inherited Npgsql's undocumented 30 s default. That count is a correction: [#2874]'s census says 192, and the 193rd is a shape neither of its regexes can match - `ViewerDataService.Blocking.cs` handed `connection.CreateCommand` over as a bare METHOD GROUP to `PgBlockingPairRowQuery.AppendDmvSnapshotRowsAsync`, which builds the command and sets its `CommandText` in a different project entirely, so the site read as clean while inheriting the default; it now takes a factory lambda that stamps the deadline, and the pin has a second scan for that shape. **Three budget regimes, three constants - and the interesting result is how few.** Interactive and background-refresh reads are NOT separable here: the fleet timer and the per-tab auto-refresh timer call the same `ViewerDataService` methods a user gesture calls (`RefreshActiveInnerTabAsync` is literally the tab-activation method), and the set of reads reached ONLY by the unattended fleet fan-out is **empty** - all seven are also reached by a gesture or a visible-tab load - so splitting them needs a budget threaded through every call site, not a constant. An export regime was looked for and does not exist: `PerformanceMonitor.Ui.DataGridExport` is synchronous and store-unaware and iterates `grid.Items`, so all ten CSV/copy surfaces format rows a panel load already paid for. The 188 interactive/refresh sites take a new **15 s** `ViewerCommandDeadlines.InteractiveReadSeconds`, bounded below by measurement and above by a PERMIT rather than a budget. Below: a store stood up by the product's own `PgMigrations.MigrateAsync` at V109 with TimescaleDB and seeded to exact production per-server density (from `get_collector_cost` over 2 days x 42 servers - 189,414 `query_stats` rows/server/day) across the collector's full 30-day retention horizon, 5.68 M `query_stats` and 1.98 M `procedure_stats` rows for one server; the heaviest shipped per-server read, `TopQueriesSql` (dumped from the built assembly, not retyped), measured **cold** at 588 ms on the default 1-hour preset, 1.12 s on the widest 7-day preset and **3.01 s** on a 30-day custom range - the widest window any shipped read can ask for, since retention drops the data behind it. Above: **nothing encloses these reads at all** - no `CancelAfter`, no `SemaphoreSlim`, no `WaitAsync`, and no request timeout, because the viewer is a WPF `WinExe` that hosts no web endpoint - the same finding [#2882] and [#2888] both made, and the same reason this errs short. What they compete for is the ten-connection pool (`MaxPoolSize = 10`, set on the managed-derived string for [#1566]), and `CorrelatedTimelineLanesControl` awaits one `Task.WhenAll` over exactly TEN reads, so a single panel can hold every permit while the sidebar freshness dots, the alert poll and every other panel get nothing - and read eleven waits `ConnectionTimeoutSeconds` (default 5) for a slot and then throws a CONNECT error, misattributing a slow store to the network. Ten concurrent 30-day reads on that rig measured **24.4-64.1 s**, six of them past the silent default they used to inherit, so halving the ceiling returns permits sooner in exactly the state that produces the misdiagnosis. **This is not `StorageCommandDeadlines.McpReadSeconds` and 30 was re-derived, not copied**: the viewer's worst read is 4.4x slower than the MCP family's 685 ms worst, yet its permit is ten times scarcer and it has a fan-out that can take all ten, so a slower read against a scarcer permit lands BELOW 30 s rather than at it. The three command-plane sites take **5 s** `CommandPlaneSeconds`, pinned RELATIONALLY against the 45 s `DefaultCommandTimeout` poll loop they run inside rather than against a copied number - the poll is a single-row primary-key lookup at 3.9 ms cold, and 5 s keeps it an order of magnitude under the smaller of the two enclosing budgets. That regime also **narrows** a real overshoot rather than closing it: `PollCommandResultAsync` checks its budget only BETWEEN iterations, so with no deadline on the read a hung poll could exceed the 45 s a dialog promises by up to Npgsql's 30 s; it is now bounded at 5 s, but the loop still does not cancel an in-flight read, so the stated budget can still be exceeded by that much. The two connect-gate probes take **10 s** `ConnectGateSeconds` - `information_schema` and `has_table_privilege` only, so they do not grow with the store (all 85 schema sentinels measured 79 ms cold) - bounded above by the 60 s ceiling the viewer's own connection-timeout preference is clamped to, because these are the first two statements after connect, they run in `OnLoaded` before any timer starts and before the window is usable, and there is no splash to explain a wait. Their asymmetry is why they are separate: both CATCH their own failures - the schema probe fails OPEN and the read-only probe fails SAFE - so a blown deadline there raises nothing and instead silently MIS-CLASSIFIES the store, hiding every write affordance on a writable one. **What this does not do**: no user-reported viewer failure is closed by it - unlike [#2871] and [#2882] there is no observed production timeout on this surface, so the numbers come from measurement and from the permit arithmetic rather than from a failure distribution, and the change removes an inherited default nobody chose rather than fixing a known break. The timings are from the locally seeded V109 store, not from the production store itself, whose per-server density they are matched to. Pinned directory-scoped over both construction shapes plus the method-group scan, and RECURSIVELY where the [#2888] pin it is modelled on used `TopDirectoryOnly` (with `bin`/`obj` excluded by path segment, 190 source files from 234 enumerated), so a future viewer file is covered the day it appears even under a subdirectory - proven load-bearing by dropping an untimed command into the existing `Themes/` folder, which `TopDirectoryOnly` did not see; values are pinned as BANDS carrying their derivation rather than as equalities, its span walker terminates at `depth <= 0` (the depth-clamping bug that made two scanners miss real sites during [#2888]), its method-group scan strips comments and strings so the prose explaining the fix cannot fail the build, and it was proven red six ways, each failing a DIFFERENT assertion: removing one site's deadline, reverting the method-group fix, dropping an untimed command into a subdirectory, raising the interactive value to 30, dropping it to 3, and pushing the command-plane value past half its enclosing budget.
- **Every command in the Darling storage layer now carries an explicit deadline** ([#2874]) - seventy of the project's 124 command sites ran on Npgsql's undocumented 30 s inherited default, across five distinct budget regimes, and each regime now binds itself with its own deliberate constant rather than one blanket value: the MCP read surface (the `DarlingPg*Reader` family, trend reads and Query Store trend routing) at a new 30 s `StorageCommandDeadlines.McpReadSeconds` - bounded above every verified production read (97-685 ms across both stores, with a deliberately harder unfiltered superset at 35.2 s) and below the point where an unbudgeted interactive read holding a pooled connection should fail rather than hang; the migration session's six scaffolding statements (search_path, version table, version read, stamp, database search_path) at the existing 300 s `MigrationCommandTimeoutSeconds`, and its `pg_advisory_lock` acquire - a LOCK WAIT rather than a statement - at a new `MigrationLockWaitTimeoutSeconds` derived separately below; TimescaleDB setup/rearm at `SetupTimeoutSeconds` and its five catalog and rollup-coverage reads at `JobCatalogReadTimeoutSeconds` (the coverage probe's unbounded `min(collection_time)` measured 685 ms cold on the largest store - chunk exclusion answers it, unlike the #2795 shape); the startup self-test at its own per-layer 8 s budget; plan-dim maintenance at a hoisted 300 s (VACUUM FULL keeps its deliberate `CommandTimeout = 0`); and the slice repair's rail-lift at its session's 900 s. **The lock wait needed its own number rather than the statement bound's.** The advisory lock is taken ONCE and held while every pending rung applies in the same session, so a sibling waiting there waits for a whole multi-rung upgrade, not for one statement - at 300 s a several-rung upgrade could outlast it while every individual statement stayed inside its own limit, which is why this entry no longer claims to CLOSE that startup failure. `MigrationLockWaitTimeoutSeconds` is `5 x MigrationCommandTimeoutSeconds` (1500 s), floored on what the lock can legitimately be held for: exactly FOUR of the ladder's 108 rungs touch pre-existing data - V22's index across every chunk of the populated `index_object_stats`, V23's `migrate_data => true` conversion of `collection_log`, V39's two partial indexes over `query_stats` and `procedure_stats`, and V104's index on `pg_deadlocks` - while every other rung either creates the table it then indexes or is a metadata-only `ADD COLUMN`/`SET SCHEMA`/`DROP NOT NULL`/view refresh, and the whole 108-rung ladder measured **0.301 s end to end** on a fresh PostgreSQL 17.11 / TimescaleDB 2.29.2 store. Each of the four is one command already capped at the statement bound, so four multiples is the floor and the fifth is margin for the next data-moving rung (seeded on that store: V22 1.29 s over 907 MB/90 chunks, V23 9.37 s over a 608 MB heap, V39 1.44 s over 1.26 GB - against 2.72 GB, 0.69 GB and 24.5 GB in those same tables on the live 42-server store, so the real figures are larger and colder). **Nothing bounds the waiter from above at all**: `MigrateAsync` has exactly one production caller, `DarlingWorker.RunCollectionLoopAsync`, on the plain stopping token - no `CancelAfter` on the path, no configured `HostOptions.StartupTimeout` (so the framework default is infinite), no health check or readiness probe in the repo, no container `HEALTHCHECK`, no orchestrator manifest, and the installers' `WaitForStatus('Running')` returns before the first migration statement runs because the worker is a `BackgroundService`. So the value comes from the asymmetry instead: a waiter that dies mid-wait hits `LogCritical` and returns out of the collection loop, collecting NOTHING until an operator restarts it, while one that waits longer only delays its own first cycle, its MCP and web surfaces having started independently. Verified wired rather than merely declared - with the statement bound temporarily at 3 s and the lock wait at 12 s, a contended `MigrateAsync` against a held advisory lock failed at 12.02 s, and reverting only the acquire to the old constant moved that to 3.02 s, both surfacing as `Exception while reading from stream`. It NARROWS that startup failure rather than closing it: a fifth data-moving rung, or one needing longer than its own bound, still outlasts the wait. The directory-scoped pin sweeps every file in the project for both construction shapes, so a future file is covered the day it appears - and its span walker terminates at `depth <= 0`, pinned by a fixture reproducing the nested-`using` shape that made two depth-clamping scanners each miss a real site during this change.
- **The analysis pass's last two commands now carry an explicit deadline, and the pin can finally see the shape that hid them** ([#2874]) - [#2871] swept this assembly and declared "every command constructed anywhere in it sets a deadline". The claim was right and the scan was wrong: the sweep read **71 sites as covered while 73 existed**. `PgFactCollector.Waits.cs` and `PgDrillDownCollector.Blocking.cs` both handed `connection.CreateCommand` to `PgBlockingPairRowQuery.AppendDmvSnapshotRowsAsync` as a bare METHOD GROUP; that helper sets only `CommandText` on what the factory returns, so the deadline could only come from the hand-off, and a method group has no `(` after it for either census regex to match - the identical shape and identical blind spot as the viewer's 193rd site. Both now pass a factory lambda that stamps the deadline before returning. **Which half of this was actually unbounded, stated precisely.** On the scheduled path `context.CancellationToken` is armed by `DarlingWorker.s_analysisTimeout`'s 120s `CancelAfter`, so a stuck read there was already bounded and merely ate the whole pass instead of the collector's own budget - worth fixing, but not unbounded. `AnalysisContext.CancellationToken` however defaults to `CancellationToken.None`, and **every on-demand entry point omits it**: `DarlingWorker` is the only caller in the repo that passes a token, while `analyze_server` reaches BOTH reads (`CollectFactsAsync`, then `EnrichFindingsAsync`) and `get_analysis_facts`, `compare_analysis` and `audit_config` reach the fact collector's. On those four paths the read had no deadline AND no token and ran purely on Npgsql's undocumented 30s default, so this is what bounds them rather than a nicety on top of a token already doing the work. **Both constants were reused, not invented, and deliberately differ.** The fact collector's read takes its own `FactCommandTimeoutSeconds` (60s), which [#2810] derived from sitting inside that same 120s pass budget and whose doc comment already claims every command in that collector; the drill-down's takes `DrillDownCommandTimeoutSeconds` (30s), which [#2871] looked at and left alone. The drill-down value equals the default it replaces, so **this changes no timing there at all** - the point is that 30 is now chosen rather than arrived at, which is verbatim what that constant's own doc comment exists to say. That equality is also why a shared floor inside the helper was rejected rather than merely not chosen: the three callers sit in three different budget regimes (60s, 30s, the viewer's interactive read deadline), so there is no single value to apply, and the "apply a floor only if unset" variant is unsound here because Npgsql's default IS 30 and the drill-down's deliberate choice is ALSO 30 - indistinguishable from inside the helper, which is the exact ambiguity this issue exists to remove. Taking the deadline as a required parameter would have been compile-time enforced and was the strongest alternative; it lost on pin visibility, because the helper's `createCommand()` invocation matches neither census regex, so the one line the whole fix depended on would have become the only unguarded line in the pass. Keeping the stamp at the call sites puts the fix where the assembly's own pin can see it, and keeps the signature identical to Lite's DuckDB twin. **Pinned by widening the existing assembly-scoped sweep to both construction shapes and adding the method-group scan the viewer's pin uses.** Deliberately NOT repo-wide: the same shape appears four more times in `Lite` (`DuckDbFactCollector.Waits.cs`, `DrillDownCollector.Blocking.cs`, two in `LocalDataService.Blocking.cs`) but those build `DuckDBCommand`, which has no 30s default to inherit and may not honour `CommandTimeout` at all, so sweeping them would assert a bound that cannot be shown to bind anything - a pin that cannot fail. The sweep's floor moved from 71 to **73** in the same edit, and for a reason worth naming: widening the regex made two sites VISIBLE that had always existed, so the true count moved without any command being added - a floor left at the old number would have let both vanish again silently, which is exactly what it exists to catch. A floor therefore has to be re-measured whenever the scan's REACH changes, not only when code is added. Proven red two ways, measured rather than asserted. Reverting both call sites fails the method-group scan (naming `PgDrillDownCollector.Blocking.cs:161` and `PgFactCollector.Waits.cs:231`) and the bumped floor (71 found, 73 expected) - while the per-site half of that same test still reports **zero missing**, because a method group remains structurally invisible to it, which is the whole reason the scan had to be added rather than the regex merely widened. Keeping the lambda while deleting its deadline fails ONLY the per-site half, at `PgFactCollector.Waits.cs:245` - a site the pre-#2874 narrow regex would also have missed, measured: it does not match `connection.CreateCommand()`, so the widening is independently load-bearing. The method-group scan's character class carries `;` as well as `,` and `)`, which the viewer's otherwise identical regex does not: the argument forms catch an inlined hand-off, the only form in the repo today, but a hand-off through a local (`Func<NpgsqlCommand> factory = connection.CreateCommand;`) ends in a semicolon and would slip past both those AND the ctor regex, a method group having no `(` either way. One character, measured to add no false positive in either project - declining to close a KNOWN missed shape in the very regex that exists because a census missed a shape would be this issue's own mistake in miniature. The comment/string stripper is carried from the sibling pin and is **defensive rather than load-bearing here** - three comments in this assembly name `connection.CreateCommand` in running text, but all three are followed by a backtick or `</c>` instead of a comma, so the unstripped scan measures zero false hits today; one comma is the whole difference, and a theory fixture pins the behaviour so it cannot be dropped as dead weight. **What this does not do**: no user-reported failure is closed by it. Unlike [#2871]'s io_latency baseline and [#2810]'s plan-regression read, **neither of these two reads has an observed production timeout**, and their cost was not measured against the production store for this change - the fix removes an inherited default nobody chose and closes a census gap, and it should not be read as fixing a known break. No schema change and no version bump.
- **The retention live tests now carry their evidence into the failure message** ([#2818]) - `EnsureRetentionPoliciesAsync`'s per-relation catch logs a warning and moves on, which is right for the service (one bad relation must not abort the other sixteen) - but both live tests passed `logger: null`, so when the nightly failed once with "expected 17, got 0" every one of the seventeen discarded warnings was the diagnosis and the bare count was all that survived. It was written off as a flake because it could not be anything else. The tests now pass the `CapturingTestLogger` that [#1564] added to the purge E2Es for exactly this shape (the issue assumed no such logger existed; it has since #2128) and fold `Joined` into every count assertion - including the `EnsureContinuousAggregatesAsync` call, since a swallowed CAGG failure is a plausible upstream cause of the same all-relations-throw symptom. Proven against a live TimescaleDB 2.29 store by running the SHIPPED methods both ways on a store with no hypertables: the old shape reports `applied=0` and nothing else, the new shape carries one warning per relation naming the actual Postgres error (`TS001: "query_stats" is not a hypertable or a continuous aggregate`), and the healthy path still applies and re-applies every policy with zero warnings captured. The shared logger's empty-case placeholder also stops claiming to be about purges. The next occurrence names its cause in the CI log on the first failure instead of costing a round.
- **A healthy server mid-sweep is no longer painted with the red Offline overlay** ([#2794]) - the display's freshness classifier banded a server `Offline - no recent collection` past a bare 15-minute constant, while the alert engine had deliberately decided collection is not "stopped" until 30 (`DarlingSelfAlertEvaluator.StaleWindow`, configurable as `collectionStaleMinutes`) - one condition, two definitions, and only the alert side configurable. The tighter one false-alarmed by design: the sweep skips relaunch while a server's collection body is still running, so one long `query_store` cycle holds the whole body and NOTHING writes to `collection_log` for the duration - a healthy server legitimately goes 12-19 minutes quiet with zero failures anywhere, and five production shards wore the red overlay at a snapshot while actively mid-cycle, which sent an investigation chasing phantom dark servers. **Measured before changing anything, per the issue's own instruction**: with [#2792] deployed, the worst legitimate inter-collection gap across the whole fleet in 24h is 12m12s and ZERO real servers cross 15 minutes (the sole >15m "server" is `server_id = 0`, the service's own bookkeeping rows, whose gaps end exactly at the deploy restarts) - but issue-day load produced 235 crossings on every server in 12 hours with legitimate stretch reaching 19m18s, so the margin between routine operation and the threshold was load-dependent and paper-thin, while genuinely dark servers run HOURS. `OfflineThreshold` now derives from a shared `CollectionStoppedMinutesDefault = 30` alongside every other spelling of the same claim - the evaluator's `StaleWindow`, `AlertsConfig.CollectionStaleMinutes`'s default, Lite's `AppAlertEngineSettings`, Lite's `AgentStatusRow.StaleWindow` (whose comment already said it "mirrors" the service's window, at a numerically-equal but independently-editable copy, the exact [#1562] drift shape), and `DarlingWorker`'s Postgres long-running-query recency bound, whose own doc comment derives it from the fleet staleness convention and which would otherwise have re-created the alert-blindness it warns about. A server between stretched sweeps now bands `Stale` - amber, "collection has lagged" - which is the honest reading; every surface follows because all four banding reads resolve through the one `ClassifyFreshness` ([#2473]), and `CollectionStoppedThresholdAgreementTests` pins the definitions together BY VALUE on both apps, proven red against the pre-change tree (15m != 30m, and the measured 19m18s stretch banding Offline). The Agent-header honesty suite's own window assertion needed the opposite treatment: with `AgentStatusRow.StaleWindow` now DERIVED, its bare `FromMinutes(30)` no longer said anything about the two surfaces agreeing, and a deliberate retune of the shared constant would have turned it red under a name (`StaleWindow_MatchesTheHeadlessServicesRefusalWindow`) that blames cross-surface drift for a definition that in fact still agreed. Symbolising the literal was not the fix either - that assertion becomes `FromMinutes(CONST) == FromMinutes(CONST)`, a tautology that can never fail - and neither was deleting it, because the suite does depend on the window's magnitude: its 1-minute and 45-minute fixture ages have to STRADDLE the window or the staleness cases quietly stop exercising staleness. That straddle is now the assertion (`TheStalenessFixtures_StraddleTheWindowTheyAreMeantToTest`), against the real symbol and against named fixture constants rather than retyped copies, so a widening past 45 minutes fails saying which fixture went out of date instead of implying the surfaces split.
- **Abandon-path forensics no longer record a session id of 0** ([#2884]) - every `ABANDONED` row V109 wrote carried `target_session_id = 0` - a value no real session has - while successful runs recorded real ids, and the abandon path is the ONE place the id is load-bearing: it exists to join a stalled run to `waiting_tasks` / `dmv_blocking_snapshot` / `query_snapshots`. Two defects compounded. The capture sat BEFORE `ExecuteReaderAsync`, and `SqlConnection.ServerProcessId` is not reliably populated until the connection has round-tripped a command, so a connection that had not yet completed that exchange reported the provider's not-populated 0. And the write side guarded every OTHER forensic figure - `>= 0` on the row, byte and last-read counts, rejecting the in-memory -1 sentinel - but passed the session id through bare, so the 0 landed in the column as though it were data. The capture now happens AFTER the open round trip: a drain-stall abandon (open completes in ~100-200ms; the [#2673] budget fires minutes into the read) therefore records its REAL id, which is exactly the case the column was built for, while an abandon that fires INSIDE `ExecuteReaderAsync` leaves it NULL - the declared NOT-RECORDED convention, and the honest answer for a connection that never finished its first exchange. `TryReadTargetSessionId` normalizes non-positive values to null at the source, and the writer grows the same-shaped guard as its sibling columns (`spid > 0`) - belt beside braces, because `DrainForensics` is a public record any caller can construct with a 0 the capture path would never produce. Pinned three ways, each proven red against the unfixed source: the capture must sit AFTER the `ExecuteReaderAsync` it depends on (a source-order pin, because the property is unmockable without a real `SqlConnection` and a refactor hoisting the capture back above the open reintroduces the bug while compiling clean), the helper's normalization, and the writer's guard.
- **Every read in the alert evaluation pass now carries an explicit deadline** ([#2874]) - all forty-five commands across the six alert-pass types set no `CommandTimeout`, so every one inherited Npgsql's undocumented 30s default. On 2026-09-04 the forced-plan read failed five times on the production store, each surfacing as "Exception while reading from stream" - which is how Npgsql renders its OWN deadline, the misdiagnosis [#2826] exists to prevent. Unlike [#2810] and [#2871], which sit under `DarlingWorker.s_analysisTimeout`'s 120s `CancelAfter`, **this pass has no enclosing budget at all**: `EvaluateAlertsAsync` is called with the plain stopping token, so the per-command deadline IS the pass budget multiplied by the number of sequential reads - 45 x 30s of worst-case exposure while the body holds one of only four fleet sweep permits and that server's relaunch is skipped throughout. The value is therefore the first in this family set BELOW what it inherited rather than above: 10s, bounded under by the measured worst case (the shipped queries timed on the production store's three busiest servers put the whole pass's cost in one read - the forced-plan check at 1,744.9ms cold over ~6.0GB, every other read under 3ms) and bounded over by the 30s `s_alertSweepInterval` the pass runs on, so one stalled read still lets it finish inside the interval that restarts it. The asymmetry is what justifies erring short: an exceeded deadline skips one alert check and logs it, retrying 30s later, while a long-running read starves fleet-wide collection. Every observed failure was killed AT the 30s ceiling, so the record is right-censored - 10s is chosen from the measured cost and the cadence, not fitted to a failure distribution the data cannot show. Pinned structurally over both construction shapes: [#2874]'s census counted only `new NpgsqlCommand(`, missing `NpgsqlDataSource.CreateCommand(sql)`, which inherits the same default and accounts for four of this group's own sites - the repo-wide recount is recorded on the issue.
- **A held Ctrl+V during a busy clipboard no longer spawns several 'Pasted Plan' tabs from one keypress** ([#2870]) - [#2837] moved the clipboard can't-open retry off a synchronous `Thread.Sleep` onto an awaited `Task.Delay`, which keeps the UI pump responsive but also yields the thread for the ~175 ms retry window. The old `Thread.Sleep` had incidentally serialized input, so a HELD Ctrl+V (OS key-repeat) could not dispatch a second paste until the first finished; with the async retry, on a transiently busy clipboard several paste KeyDown handlers can sit in their retry loops at once, and when the clipboard frees each succeeds and loads its own tab. Each paste surface now carries a `_pasteInProgress` re-entrancy flag: set synchronously before the awaited read and cleared in a `finally` that runs only after the load finishes, so it spans the clipboard read AND the off-thread plan parse (the loaders return `Task` and the paste handlers `await` them, not fire-and-forget) and a repeat paste arriving during either window is dropped (its key stays claimed via `e.Handled`). The same flag guards both the Ctrl+V handler and the Paste XML button on each surface, across all three front ends (Lite, the Darling viewer, and the deprecated Dashboard). A rare, benign burst that the [#2837] async change had newly made possible; source-pinned so the guard can't be dropped without failing the build.
- **Retention held by the rollup-coverage gate is now visible, instead of reading as a healthy job** ([#2813]) - the #1680/#1877 gate PAUSES a retention policy so it cannot drop history a rollup has never materialized, which is correct and is not being weakened here. But a paused job is not a failing one: it reports `total_failures = 0`, `last_run_status = Success` and a plausible last-run duration, and `StoreSelfMetrics` never recorded `j.scheduled`, so every stored metric read clean. On the production store five `query_store_stats` policies sat held for **16 days** while the raw tier grew to 19 chunks and 65 GB - 4.5x its own 4-day horizon, and the store's largest object - and the only signal was one WARNING per service start ([#2809]). `get_store_metrics` now reports every held policy LIVE from the catalog, with the tier's actual span and how many times its configured `drop_after` it is really holding, and a `Retention Held` self-alert fires on the same hourly sweep as its [#2136] sibling. Judged on the CONJUNCTION of held AND past-horizon, never on the paused flag alone: `EnsureRetentionPoliciesAsync` deliberately creates every policy paused, so flag-only would fire on every fresh store at every start, and retention drops whole CHUNKS, so a 4-day policy with 1-day chunks legitimately sits at 1.25x while working perfectly. The ratio is deliberately not a hold duration - nothing records when a policy was paused - and measuring the consequence instead is what makes one threshold serve a 4-day raw tier and a 35-day baseline tier alike. Warning at 2.0x (clear of the 1.25x chunk floor, and ~8 days on the raw tier rather than the 16 it actually took), Critical at 4.0x, so the motivating incident reads Critical. The check never ACTS: arming a held policy drops the only copy of the history it holds, which is exactly what the gate prevents, so the release stays the `--backfill-rollups` operator decision and this makes the need for one visible rather than silent. Verified against a genuinely held state on TimescaleDB 2.28.1 - 19 chunks under a 4-day policy, measured 4.52x - by running the shipped reader itself, with the span normalization proven session-independent under UTC, UTC+14 and UTC-7. Persisting `scheduled` into the metrics series needs a migration rung and is tracked separately.
- **Every command in the analysis pass now carries an explicit deadline, not just the fact collector's** ([#2871]) - [#2810] gave `PgFactCollector`'s thirty-one commands a deliberate `CommandTimeout` and left the other THIRTY-TWO in the same 120s pass inheriting Npgsql's undocumented 30s default, and one of them was failing in production: on the dogfood box the `io_latency` baseline timed out nineteen times over two days and every sample landed between **30.1s and 31.4s**. A fixed wall, not a variable-duration fault and not the 15s connection timeout - the shipped query (dumped from the built assembly, not retyped) runs in **~1.6s** against the live store on the three busiest servers, so it crosses the ceiling only when the store stalls, which is why it read as intermittent. [#2820] had already made that query 5.6x faster (23.7s to 4.2s) and [#2826] had made the failure visible; neither touched what it was failing against, exactly as [#2827] and [#2826] had not touched [#2810]'s. The cost when it fires is larger than one pass: `GetOrComputeBaselinesAsync` assigns `_cache[cacheKey]` unconditionally, so a null result is CACHED and a single stall silences that (server, metric) pair's anomaly detection for the full 1-hour `CacheTtl`. **60s, bounded on both sides.** Below: the failure record is RIGHT-CENSORED - every run was killed at the ceiling, so nothing says whether it wanted 35s or 300s, and the value is therefore chosen as twice what it had rather than as a fitted number the data cannot support. Above - the bound that actually binds: `DarlingWorker.s_analysisTimeout` gives the pass 120s and the fact collector's thirty-one reads, up to eleven baseline computations per server, the anomaly detector and the drill-down all share it, so a per-command deadline near that figure would let ONE stalled command consume the pass and cost the server every other fact - strictly worse than the failure being fixed, which loses one metric. It matches `PgFactCollector.FactCommandTimeoutSeconds` deliberately, because two numbers in one budget would have to be reasoned about together every time either moved. Swept by SHAPE across the whole assembly rather than the one reported site (the [#2344] and `PgStatementText` mistake, twice paid for): `PgAnomalyDetector` 12, `PgFindingStore` 8, `PgDrillDownCollector` 10, `PgBaselineProvider` 1, `DarlingAnalysisService` 1. The drill-down's ten take that collector's OWN existing `DrillDownCommandTimeoutSeconds = 30` rather than a new value, so an author's deliberate choice on its other eight sites is not silently overridden. Pinned structurally over the pass - every command constructed in the assembly sets a deadline, and the value stays inside the justified band - and proven red three ways, each failing a DIFFERENT assertion: reverting the reported site, restoring 30s, and raising to the full 120s budget. Repo-wide this leaves 133 untimed `NpgsqlCommand` sites outside the analysis pass, which have different budgets and are filed separately rather than swept in here.
- **The slice-repair survey no longer holds one read lock across its entire multi-phase read, and can now actually be cancelled** ([#2761]) - `RemoteCollectorService` takes the DB WRITE lock for every collection write and `ReaderWriterLockSlim` blocks a writer while any reader holds the lock, so the duration of a single read-lock hold IS the duration of a fleet-wide collection stall. `SurveyAsync` took one lock at the top and kept it across the hot-table `GROUP BY` **and** a `read_parquet` of every monthly archive file - on a store like the one [#2748] reported (31,426 split intervals plus multiple archives) that is minutes in which no new data arrives anywhere, which from outside is indistinguishable from the app having failed to start. The survey now scopes its lock per phase, acquiring and releasing around the hot table and again around each archive file, so a waiting writer gets through at every boundary; the lock and the connection are taken and released TOGETHER, because a connection outliving its lock is a connection open across a maintenance operation that reorganizes the file, which is where "Reached the end of the file" comes from. That is the idiom `RewriteArchiveFileAsync` already used for exactly this reason. **The second half was a call site, not the service.** [#2465] had already threaded a token through every entry point here, but `MainWindow` fired the repair as `Task.Run(() => sliceRepair.RepairOnStartupAsync())` with no token - and `DuckDbInitializer.AcquireReadLock` BRANCHES on that, taking the uninterruptible `EnterReadLock()` path for a token that cannot be cancelled and the pollable, abandonable one otherwise. So every carefully-forwarded token stopped at the door and nothing could interrupt a stuck survey: not shutdown, not the operator closing the window. It now receives the app-lifetime `_backgroundCts` token, whose construction moved a few lines earlier so the call can have it - still one source, still cancelled once in `MainWindow_Closing`. **A fixed timeout was considered and rejected**: `MaxStartupAttempts` is 2, and [#2770] records an attempt BEFORE the repair runs, so two slow-but-healthy launches under a timeout would burn the cap and abandon the collapse permanently - converting "slow" into "never". Shutdown is the honest bound. Handing over a real token also made `OperationCanceledException` reachable for the first time, and the repair is started with a bare un-awaited `Task.Run`, so an uncaught one would have become an unobserved task exception - a repair that stopped with nothing anywhere saying so, the silent-failure shape this repo keeps re-finding. It is now caught and logged at Information, because being abandoned on shutdown is this service working as designed: the marker is withheld and the next launch redoes the work. [#2770]'s give-up-after-N-attempts behaviour and its own short, deliberately uncancelable attempt-marker write are untouched - that write's whole value is being durable before the next thing goes wrong. Pinned structurally rather than by timing, because a race against how long a phase happens to take on a CI machine gets muted rather than fixed; the pin was proven red against `origin/dev`, which fails three of its four assertions.
- **The one-time Query Store slice repair no longer runs out of memory on a large store** ([#2771]) - [#2770] stopped a failed repair from BRICKING startup but deliberately left its memory cost alone, so a store big enough to hit this still never completed the [#1912] collapse; it just failed safely instead of killing the process. The staging step built `CREATE OR REPLACE TEMP TABLE ... GROUP BY <9 key cols>` over the FULL projection, which materializes every column of every collapsed group **including `query_plan_text`** - full showplan XML. At the reporting store's 31,426 split intervals that does not fit in Lite's 1 GB `memory_limit`: `Out of Memory Error: failed to pin block of size 256.0 KiB (953.4 MiB/953.6 MiB used)`, reproduced deterministically by `tools/SliceRepairRepro` against the real linked collapse expressions. Platform-dependent surface, and the reason the catch never ran: on macOS this raises a catchable `DuckDBException`, on Windows x64 the same pressure aborts the process with a `0xc0000409` fast-fail inside `duckdb.DLL`. **The attributes never needed to be in the aggregate at all.** `ANY_VALUE` was picking an arbitrary row's copy of a value that every slice of one interval already carries identically - so the staging now carries the key, the surviving row's `rowid`, and the recombined MEASUREMENTS only, and the wide payload stays on the row that survives, which is the same answer reached without moving the bytes. The collapse became DELETE-the-others then UPDATE-the-survivor instead of DELETE-all then INSERT. **The order is load-bearing**: DuckDB implements UPDATE as delete+insert so an updated row's `rowid` CHANGES, and updating first would leave the survivor carrying a rowid the delete predicate no longer recognises - deleting the row it had just repaired. After the delete each group holds exactly one row, so the update matches on the key alone and never trusts a rowid across a mutation. Measured with the same harness: staging goes from OOM to **37-132 ms** at 31,426 groups, and the repair still completes at **100,000 groups / ~12.8 GB of plan text - 3.2x the reporting store** - inside the same 1 GB budget, with every surviving row's plan text intact. Proven byte-identical to the original where BOTH strategies complete (2,000 groups): same final row count, same `SUM(execution_count)`, same full-row hash `18142744224640971181719`. The two approaches [#2770] already measured and rejected are untouched and stay rejected - bucketing on `hash(key) % N` (a single bucket still exceeded the limit) and staging without the wide text columns (still OOMed); neither removed the aggregate, which was the actual cost. `IsMeasurement` is DERIVED from `CombineExpression` rather than restated as a second list, so a rule added there is picked up here for free - a parallel list would drift, and on this path drift means a measurement silently left uncombined, which is the [#1912] defect reintroduced by its own fix. Pinned non-vacuously in both directions and both proven red before shipping: classify everything as a measurement and the no-attribute-in-staging assertions fail, classify nothing and the measurements-still-recombined assertions fail. The archive rewrite path is unchanged - it already raises its own copy memory limit.
- **The Plan Viewer's inner tab handler is now subscribed once, not re-subscribed on every reopen** ([#2828]) - the shared `StandalonePlanViewerController` wired the inner plan `TabControl`'s `SelectionChanged` handler inside `EnsureInitialized`, and `Reset()` (Plan Viewer close) re-arms that path by clearing the `_initialized` guard. The controller instance and its `TabControl` both persist across open/close cycles, so each reopen added another handler to the same control - N cycles leaked N handlers. Not a crash and not a double-add: the #2825 `_addTabInsertDeferred` one-shot latch collapses the duplicate invocations to a single add, so this was a delegate/CPU leak rather than a correctness bug, which is why it was filed separately. The subscription now lives in the constructor, wired exactly once for the controller's lifetime; the first `EnsureInitialized`'s `Items.Add` still fires it once while it is live, which defers-then-no-ops through the same latch. The deprecated Dashboard is unaffected - it builds a fresh `TabControl` per open and nulls it on close, so it never accumulated.
- **The plan-regression analysis read had no command deadline, so it inherited one nobody chose** ([#2810]) - every one of `PgFactCollector`'s thirty-one commands ran with no `CommandTimeout`, taking Npgsql's undocumented 30s default. Nobody picked 30s; it was simply what happened, and on the largest servers `PlanRegressionSql` needed more. Two earlier changes aimed elsewhere and neither could have fixed it: [#2827] made the query 2.4x faster and [#2826] made the failure visible, but the binding constraint was a ceiling neither touched. Measured on the production store AFTER retention was restored ([#2809]) and with [#2827] in place, the read still crosses 30s on a COLD run - 34.7s and 32.4s on the two largest servers, against 10.6-21.2s for those same servers warm, so it fails on the cold/large combination only. That is why it presented as intermittent rather than broken, and why the store's own log showed cancellations while the query looked fast whenever anyone measured it by hand - a warm second run is the one you get when you go looking. **60s, and the upper bound is the one that matters.** Below, it must clear the 34.7s cold worst case or the defect reproduces. Above, `DarlingWorker.s_analysisTimeout` gives the WHOLE analysis pass 120s and thirty collect methods share it, so a per-command deadline near that figure would let one stalled read consume the pass and cost the server its other twenty-nine facts - converting "one fact is missing" into "every fact is missing", strictly worse than the failure being fixed. 60s clears the measured worst case by 1.7x and still leaves half the pass for everything else. Applied to all thirty-one commands rather than the one that exposed it: they share the same pass budget and had the same missing value, and pinning one site while leaving thirty identical ones is the enumerated-list mistake this repo has paid for twice ([#2344], `PgStatementText`). A generous ceiling is inert on a query that returns in milliseconds. Pinned structurally - every command constructed in the collector must set a deadline, and the value must sit in the band where both bounds hold - and proven red three ways: reverting one site, restoring the 30s default, and raising it to the full 120s pass each fail a different assertion. No query, schedule or retention change.
- **Managed-store Postgres sizing now re-derives when the HOST changes, not only when the formula does** ([#2845]) - every conf heal block from v1 to v7 is keyed on "is this marker absent?", a question answered once in a cluster's life, so nothing re-derived when the machine underneath it was replaced. All three monitoring boxes were resized 16 GB -> 31.5 GB and kept `effective_cache_size` at 11.86 GB - 75% of the RAM they no longer had - which biases the planner toward sequential scans on a store serving on the order of 670k small index lookups a day. The new v8 block is keyed on a FINGERPRINT of the derivation inputs (RAM and hypertable count) rather than a version, so the two triggers compose: a version marker still heals "we changed our mind about the formula", and this heals "the machine changed". Precedence cannot be ambiguous even though both write the same file, because v8 calls the SAME helpers the version blocks do (`DeriveMemorySettings`, and `DeriveWorkerSettings` extracted here so v2 and v8 cannot drift) - it can only ever differ by being fresher, never by disagreeing. It compares the LAST fingerprint in the conf, not any: a host resized 16 -> 32 -> 16 GB has both, and a `Contains` test would find the stale first one and skip, leaving the 32 GB block still winning by last-occurrence-wins on a box that no longer has 32 GB - the check has to ask the question postgresql.conf itself answers. **The omissions are the load-bearing part.** `shared_buffers` is excluded STRUCTURALLY rather than by trusting its cap to hold: the 1 GB cap is the Windows error-487 mitigation ([#1559], pgsql-bugs BUG #14050 / #18954) and that condition is live on this fleet (measured 4-205 occurrences/day across the three boxes, zero `could not fork` - the retry path holding is exactly the margin a larger segment would spend), so if the cap is ever raised deliberately that is a reviewed formula change, not something a resize propagates on its own. `work_mem` is excluded because the formula would take it 31 -> 63 MB and the only measurements above 31 MB on this store's heaviest read are worse (PlanRegressionSql: default 26,565ms, 31 MB 25,617ms, 512 MB 59,323ms) - and more fundamentally because it is the wrong KIND of setting for a hardware block, a per-sort per-connection ceiling that follows from the query mix rather than from the machine. `max_parallel_workers` is untouched: this class has never set it, so it sits at the PostgreSQL default of 8 regardless of cores, and deriving it is a behaviour change that multiplies the same memory story (each parallel worker gets its own `work_mem`), wanting its own evidence. Re-stating the two worker settings is also what finally makes the v2 block's "never goes stale as collectors are added" claim true - that was true of the formula and false of its marker-keyed application.
- **Four enumerated-path phase stamps were skipped whenever the phase they timed threw** ([#2854]) - `PerItemPlanFetchMs`, `PerItemTextFetchMs`, `PerItemOpenMs` and `PerItemWatermarkMs` were assigned on the line after their `await`, so a phase that threw skipped its own stamp and reported zero. The two fetch stamps are the parents of the sub-split fixed in #2816, and they are the live half: their fetch methods catch their own exceptions and return normally, so the item still completes and still logs, and a failed fetch printed `plan_fetch:0ms` above non-zero `probe:`/`target:`/`write:` children while `PlanFetchOtherMs` clamped the impossible negative residual to zero - a line that reads as precise while being arithmetically impossible. The open and watermark stamps were bare for the same reason but are latent, not live: a throw there propagates out of the driver's per-item `try`, so the item is skipped and prints no split line at all. All four now stamp from `finally` regardless, since the field's honesty should not depend on a caller two files away continuing to discard the item.
