The log site gates on a new `PerItemPhasesMeasured` flag rather than `PerItemOpenMs > 0`, which suppressed the whole split for a genuinely instant open. The #2851 IL reachability pin is now DERIVED from `CollectorContext` rather than naming two setters, so it covers all twelve stamps on both paths and any stamp added later; naming them is why it did not catch these four.
- **`Collector Cost Regression` compared the day's TOTAL cost, so a cadence recovery read as a regression** ([#2846]) - total daily cost is `runs x cost-per-run`, and the predicate compared totals, so it could not tell "each run got more expensive" from "the same work ran more often". When use1's collection cadence recovered - it had been starved by `query_store` occupying the shared collection body - run counts rose sharply and daily totals rose with them while cost PER RUN fell, and the alert reported the improvement as a regression: 3,259 firings across 612 (server, collector) pairs in one day, 53% of them on collectors whose per-run cost had gone DOWN. Running the shipped predicate against that store, the total-cost rule matched 610 pairs and a per-run rule matched 14. The comparison is now cost per run, against a run-weighted baseline (`sum(sql_ms) / sum(runs)` over the prior days, not an average of daily averages, so a low-run day cannot dominate the baseline it contributes to). That fixes a second defect for free: `latest_day` is a PARTIAL day compared against FULL days, so a total grows monotonically until midnight and the tripping population could only grow within a day - which is exactly the observed shape, 510 -> 1,508 -> 1,894 -> 2,317 across four hourly samples with no decay possible. A per-run average is scale-free, so a half-finished day is directly comparable to a whole one. The total-cost floor stays on the daily total deliberately - it is what stops a 3 ms/run collector alerting on a doubling to 6 ms - and there is deliberately NO minimum run count on the latest day, because `index_object_stats` legitimately runs once per server per day and a min-runs guard would blind the alert to every daily-cadence collector. `metric_name` stays `Collector Cost Regression` (a webhook automation key the [#2768] triage map reads); the reported values change unit from ms/day to ms/run because what is measured changed, with the daily total and run count kept in the detail text. Pinned against live Postgres by an adversarial fixture whose two collectors have IDENTICAL latest-day totals and differ only in run count, so reverting the predicate fails on the cadence-recovery case specifically.

- **PR CI now runs `Lite.Tests` when only Darling changes.** `build.yml`'s `Run Lite tests`
  step gates on the `lite` path filter, which named `Darling/PerformanceMonitor.Darling.Service`
  but not `Darling/Darling.Tests` or the rest of `Darling.Viewer` - so the cross-app parity guards
  that read the other app's SOURCE were the one class of test a Darling-only PR could not run.
  #2830 went six-green, merged, and the nightly on its merge commit failed what its own CI had
  skipped. The `darling` filter had the mirror gap, naming `Lite/**/*.xaml` while `Darling.Tests`
  also reads `Lite/Analysis`, `Lite/Mcp` and `Lite/Services` `.cs`. All three covered, plus a
  derived guard that scans both test projects for the other app's paths and fails when a filter
  cannot reach one - an enumerated list is what stopped covering the next file added, four times
  this week. ([#2839])
- **Plan Viewer clipboard retry no longer blocks the UI thread while the clipboard is busy** ([#2837]) - the [#2833] guard retried a can't-open clipboard with a synchronous `Thread.Sleep` between attempts (8 tries, ~25 ms apart), and every paste path runs on the UI thread, so the rare `CLIPBRD_E_CANT_OPEN` failure path froze the WPF message pump for up to ~175 ms before the graceful notice. `ClipboardText` gains an async sibling `TryReadAsync` whose backoff awaits `Task.Delay` instead, keeping the pump responsive; both variants share one guarded single-read helper so the retry bookkeeping is written once. All seven paste call sites now route through it - the Ctrl+V handlers were already (or became) `async void`, and the two Paste XML buttons became `async` lambdas. Each Ctrl+V handler now claims the gesture (`e.Handled = true`) synchronously before the async read, since the retry path awaits and would otherwise finish routing unsuppressed. A first-attempt read still completes synchronously (awaiting an already-finished task does not yield), so the successful-read behavior stays byte-identical; only the rare retry wait moved off the thread. The synchronous `TryRead` stays for any non-async caller.
- **Plan Viewer paste no longer crashes the app when the clipboard can't be opened** ([#2833]) - every paste path called `Clipboard.GetText()` bare, so a transient `COMException` (`CLIPBRD_E_CANT_OPEN`, 0x800401D0) - Windows serializes clipboard access, and a clipboard manager, Office, a browser, or an RDP/locked-desktop session momentarily holding it is routine - propagated as an unhandled domain exception and took the whole process down (surfaced in the Darling viewer). All seven call sites across the shared controller, the shared `PlanViewerControl`, Lite, and the deprecated Dashboard now route through one guarded `PerformanceMonitor.Ui.ClipboardText.TryRead` helper, which wraps the read in a short bounded retry (the condition is transient) and returns false on persistent failure: the button paths show a graceful notice and the Ctrl+V paths no-op, exactly as each already handled an empty clipboard. Behavior on a successful read is byte-identical. Source-pin tests in both plan-viewer capability pins (and the Lite ServerTab pin) now fail the build if a bare `Clipboard.GetText(` reappears in any paste path.
- **The plan-regression query, ~90% of all store statement cancellations, is no longer sorted globally** ([#2827]) - `PgFactCollector.PlanRegressionSql`'s interval-grain dedup used `ROW_NUMBER() ... WHERE rn = 1`, which forces one sort of the server's whole Query Store slice on all eight keys (~548MB spill). Expressed as `GROUP BY` + ordered `array_agg(...)[1]` the planner hash-aggregates at the interval grain and sorts only within each small group: 23.4s to 9.6s for the dedup in isolation on the busiest server, byte-identical results verified by md5 across six servers including a zero-row one. `DISTINCT ON` (49.8s) and a larger `work_mem` (512MB took the query from 25.6s to 59.3s) were both measured and are both worse.
- **A fact collector that could not run is no longer indistinguishable from one that found nothing** ([#2826]) - all 54 swallow-catches across `PgFactCollector.*` and Lite's `DuckDbFactCollector.*` had EMPTY bodies, so a `PlanRegressionSql` cancelled by its inherited 30 s Npgsql deadline produced output byte-identical to the `if (offenderCount == 0) return;` four lines above it - 325 times in one day on the dogfood box, with plan-regression detection in effect off and nothing downstream able to tell. Each catch now reports WHY it degraded to 'no facts': a command timeout at WARNING (classified structurally by `PgBaselineProvider.IsCommandTimeout`, never by message - Npgsql renders its own deadline as "Exception while reading from stream", which read literally says the network broke), the schema not having what the query asked for at DEBUG since that is the documented expected case - BOTH 42P01 undefined_table and 42703 undefined_column, because the site whose comment reads "Columns may not exist yet (pre-migration)" selects columns a later migration rung added, so a rolling deploy raises the COLUMN shape there and classifying only the table shape would have logged an ERROR every pass for the whole migration window - and anything else at ERROR. The site names itself via `[CallerMemberName]`, so a renamed or copy-pasted collect method cannot report under the wrong name. Swept by SHAPE rather than by the old `"Table may not exist or have no data"` comment: the comment matched 50 sites, the shape matched 54, and the four it missed were the same defect wearing different wording. Both collectors are pinned from one test because they are a method-for-method port and a blind spot fixed on one side would silently re-open on the other - Lite gets a single ERROR arm, because neither of Darling's other two is REACHABLE there: its only deadline is the pass budget itself, which `AcquireReadLock` raises as `OperationCanceledException` and `AnalysisAbandon.IsExpected` then filters upstream, and DuckDB distinguishes a missing relation only by message text.
- **FinOps "View Stored Plan" no longer crashes the whole Darling Viewer** ([#2825]) - right-clicking a High Impact or Expensive Queries row and choosing "View Stored Plan" on a not-yet-opened Plan Viewer took the entire app down with an unhandled InvalidOperationException, "Cannot call StartAt when content generation is in progress". Opening the Plan Viewer tab for the first time realizes its inner tab strip, and WPF re-raises that strip's SelectionChanged for the auto-selected "+" sentinel from INSIDE the container generator - the shared StandalonePlanViewerController answered by inserting a fresh sub-tab right then, and mutating a TabControl's Items mid-generation throws. The "+"-tab auto-add now defers off the generation pass while only the sentinel is present (the transient that exists solely during first-time setup) and stays synchronous for a genuine user click, so the opener's own sub-tab is the one that renders: no duplicate tab, no reordering, no crash. The same controller fix covers Lite, and the deprecated Dashboard's separate copy of the plan-viewer code got the identical guard.
- **The `query_store` fetch split lines now report the probe's INPUT size, not just the ids it attempted** ([#2823]) - `probe:` is the store half of `plan_fetch`/`text_fetch`, and it scales with the number of references handed to the touch/probe round trip. The line beside it logged `PerItemPlanIdsAttempted`, which is incremented only inside the TARGET chunk loop and therefore counts what came back MISSING - a different quantity by orders of magnitude on a healthy database. A pass that probes 847 references and owes nothing logged `0 ids` next to 517ms of real store work. Measured on production the probe costs ~0.61ms per reference, dead linear (78 references 49ms, 272 166ms, 847 517ms), so dividing probe time by the attempted-id count produced a number with no physical meaning - and it was divided that way twice: [#2819] read a 673ms cost on `ids=0` passes as pure `OpenConnectionAsync` and derived a phantom ~140x production-vs-benchmark gap, then [#2822] was expected to drive that floor to zero and briefly read as a failed fix when it did not. It was not: the floor fell 544ms to 397ms and the 147ms delta matches that box's measured 139ms connect cost, so [#2822] removed exactly the acquisition it claimed. What remained was never acquisition. Ruled out with evidence rather than argument - the reconnect path logged zero reopens all day, 500 server-side no-op round trips took 1.475ms total, and the store sat at 19 connections with 1 active and 0 waiting. Both fetches now stamp their own probe input size and the split line carries it as `N probed`, pinned by IL reachability so a refactor cannot drop one side and leave that denominator wrong again.
- **The Query Store plan and text fetches stop opening their own store connections** ([#2819]) - [#2811]'s split named `probe:` "the store connection open PLUS the touch/probe round trip" and declined to separate them, reasoning that doing so "would name a connection pool rather than a cost". It was a connection pool. The probe SQL runs in **0.39ms** with nothing stale and 37ms with 40 stale ids - every access an index scan, and none of its four tables a hypertable, so [#2796]'s missing-chunk-pruning shape is ruled out by fact rather than resemblance - while the phase itself measured **673-6,663ms**, and a cycle with zero ids, which issues no SQL whatsoever, still cost 673ms. That floor is acquisition. Each of the 114 (server, database) pairs was opening a SECOND and THIRD store connection per item, on top of the one the collector body already holds and documents as "one pooled store connection for the whole body" - roughly 228 acquisitions per cycle against a `MaxPoolSize` of 24. Worse, `await using` scoped the private connection to the whole method, so a pool slot was held across the SQL Server target fetch - measured at **104,799ms** on one database, one slot of twenty-four doing nothing store-side for a hundred seconds. Both fetches now BORROW the caller's connection, which is safe for a structural reason rather than a hopeful one: the enumerated driver reads an item and then awaits its write, so that connection is provably idle across exactly the window these fetches occupy, and neither the probe (autocommit) nor the writer (which opens and commits its own transaction) spans the target fetch with one. The borrowed connection is the collector BODY's, reused for every database in the sweep, so a store fault inside a fetch would leave it broken for every subsequent item - and because the fetches swallow their own exceptions and return a batch, `writeBatch` (which runs OUTSIDE the driver's per-item catch and is documented to propagate, "storage failure is systemic") would then fail the runtime-stats write and abort the REST OF THE SWEEP, which private connections made structurally impossible. So the fetches now reopen the borrowed connection on the way out of their catch: a transient fault (a cancelled command, a dropped socket) recovers and the caller's write proceeds as if the fetch had owned its connection, while a genuinely unreachable store fails the reopen too and is left to propagate - which is the correct handling of a systemic store failure and unchanged from before. `MaxPoolSize` is deliberately NOT raised: every pooled connection is a postgres.exe PROCESS and this store logged 8 "could not reserve shared memory region" retries the same day, so the fix is fewer acquisitions, not a bigger pool - and the multiplier that matters is concurrency, not acquisitions: per database the fetches ACQUIRED three connections (body + plan + text), but they acquire sequentially, so peak CONCURRENT holds per swept server were body + one in-flight fetch = 2. `MaxPoolSize` bounds concurrency, so 2x the sweep width is the number that had to fit - 8 at the 4-wide default, 32 at the 16-wide `ClampConcurrentSweeps` limit, over this bound. Borrowing puts peak concurrent demand back at the sweep width itself, where even a 16-wide sweep fits. Pinned by IL reachability rather than by a duration assertion, which would be flaky when honest and vacuous when loose: the two fetch state machines must contain zero calls to `OpenConnectionAsync`, with positive controls that resolve through the same MemberReference machinery so a scanner that has stopped resolving tokens fails the controls too instead of reporting the acquisition absent for the wrong reason.
- **Web dashboard: a sparse trend chart spanned only its data burst, so an old incident read as current** ([#2802]) - `renderLineChart` set its x-axis domain from the first and last DATA point, not the requested window. A dense series (CPU, memory) fills the window so it looked right, but a sparse discrete-event series (Blocking Events, Deadlocks) only has rows inside a short burst, so a chart titled "last 24 hours" zoomed its axis to that ~30-minute burst and dropped the calendar date from the tick labels - a burst ~19 hours before the view time read as if it were happening now. The renderer now takes an optional window (windowStart/windowEnd, UTC-epoch ms) and spans the axis over THAT window when given, so the points plot at their true position and the date survives; the window end is the query-time now, never the last data point, so an old burst can no longer slide to the right edge. The server tabs and the shared line renderer pass the range selector's hours ending now, and a composed panel passes the same window it fetched over (a brush-zoom, then the #2788 per-panel pin, then the view scope). No window passed leaves the chart byte-for-byte unchanged, a lone point still renders as a dot at its true x, and a brush-zoom still wins. Pinned with a source-scan test - there is no JS execution harness for the chart module.
- **The `io_latency` baseline no longer times out, and eight other metrics got faster with it** ([#2820]) - `PgBaselineProvider`'s shared `RobustTierScaffold` joined its MAD tiers with an OR'd non-equi predicate PostgreSQL cannot hash, reaching a planner estimate of cost 596,208,924 for a 193-row result and spilling to temp. The tiers are now expanded with `UNION ALL` and joined by equality - identical rows, identical checksum, 23.7s to 4.2s measured on a 476,431-row window. On the dogfood box `io_latency` had been exceeding its command timeout on eight of the last nine days, losing its baseline and silencing its anomaly detection each time.
- **A `query_store` fetch probe that throws no longer reports `probe:0ms` and hands its whole cost to the `other:` residual** ([#2816]) - `FetchAndStorePlansAsync` and `FetchAndStoreQueryTextAsync` stamped their probe sub-phase on a plain assignment at the END of the probe phase, so an `await` that threw jumped over it. The method catches and returns normally, the call site stamps the parent, and the split printed `probe:0ms` - leaving the `other:` residual, documented as time spent in neither database, to absorb a store round trip that had timed out. On use1 on 2026-09-03 a single failed probe put 43,053ms into `other:`, 97% of the whole fleet's residual budget that day. The `target` and `write` phases already stamped from `finally` blocks for exactly this reason (#2811); the probe was the one phase that did not. `probeWatch` is now hoisted beside `carryKey` and stamped from the `catch` when the success-path stamp did not run, guarded by a flag so a later throw cannot overwrite an honest reading. Pinned by an IL-reachability test rather than an arithmetic one: the existing sum-to-parent pin held on all 311 production rows, because the arithmetic was never wrong.
- **Headless web Alert History: a tray-only alert now reads "Logged", not a misleading "Sent"/"Not sent"** ([#2814]) - the Darling web is headless with no system tray, so a `tray` alert (Lite's delivered-without-email taxonomy) was never delivered on this surface, yet the status was derived from `alert_sent`, which the engine sets inconsistently across paths - true for the regular engine's cleared/resumed alerts, false for its problems - so a problem read "Not sent" while its own clear read "Sent" (as if we notified you it recovered but not that it broke). Both status collapses (the Alert History `statusCell` and the triage page's `alertStatusText`, kept in lockstep) now render a single neutral "Logged" for a `tray` alert; a real email/webhook channel still shows Sent / Not sent. Extends [#2781], which dropped the meaningless `tray` channel label.
- **Web dashboard: the server Daily Summary showed "Health: NaN"** ([#2807]) - the Health card fed `overall_health` through the numeric `num1` formatter, but `overall_health` is the health band's text LABEL (`DailyHealthBandCalculator.Label(HealthBand)`, the same value the Band card carries), so "Critical" formatted as a number rendered NaN. The redundant Health card is gone, since the Band card already shows the band, and `fmtInt`/`fmtNum`/`fmtPct`/`fmtMb` now render a non-finite value as the missing-value placeholder the way `fmtMs` already did, so no int/num/pct/mb stat or table cell can show "NaN" again.
- **A wall-clock-budget-abandoned cycle no longer records as `SUCCESS`** ([#2801]) - a collector cycle the #2673 whole-server budget gives up on stores nothing and advances no watermark, but reached the log site by RETURNING rather than throwing, so it took the ordinary path and inherited that path's hardcoded `"SUCCESS"` in both hosts. Every one of the 36 abandonments in the use1 store's 17-day retention was `SUCCESS` with `rows_collected = 0`. Beyond the wrong label it claimed a collection that never happened: `ReadCollectionSignalsAsync` takes `last_success` and `recent_success` from `status IN ('SUCCESS', 'SKIPPED')`, so a collector abandoning every cycle read as perpetually fresh, and its message landed in the #1837 note channel whose whole claim is that the run succeeded. Now its own `ABANDONED` status, shared by both hosts through `EnumeratedCollectorDriver.ClassifyReturnedRun`. Not `ERROR` (that would page on a guard doing its job), not `YIELDED` (documented as the 1s `LOCK_TIMEOUT` guard and read as target lock contention), not `SKIPPED` (a healthy no-op that counts as success). Safe to add because every read buckets by explicit list rather than by complement, and `collection_log.status` carries no CHECK constraint, so no migration rung. The underlying slowness is NOT new - the affected server had 2-11 `procedure_stats` runs over 60s every day of the retention window, with its worst maxima (246-278s) predating #2673.
- **Web dashboard: a pinned notebook panel cell showed no "Pinned" badge in the read view** ([#2788]) - the badge read only the nested #2735 `range` pin, so a cell pinned with the composer's per-panel "Time range" dropdown (a top-level `hours` override) rendered its pinned window but wore no badge, and the scope bar silently claimed a different range for that panel. The badge and the applied window now resolve through one shared `effectivePin`, so both pin shapes badge identically and the badge can never disagree with the window the panel actually renders.
- **The server-scoped watermark read now carries #2344's chunk-pruning bound** ([#2795]) - `DarlingCollectorRunner.GetLastCollectedTimeAsync` ran `SELECT MAX(<watermark>) ... WHERE server_id = $1` with no predicate on `collection_time`, the partitioning column, so it scanned every chunk in retention for that server on every cycle. #2344 fixed exactly this, but only in the per-database sibling `GetLastCollectedTimeForDatabaseAsync`; query_store declares both `WatermarkColumn` and `PerDatabaseWatermarkColumn`, so it kept paying the unbounded cost here. Measured on a 62.5 GB `query_store_stats`: 40.7 s and 50.6 s cold against Npgsql's 30 s default `CommandTimeout`, so the read was cancelled mid-flight - the store's own log recorded 2,092 such cancellations in one day while the bounded sibling recorded 17. Each one returned null, which is indistinguishable from a first run, so the collector fell back to query_store's 60-minute window instead of the ~5-minute incremental one and re-collected what it already held. Bounded at `WatermarkPolicy.ReadFloor` the same read is 2.9-5.9 s. The timeout is now set explicitly rather than inherited, and a failed watermark read is logged instead of silently swallowed.
- **query_store plan and text fetch: force HASH JOIN so the in-memory Query Store TVF is read once** ([#2791]) - `sys.query_store_plan` unions the on-disk table with the TVF `QUERY_STORE_PLAN_IN_MEM`, and the optimizer has no statistics for it: on OMEGA it estimated 1,000 rows against 14,633 actual (1,463% off). That guess put the TVF on the inner side of a Nested Loops join, re-executed once per candidate plan_id up to the 512 `MaxCandidatePlans` cap, each execution scanning the whole in-memory Query Store before any plan was decompressed. sp_QuickieStore put these statements at **55,000-61,000ms CPU each**, top of the whole instance, while the `TOP (50000)` runtime-stats payload this was long assumed to be averaged ~2s. `OPTION(RECOMPILE, HASH JOIN)` reads the TVF once: **~60,000ms CPU to 0.508s** measured on OMEGA. The same shape and the same fix apply to the text fetch, which joins two TVF-backed views directly. Rowsets verified byte-identical with and without the hint at 16/128/512 candidates under the collector's own ARITHABORT OFF.
- **Web dashboard: the composer's partial-window notice read "1 days" on a one-day store** ([#2785]) - the day count in BuildRetentionNotice was interpolated with a hardcoded "days" plural, so a store reaching back a single day read "reaches back about 1 days, but the requested window starts 1 days back". It now pluralises on the rendered number - "1" singular, "1.5"/"30" plural.
- **PostgreSQL statement text was never stored on ANY Aurora server, because the Aurora fetch does not deduplicate `queryid`** ([#2786]) - `aurora_stat_statements` keys on `(queryid, userid, dbid, toplevel)` exactly as `pg_stat_statements` does, so one `queryid` returns once per user and database that ran it, and the `(server_id, queryid)` upsert meets those duplicates as `21000: ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE command cannot affect row a second time`. That aborts the STATEMENT, so the batch's non-duplicated rows are lost with it - one repeated id stores nothing at all - and it is deterministic, so nothing self-heals. Measured on the monitoring fleet before the fix: 50 of 50 Aurora servers failing, ZERO succeeding, and `get_pg_top_queries` returning `query_text: null` on every row of every server. [#2651] met this exact failure while adding the vanilla arm and fixed it THERE; this arm predates it ([#2284]) and was only renamed, so the defect survived behind a green suite - the pin it added named the arm it was written for. Both pins now run over EVERY arm `FetchSqlFor` can return, which is exhaustive rather than enumerated, and the shared write path deduplicates as a backstop. The cadence guard made it worse rather than containing it: `IsDueSql` COALESCEs a missing answer to TRUE, which is correct for a first fetch and indistinguishable from a server whose every write has failed, so a broken server re-fetched on every sweep instead of hourly - ~12,000 `showtext` calls a day against 50 production Aurora clusters, to store nothing.
- **Web dashboard: an offline server no longer shows a green "Collectors OK"** ([#2779]) - the summary Collectors chip keyed its verdict on the FAILING count alone, and a stale collector counts as neither healthy nor failing, so a server that had stopped collecting kept a green "Collectors OK - N healthy - 0 failing" even while its own header read "no recent collection" and its Collection Health tab showed every collector STALE. The chip now reuses the reachability signal the card already carries (is_online, the same one that bands the card Offline): an offline server reads "Stale - no recent collection" in the neutral tone instead. One shared metricBands builder, so the fleet cards and the per-server detail header are both fixed at once - and no new stale-count threshold to over-fire on normally-infrequent collectors.
- **Darling Viewer and Dashboard: an offline server's Overview card no longer shows a green "Collectors OK"** ([#2784]) - the cross-app sibling of [#2779], which fixed the same green-on-offline reading on the web fleet cards. The WPF Overview card's Collectors verdict keyed on the FAILING count alone, and a stale collector is neither healthy nor failing, so a server that had stopped collecting kept a green "OK" and a green dot over "Healthy: 0, Failing: 0". It is latent in the viewer because the offline scrim covers the card, but the reading was still wrong, and the Dashboard's ServerHealthStatus carried the identical shape (the origin the viewer's own comment cites). Both now reuse the reachability signal the card already carries - IsOffline in the viewer, the live IsOnline ping in the Dashboard, the same signal that drives the offline overlay - so an offline server reads a neutral "Stale" over "No recent collection" instead of a green OK. A real collector failure on a reachable server still reads "N failed" in red, a healthy server still reads OK, and there is no new stale-count threshold. The overall card band and fleet score still read the raw failing count, so an offline server's overall severity is unchanged.
- **Web dashboard: an over-long time range showed a raw API validation error instead of a range hint** ([#2780]) - the Range dropdown offers "last 30 days", but the per-server CPU/query reads cap at 168 hours and return an error for anything wider, which the web dashboard mounted verbatim ("hours_back value '720' exceeds maximum of 168 hours..."). A shared read-error renderer (used by every panel, inline or composite) now recognises that specific over-range error and renders a notice naming the window the view keeps ("This view keeps up to 168 hours (7 days) of history - pick a shorter range") rather than the API's developer wording.
- **Web dashboard: Alert History dropped the meaningless "tray" delivery channel** ([#2781]) - the status cell appended the notification channel, which the store always records as "tray" (the Lite/Dashboard system-tray toast) - a channel the headless web surface has no equivalent for, so "Not sent - tray" read as noise. The "tray" channel is now suppressed on the web; a real channel (email/webhook) still renders. Fixed on both the Alert History page and the triage deep-link page, which mirror the same status cell.
- **`query_store` plan and text fetches: an EXPLICIT command timeout on both halves, replacing two defaults nobody chose** ([#2776]) - the fetches were being cancelled mid-write and the cancels were invisible as timeouts: the collector logged `NpgsqlException: Exception while reading from stream` while the store logged `ERROR: canceling statement due to user request` within ~200ms (three pairs timestamp-correlated on a live box; store otherwise healthy, 0 PANIC and 0 out-of-memory). The store-write path - `QueryStoreFetchProbe`, `QueryStorePlanWriter`, `QueryStoreTextWriter`, and the `PayloadDimensionWriter` flush that writes the plan XML itself - set no `CommandTimeout` at all and fell through to **Npgsql's 30s default**, while the SQL Server side sat on the runner's 60s default against measured `plan_fetch` phases reaching 91,526ms. Because the store IS the watermark, a cancelled write leaves those plans reading as missing, so the target re-decompresses the identical plans every following cycle - `candidate cap clamped to 512` fired 2,965 times in 14.9h, a missing set that never drains. `QueryStoreCollector` now sets `CommandTimeoutSecondsOverride = 500` and the runner hands that same value to the store commands, so one number covers both halves of what is one logical operation. **500s is an empirical over-shoot pending measurement, not settled tuning**, and it cuts both ways: a fetch that previously failed fast now holds its slot for up to 500s against a fleet concurrency of 4 and a 60s sweep budget, so `skipping relaunch` may get worse even as the failure count improves - both directions have to be read together. `PayloadDimensionWriter`'s new timeout parameter is optional and defaults to unset, so the general per-collector payload flush it shares (observed store phases 1.8-11.4s) is byte-for-byte unchanged.
- **query_store fetch failures re-paid full cost every cycle, forever** ([#2776]) - a plan or text fetch whose store write threw kept its entire carry-over set and re-attempted the identical width on the next cycle, so a database whose write kept timing out re-decompressed the same plan XML on the monitored server indefinitely. Measured on one monitoring host over 14.9h: 125 plan-fetch failures, 16 text-fetch failures, and the candidate cap pinned at its 512 ceiling 2,965 times - a missing set that never drained. Both fetches now halve their attempt width per consecutive failure (512 -> 256 -> 128 -> 64 -> 32), which halves the plan XML the store must commit in one transaction - the operation that was actually timing out - and halves what the target decompresses to produce it. Floored at the candidate cap's existing minimum rather than stopping, so a backed-off database keeps working and recovers on its own with no restart; one completed pass restores full width immediately. Inert until something throws, so healthy databases are unaffected.
- **Web dashboard: server cards got NARROWER as the window got WIDER, until the stat tiles clipped** ([#2772], reported from the field) - the fleet card grid (`.grid`, and the Custom Views list's `.view-cards`) sized its tracks with `auto-fill`, which lays down as many 260px tracks as the viewport holds whether or not there are cards to fill them; with fewer cards than tracks the cards shrank into their tracks while the rest sat empty, so maximising the browser drove each card below what its inner three-tile `.stats` row needs and the third tile clipped mid-text (`BP 4.0 G`, `0 fai`). Both grids now use `auto-fit`, which collapses the empty tracks so cards no longer shrink; the fleet server-card grids additionally cap each track at 260-460px and centre the leftover space, so a wide screen adds COLUMNS rather than shrinking the cards or fattening a handful of them. Invisible at fleet scale (every track occupied) and obvious at two servers; verified live at a two-card width before and after, and pinned with a source-scan test.
- **Web dashboard: a chart panel with a single collected time-bucket read as "not enough data points" beside siblings that plotted** ([#2773]) - `renderLineChart` treated one row like zero (`rows.length < 2`), so during collector warm-up a series that had reached exactly one time-bucket showed the warming-up strip while panels whose reads reach two buckets faster (deadlocks and waits sample every cycle; blocking snapshots are sparser) charted the same window - the same tab reading as half-broken. One bucket is data, not an absence: the whole-chart gate now fires only at ZERO rows, and a single point renders as a centered dot per series across every reachable mode (line, area, stacked-area, and the dual-axis overlay; stacked-bar already drew a bar per bucket). Zero rows still shows the strip, and a read whose empty array means "this never happened" still shows its own emptyText. Verified live against the deployed module and pinned with a source-scan test (there is no JS execution harness for the chart module).
- **`/api/triage`: store self-alerts no longer render three "Could not resolve server" errors** ([#2768]) - the fleet-level store alerts (Store Disk Pressure, Store Runtime Upgrade, Store Job Over Cadence, Compression Job Stuck, and each one's resolution edge) fire under the synthetic `Monitor Store` label, which is not in the monitored-server registry and cannot resolve by design. The triage page had no mapping for them, so it fell back to the per-server sections and ran `get_server_summary`, `get_collection_health` and `get_collection_log` against that label - three resolver errors plus a page-level warning on every store alert, against [#2710]'s own "degrade to notes, never a 500" contract. The page now recognises the label (a shared const, no longer twelve literals), skips resolution entirely, and serves the reads that actually apply: `get_store_metrics` for size, growth, per-object compression and the background-job rows carrying `duration_vs_cadence_percent`, plus `get_collector_cost` for the ingest volume those numbers move with. Sections are now marked fleet-level so they never bind a server, and the response carries `fleet_level` for the UI. Per-server alerts are unchanged.
- **Lite: a store with a large pre-#1907 Query Store backlog could not start at all** ([#2748]) - the one-time #1912 slice repair ran on every launch until it completed, and on a store carrying enough split intervals it never did: the staging statement has to materialize every collapsed group, `query_plan_text` included, inside Lite's `memory_limit=1GB` connection. Reproduced locally at the reporting store's exact scale (31,426 split intervals, realistic showplan payloads): `Out of Memory Error: failed to pin block of size 256.0 KiB (953.6 MiB/953.6 MiB used)`. On the reporter's Windows build the same pressure did not raise a catchable error at all - `duckdb.dll` aborted the process with a native fast-fail (`0xc0000409`, subcode 7), so the `catch` that exists precisely to keep a bad repair from stopping the app never ran, nothing was recorded, and the next launch repeated it exactly. The repair now records and commits an attempt BEFORE it starts, and stops attempting on startup after two incomplete ones. The app starts and collects normally; only the historical collapse is left undone, and #1907's read-side tie-break still resolves those rows deterministically. Dropping the `query_store_slice_repair_attempts` table re-arms it. The underlying memory cost is filed separately - this change is about never letting it cost the user their app.
- **`plan_correction` collector: `OPTION(RECOMPILE)` restored to the payload's two statements** ([#2766]) - [#2760] removed both hints on the theory that ~4,237ms of avg CPU seen on one prod database was compile cost. That was inferred from a telemetry signature, never measured, and it was wrong: the cost was the live Query Store catalog-view joins that [#2764] then replaced with staged temps. Measured with `SET STATISTICS TIME ON` against SQL Server 2025, compile for the post-[#2764] payload is ~84ms CPU total across all five statements - a ceiling, since it was taken under emulation. The hints are back for their real reason, plan cache hygiene: this body runs once per database, per server, every cycle, so caching a plan for each would accumulate cache bulk fleet-wide for plans nobody reuses. [#2764]'s staging is untouched.
- **`plan_correction` collector: the shipping SELECT still joined the Query Store catalog views live** ([#2764]) - [#2673] staged `sys.dm_db_tuning_recommendations` into a #temp so the statistics-free DMV stopped driving a plan-store scan, but the final SELECT went on LEFT JOINing `sys.query_store_query`, `sys.query_store_query_text` and `sys.query_store_plan` directly - the naive-catalog-view join the `sp_QuickieStore` pattern exists to avoid, paid per database, per server, every cycle. The Query Store side is now staged the same way `sp_QuickieStore` does it (plan -> query -> text): three temps pull only the rows the already-small recommendation set names, and the shipping SELECT joins the temps. Each catalog view is read exactly once, by a seek keyed off a materialised row count, and never appears on the JOIN side of the shipping query. Output columns, their order and the LEFT JOIN semantics are unchanged - a recommendation whose plan has aged out of Query Store still lands with NULL Query Store columns rather than being dropped.
- **query_store collector's open-interval re-read cost outgrew the window it was tuned against, fleet-wide** ([#2759]) - QueryStoreOpenIntervalState.RefreshEvery moved from 15 to 30 minutes. The 15-minute skip window (#2312) was still firing as designed - measured live on <host> (2026-09-01, 3-hour collection_log window), open-interval-inclusive runs landed every 12-19 minutes, correctly spaced by the mechanism - but each occurrence now cost 110-133 s, 2-2.5x the ~50 s/run yardstick the constant was tuned against (#2312's own recorded decision criterion: the #2312 yardstick decides whether it ever needs to move). Fleet-wide, query_store averaged 41 s/run and peaked at 373 s over the prior 24h - 6x the cost of the next-worst collector. Doubling the window halves how often the expensive re-read fires without changing the query itself, the closed-interval fast path, or what a reader sees once an interval closes; the current interval's view now lags real time by at most 30 minutes instead of 15. A join-shape theory (staging query_store_plan/query into #temp before the final join, matching sp_QuickieStore) was investigated and set aside - #2133's own benchmark already measured that join at 409 ms on an 82k-plan catalog, and real collection_log evidence pointed at the open-interval re-read, not the final join, as the actual driver.
- **Darling Viewer: the Fleet Health overview tab's server total wobbled against the sidebar's stable count, and could claim a false all-clear** ([#2753], reported from the field) - `FleetRollup.TotalServers` was derived from `summaries.Count`, the per-server summary cards that actually loaded THIS refresh cycle - and `LoadOverviewAsync` silently drops any server whose read failed (a transient timeout) before building that list. A bad cycle shrank the Overview's total while the sidebar, sourced from the stable `config_monitored_servers` registry, never moved. `FleetRollup.Build` now takes the registered fleet size explicitly, so the two counts always agree. Review caught a second-order bug in the first fix: with `TotalServers` corrected but the band counts still derived only from what loaded, the panel could render "All N servers healthy" while some of those N had no summary at all this cycle - an affirmative false claim, worse than the original undercount. A new `UnknownCount` (registered minus loaded) now gates the all-clear text via `IsAllClear`, and unaccounted-for servers get their own "N servers didn't report this cycle" line instead of silently landing in either the healthy or the problem count.
- **MemoryPressureEventsCollector threw "Arithmetic overflow error converting expression to data type int" on every collection, minutes after #2749's fix deployed** ([#2755], caught live during a fleet install) - #2749's fix switched the shared ring-buffer sample_time math from `DATEADD(SECOND, -((@ms_ticks - t.timestamp) / 1000), ...)` to `DATEADD(MILLISECOND, -(@ms_ticks - t.timestamp), ...)` to stop losing sub-second precision, but the removed `/ 1000` was ALSO what kept the value inside `DATEADD(MILLISECOND, ...)`'s int-range ceiling (~24.8 days of milliseconds) on the SQL Server versions the fleet actually runs - a long-uptime box crossed it within minutes, going from 447/447 SUCCESS to 24/24 ERROR. `CpuUtilizationCollector` carries the identical structural exposure (not yet observed failing, only because no monitored box happened to cross the threshold in its own ~14-minute post-install check window). Both now split the computation into a SECOND-scale `DATEADD` (safely inside int range for realistic uptimes, ~68 years) plus a `MILLISECOND`-scale `DATEADD` for the 0-999 remainder - verified bit-identical to the single-step form against a live SQL Server across the full delta range, including values well past the int boundary, so no precision is lost and no intermediate value ever leaves int range.
- **Darling Viewer: Availability Groups grid sorted numeric columns as text** ([#2757], reported by a customer via email) - every numeric column (Send queue, Redo queue, Send rate, Redo rate, Lag, Est drain, Est redo) binds to a pre-formatted, thousands-separated display string with no explicit `SortMemberPath`, so `DataGridBoundColumn.SortMemberPath` fell back to the column's own `Binding.Path` - the SAME display string - and the app-wide `DataGridSortBehavior` built its `SortDescription` against that string. A descending sort on Redo rate read 73, 72'989, 7'275, 70'743, 70, 7, 7: exact lexicographic order, not numeric. Each column now carries an explicit `SortMemberPath` pointing at its underlying numeric property; both apps carry an identical hand-duplicated copy of this grid and both were fixed.
- **The CPU chart degrades to disconnected dots after roughly an hour, on every server** ([#2749], reported from the field) - `CpuUtilizationCollector`'s ring-buffer query re-reads the whole retained `RING_BUFFER_SCHEDULER_MONITOR` history every poll (up to 60 entries) and dedups client-side on a COMPUTED `sample_time`, because a physical ring-buffer entry carries no stable key. That computation was `DATEADD(SECOND, -((@ms_ticks - t.timestamp) / 1000), SYSDATETIME())` - dividing by 1000 before the SECOND-granularity DATEADD discards the offset's sub-second remainder, and that remainder is different on every poll (SYSDATETIME() keeps moving; a given entry's own timestamp does not). Re-reading the SAME entry on a later poll could therefore recompute a sample_time that drifted forward by a fraction of a second, land just above the previous watermark, and get re-inserted as a near-duplicate row a moment after the original - a pattern of tightly-paired samples every ~60 seconds. `TimeSeriesGaps.BreakAtGaps`'s median-derived gap threshold (#1944) is exactly as sensitive to this as it is meant to be to real outages: a distribution that is half sub-second (the duplicate pairs) and half ~60s (the real cadence) collapses the median to the tiny value, so every genuine ~60s interval reads as a gap and only the tiny intra-pair dash survives - the chart was never actually broken, the data flowing into it was. `MemoryPressureEventsCollector` reads the same ring-buffer/watermark shape from `RING_BUFFER_RESOURCE_MONITOR` and carried the identical truncation. Both now compute the offset at millisecond precision (`DATEADD(MILLISECOND, -(@ms_ticks - t.timestamp), ...)`, no division), which makes recomputing the same physical entry on a later poll deterministic. Existing near-duplicate rows already stored from before the fix are not retroactively removed - they age out with the collector's normal 30-day retention.
- **query_stats grouped by object_name no longer collapses every ad-hoc statement into one null-labeled row** ([#2737]) - object_name on query_stats is the [#1568] module stitch, and the LEFT JOIN's misses are all NULL: GROUP BY folded every ad-hoc statement per database into a single unlabeled row (which usually won the ranking as an anonymous blob), a neq filter's `<> ALL` silently excluded the whole ad-hoc population - "everything except this procedure" quietly meant "every OTHER procedure" - and no filter could reach the blob at all. The compiler now folds the join's NULL misses at the one place both the grouped expression and every filter compile through: an ad-hoc row's object_name IS the literal `(ad hoc)` - the bucket is labeled, `eq`/`neq` `(ad hoc)` select/exclude the ad-hoc population explicitly, and `neq` on a procedure name now INCLUDES ad-hoc rows (the compared value is never NULL; the old procedures-only read stays available as an explicit second `neq '(ad hoc)'` filter). The per-database collapse itself is answered by a new `statement` dimension - COALESCE(module name, query_hash) - the actual "top statements" grouping: procedures keep their module names and ad-hoc statements stay distinct by hash, on both the raw [#1568] CTE route and the CAGG module_map route, with router coverage so an old window reads the daily rollup instead of silently pinning to 4-day raw. The sentinel is parenthesized so it cannot be an unquoted identifier, and `describe_custom_view_catalog` documents all three semantics.
- **A read-only Viewer seat could silently discard local Default Time Range / Auto-refresh interval changes** ([#2715]) - the Settings window's Save builds the operator's edited Default Time Range and Auto-refresh interval (`SettingsWindow.Result`, a purely local per-user preference file wholly unrelated to the shared Postgres/TimescaleDB store) BEFORE attempting the store write for the alert engine, notifications, and service flags - but the viewer only persisted that local preference to disk when the Settings window's `ShowDialog()` returned true, which required the LATER, unrelated store write to succeed. A read-only seat's `ViewerReadOnlyException` on that write silently discarded the local preference change too (reported: #2715), while the Color Theme control - which self-persists to its own local file before the store write is even attempted - correctly survived the same Save. Persisting the local preference now depends only on whether Save produced a result, never on the unrelated store write's outcome.
- **The plan-correction collector can no longer run minutes on a monitored server** ([#2673]) - a follow-up to the wall-clock backstop, from the collector_cost self-metric: plan_correction had the single WORST tail in the fleet - 260s on one prod server against a ~4s average - and unlike procedure_stats/query_stats it had no budget. It runs server-scoped on on-prem/RDS through the same single-query path #2684 now bounds, so it gets the same 120s per-server wall-clock budget; a cycle that blows it ships nothing and retries next without advancing its watermark.
- **The runaway plan-fetch cap now HOLDS instead of oscillating off** ([#2685]) - #2683's runaway detection flagged a plan-churning store but reset its streak on any drain, and such a store's candidate-count estimator oscillates (a small-capped pass drains, the next trusts the small average and caps to thousands), so the flag never survived and the store spiked back to 60-250s plan_fetch passes even after being flagged - observed on the fleet's worst store the same night #2683 deployed. The flag is now sticky with hysteresis: it arms after 24 consecutive clamps and disarms only after 24 consecutive drains, so an oscillating store stays capped at 512 and only a store that genuinely catches up clears it and regains the full cap.
- **Heavy collectors can no longer occupy a monitored server for minutes** ([#2673]) - query_store already bounded its per-database plan/text fetch, but procedure_stats and query_stats had no wall-clock cap, and the server-scoped single-query path never applied one anyway: it read and DRAINED a large result set on the bare cancellation token, and the 60s per-command timeout only covers execution, not the drain. Measured tails of 176s (procedure_stats) and 168s (query_stats) on one prod box, against 6s/1.9s averages - the "runs minutes on the server it's watching" profile the tool must never present. Both now declare a 120s per-server budget, and the single-query path in BOTH the Darling service and Lite runs execute+drain under it; a cycle that blows the budget ships nothing and retries next without advancing its watermark, so no single collector can grind on one target.
- **The Query Store collector stops grinding on a plan-churning tenant** ([#2683]) - a database that generates plans faster than the collector can capture them (one prod store held 100k plans and added ~5k/hour from ad-hoc/unparameterized SQL) pinned the plan-XML fetch at its 2048-plans-per-pass count ceiling forever - ~40s of decompression every cycle, 80% of the collection body - chasing plan XML that had already aged out of cache. It made query_store the fleet's heaviest collector and backed the collection queue up into multi-minute episodes, and lowering the byte budget could not help because the COUNT bound binds first. The fetch now counts consecutive passes that fail to drain, and once a store is clearly runaway (24 straight) it caps THAT store at 512 plans/pass instead of 2048; a store genuinely catching up drains within a few passes and keeps the full cap, so only the pathological case is throttled. A runaway store's plan-XML coverage stays partial by design - its plans age out regardless - but the collector stays light, which is what matters on a stock Query Store.
- **Deadlocks collection no longer fails on every Azure SQL DB target** ([#2681], reported from the field) - a #2641 regression: AzureQueryText's two UNION ALL blocks both carried the plan-SELECT marker, so the victim-plan column was spliced into the telemetry-blob block too, but only the ring-buffer block carried the plan-APPLY marker that defines the `vqp` alias — leaving `vqp.query_plan` orphaned and every Azure SQL DB deadlocks collection failing at parse with 4104 "The multi-part identifier 'vqp.query_plan' could not be bound" (6,600+ times a day on the reporter's fleet; on-prem/MI/RDS unaffected). That block is master-scoped and reads USER databases' deadlocks, whose plans live in a cache a master connection cannot reach, so it can never resolve a plan and now projects it as NULL at the same ordinal via its own marker. Reproduced against a live Azure SQL Database — the pre-fix query raises 4104, the fixed query compiles and runs.
- **PMLite lost Azure SQL DB deadlock and blocked-process capture until an app restart** ([#2677], reported from the field) - a database-scoped XE session on Azure SQL DB can end up in `sys.database_event_sessions` but not `sys.dm_xe_database_sessions` after a failover or scale operation, invisible to the ring-buffer reader. The recovery path recreated it once and, if that did not immediately restore visibility, set a give-up flag that was NEVER cleared - so a single TRANSIENT Azure blip stopped capture for the life of the process, and a 24/7 PMLite never restarts. The reporter's deadlocks stopped on one day and never resumed; blocked-process rides the same shared driver and the same latch, so it went dark too. The give-up is now a 30-minute COOLDOWN rather than a permanent flag - the recreate is re-attempted after it, because the invisibility usually clears once the failover settles - and the entry is cleared the instant the session reads back as visible, so capture self-heals without a restart. The #1535 concern the flag existed for (not churning DROP/CREATE every cycle, which wipes captured-but-unread events) is kept by the cooldown.
- **Query Store plan and text capture decompressed each item up to 3x per cycle** ([#2675]) - the by-ids plan/text fetch measured a plan's decompressed size with DATALENGTH and emitted the plan from the SAME inlined CTE, and because a CTE inlines rather than materialises, `CONVERT(nvarchar(max), query_plan)` — the decompression, which the view performs on every access — re-ran for each of the three references (the byte measure, the running total, and the passthrough). Measured on a SQL Server 2025 rig at 1,500 plans x ~68 KB: 2,440 ms CPU that way vs ~540 ms decompressing once — ~4.5x on the single most expensive operation in the heaviest collector. Both by-ids fetches now materialise the payload to a #temp ONCE, then run the unchanged byte-budget cut over the stored column; the query-text fetch got the same shape (text is not compressed, but the repeated nvarchar(max) reads scale with real text size, where in the field it is the larger of the two phases). No behaviour change — same missing-only set, same watermark, same budget, same rows, same order.
- **`upgrade-darling.ps1` crashed before copying anything on an install with no prior rollback backups** ([#2671], reported from the field) - a box only ever upgraded by hand, or on this script's first run, has zero `_rollback_manual_*` directories, and the run aborted right after it stopped the service with "Cannot find an overload for op_Subtraction" - leaving the operator with a stopped monitoring host and no upgrade. The cause is a PowerShell return trap: `Get-DarlingRollbackBackups` returned `@()` when nothing matched, but a bare `return @()` UNROLLS on the way out of a function, so the caller's `$backups` collected nothing and became `$null`. `@($null)` is then a one-element array holding `$null`, which slips past the `$ordered.Count -eq 0` guard, and the recency check reaches `$nowUtc - $null`. The producer now returns a real empty array with a unary-comma return, restoring the "always a list" contract every caller here already assumed, and the two consumers that index and subtract filter `$null` as defense in depth. The zero-backup state now short-circuits to "nothing recent" and the upgrade proceeds to take its first backup, exactly as the recency guard's own comment always claimed it would. Pinned by a test that plants no backups and runs the shipped functions.
